# JavaScript Internals & Mastery

To operate at an architect level, you must understand the machine that runs your code. It's no longer enough to know *how* to write a `Promise` or a closure—you must know *what* happens in memory and the runtime pipeline when you do. 

In this mega-module, we dive deep into the V8 engine, memory allocation, the execution context, the intricacies of the event loop, and much more.

---

<h2 id="module-0">Module 0 — How to Read the Language Itself</h2>




Before you can understand the *engine*, you need to understand the *rulebook* the engine follows. This module answers a question most developers never ask: **"Who decides what JavaScript does, and where is that written down?"**

---

### 📜 ECMAScript — The Rulebook Behind JavaScript

#### 🧩 What is it?
"JavaScript" is just the popular name. The actual **official standard** is called **ECMAScript** (its spec ID is **ECMA-262**). It's a long, extremely precise document that describes *exactly* how the language must behave — down to the last detail.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it?
JavaScript runs in *many* different places — Chrome, Firefox, Safari, Node.js, even smart TVs — and each is built by a different company. Without one shared rulebook, the same line of code could behave differently everywhere, and the web would fall apart. ECMAScript is the **single source of truth** every engine must obey, so your code behaves the same no matter where it runs.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The companies that build engines each write a program — an **engine** — that *implements* the rulebook. Same rulebook in → (almost) identical behavior out. Where engines differ, it's usually *speed*, not *behavior*.

```
              ECMAScript Spec (ECMA-262)
                   "the rulebook"
                         │
         ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
         ▼               ▼                ▼
       V8           SpiderMonkey    JavaScriptCore
    (Google)         (Mozilla)         (Apple)
   Chrome,            Firefox          Safari,
   Node.js                             iOS web views
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
ECMAScript is like the **official rules of chess**. V8, SpiderMonkey, and JavaScriptCore are three different **chess apps**. They look different and run at different speeds, but because they all follow the same rulebook, a checkmate is a checkmate in every one of them.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between JavaScript and ECMAScript?**
A: ECMAScript (ECMA-262) is the *specification* — the rules. JavaScript is an *implementation* of those rules, plus host extras the environment adds (the **DOM** — the page's elements — in browsers, or **`fs`** — the file system — in Node). "ES6 / ES2015" just refers to a specific yearly version of the spec.

---

### 🔒 Abstract Operations & Internal Slots — The Engine's Private Toolbox

#### 🧩 What is it?
When you read the spec, you'll meet two strange things:
- **Abstract Operations** — internal helper steps the spec uses, written like `ToPrimitive()` or `ToNumber()`.
- **Internal Slots** — hidden, internal data the engine attaches to **objects** (not to primitives), written in double brackets like `[[Prototype]]` or `[[PromiseState]]`. They aren't normal properties, and different kinds of objects carry different slots: *every* object has `[[Prototype]]`, but only a **Promise** has `[[PromiseState]]`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know them?
You **cannot call them in your code** — `ToPrimitive()` is not a real function you can type. But they explain *why* weird things happen. Almost every famous JavaScript "WTF" (like `[] + [] === ""`) is just an abstract operation quietly running behind the scenes. Knowing them turns confusing magic into predictable rules.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
When you write `[] + {}`, the engine secretly runs the abstract operation `ToPrimitive()` on each side to turn the objects into **primitive values** (here they both become strings) before joining them:

```
   [] + {}
     │
     ▼   engine secretly calls ToPrimitive() on each operand
   "" + "[object Object]"
     │
     ▼
   "[object Object]"
```

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
console.log([] + []);      // ""                -> both arrays become "" via toString()
console.log([] + {});      // "[object Object]"
console.log([1, 2] + [3]); // "1,23"            -> "1,2" + "3"
// You never SEE ToPrimitive, but it runs every single time.
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Internal slots are like the **VIN number stamped inside a car's engine block** — you can't see it from the driver's seat and you can't change it, but it's how the manufacturer knows what the car *really* is. `[[Prototype]]` is JavaScript's hidden VIN on every object.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Can you read an internal slot like `[[PromiseState]]` directly from your code?**
A: No. Internal slots are engine-internal. You can only observe them indirectly (e.g. a promise's state shown in DevTools, or through methods the spec deliberately exposes). They are not normal properties.

---

### 🚦 The TC39 Process — How New Features Are Born

#### 🧩 What is it?
**TC39** is the committee (delegates from Google, Apple, Microsoft, Mozilla, and others) that decides which new features get added to JavaScript. Every proposed feature climbs a **5-stage ladder**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know it?
Knowing a feature's stage tells you whether it's **safe to use**. A Stage 4 feature is final and reliable; a Stage 1 idea might change completely or be dropped. This stops you from betting your codebase on something that may never ship.

#### ⚙️ How does it work? (the 5-stage ladder)
```
 Stage 0 ───▶ Stage 1 ───▶ Stage 2 ───▶ Stage 3 ───▶ Stage 4
 "an idea"    "proposal"   "draft"      "candidate"  "shipped!"
  anything     has a        syntax &     spec done;   part of the
  goes         champion     semantics    implement +  yearly standard;
                            decided      get feedback  safe to use
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like a **new medicine going through clinical trials**. Stage 0 is a scientist's hunch; Stage 4 is approved and on pharmacy shelves. You wouldn't prescribe a drug still in early trials — and you shouldn't ship a Stage 1 proposal to production.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: A feature is at "Stage 3." Is it safe to use in production?**
A: Cautiously, and usually via a transpiler/polyfill (e.g. Babel). Stage 3 means the spec is complete and browsers are implementing it, but small changes are still possible. Only **Stage 4** is fully final.

---

<h2 id="module-1">Module 1 — Engine Internals: Source to Machine Code</h2>

Your computer's CPU has no idea what `const`, `function`, or `=>` mean — it only understands raw machine instructions. So how does a text file full of JavaScript actually *run*? This module follows your code on its journey from plain text to fast machine code, and reveals the tricks V8 (the engine in Chrome and Node) uses to make it quick.

---

### ⚙️ The Compilation Pipeline — From Text to Machine Code

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **pipeline** is the series of steps an engine runs to turn your source text into something the CPU can execute. In V8 the main stages are: **Parser → AST → Ignition (bytecode) → TurboFan (machine code)**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it?
The CPU can't read JavaScript directly, and fully translating every line into optimized machine code *upfront* would make pages slow to start. So engines compromise: **start fast** by interpreting, then **get faster** by optimizing only the code that actually runs a lot. The pipeline is how they pull off "instant start *and* high speed."

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
1. **Parser** — reads your text, breaks it into *tokens* (`const`, `x`, `=` …), and builds a tree called the **AST** (Abstract Syntax Tree) that represents your code's structure.
2. **Ignition** (the interpreter) — turns the AST into **bytecode** (compact, low-level instructions) and runs it immediately. It also *watches* which functions run often.
3. **TurboFan** (the optimizing compiler) — takes the "hot" (frequently-run) functions and recompiles them into highly optimized **machine code**.

```
 Your Source Code
  "const x = 1"
        │
        ▼
 ┌──────────────┐  splits text into tokens,
 │   Parser     │  then builds a tree
 └──────┬───────┘
        ▼
   AST (tree form)
        │
        ▼
 ┌──────────────┐  turns the AST into BYTECODE and runs it.
 │   Ignition   │  Counts how often each function runs.
 │ (interpreter)│
 └──────┬───────┘
        │  function is "hot"? promote it ▼
 ┌──────────────┐  recompiles that function into
 │   TurboFan   │  super-fast MACHINE CODE
 │ (optimizer)  │
 └──────────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Imagine translating a foreign book aloud for a friend. You don't spend a month perfectly translating all 500 pages first (slow to start) — you **translate page by page as you read** (Ignition interpreting). When you notice your friend keeps re-reading *one favorite chapter*, you sit down and make a **polished printed translation of just that chapter** (TurboFan optimizing the hot code).

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Walk me through what happens from source code to execution.**
A: The parser tokenizes the source and builds an AST. Ignition compiles the AST to bytecode and interprets it immediately, while collecting profiling data. Functions that run often ("hot") are handed to the optimizing compiler (TurboFan), which produces fast machine code. If that optimized code's assumptions later break, it **deoptimizes** back to bytecode.

---

### 📖 Eager vs. Lazy Parsing — Why Big Files Boot Slowly

#### 🧩 What is it?
The parser doesn't fully process every function the moment it sees it. It can **lazily pre-parse** (a quick, syntax-only scan) and only fully — "eagerly" — parse a function when it's actually about to run.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it?
Most code in a large file isn't needed immediately. Fully compiling functions you might never call wastes startup time. Lazy parsing lets the engine **skip work until it's needed**, so the page becomes interactive faster.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- On the first pass, V8 does a **light pre-parse**: "is this valid syntax?" — but generates no bytecode.
- When a function is finally **called**, V8 goes back and **fully parses** it.
- Code that clearly runs straight away gets **eagerly** parsed up front.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like receiving a 50-page contract. You don't read every clause line-by-line right away — you **skim to check nothing's obviously broken** (pre-parse), and only **read a section carefully when you actually need to act on it** (full parse).

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why does a huge JavaScript bundle slow down startup even before my code "runs"?**
A: Because V8 must still **pre-parse every function definition** to verify its syntax — even ones you never call. Less code shipped = less to parse = faster boot. *(Historically, wrapping an immediately-run function in parentheses — `(function(){…})()` — hinted to V8 to eagerly parse it.)*

---

### 🚀 The JIT Tiers — Why Code Gets Faster the Longer It Runs

#### 🧩 What is it?
**JIT** (Just-In-Time) compilation means the engine compiles code *while the program runs*, not all upfront. V8 doesn't have one compiler — it has a **ladder of tiers**, each slower to produce but faster to execute, and code climbs the ladder the more it runs.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it?
A cold function that runs once doesn't deserve expensive optimization — interpreting it is fine. A function in a tight loop running millions of times *does*. Tiers let V8 spend optimization effort **only where it pays off**.

#### ⚙️ How does it work? (the four V8 tiers)
```
   cold code                                      hot code
 (just started)                              (run many times)
       │                                            │
   Ignition  ──▶  Sparkplug  ──▶  Maglev  ──▶  TurboFan
  interpret      quick machine    mid-tier      fully
  bytecode       compile          optimize      optimized
       └── starts instantly        gets faster the longer it runs ──┘
```
A function begins in **Ignition**. If it keeps running, V8 promotes it up the ladder for more aggressive optimization.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A new employee starts by **reading the manual for every task** (Ignition — slow but no training needed). The tasks they do all day get **practiced until automatic** (TurboFan). You wouldn't send someone to a week-long training course for a task they'll do *once* — and neither does V8.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does it mean that JavaScript is "JIT compiled"?**
A: The engine compiles code *during* execution rather than fully ahead of time. It interprets first for a fast start, profiles which functions are hot, and progressively recompiles those into optimized machine code through tiers (Ignition → Sparkplug → Maglev → TurboFan in V8).

---

### 🏷️ Hidden Classes (Shapes) — How V8 Makes Objects Fast

#### 🧩 What is it?
JavaScript objects are dynamic — you can add properties anytime — and that flexibility is normally *slow*. To fix this, V8 secretly creates a **Hidden Class** (also called a **Shape**, or a **Map** inside V8) for each object structure: an internal blueprint recording *which properties exist and where each one sits in memory*.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it?
Without a blueprint, reading `obj.x` would mean a dictionary lookup every time (slow). With a hidden class, V8 knows "`x` is always at slot 0" and reads it with a **direct memory offset** (fast). Crucially, objects with the **same shape share the same hidden class**, so V8 can optimize them all together.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Add properties **in the same order** and objects share a hidden class. Add them in a **different order**, or add one **conditionally**, and you create a *new, separate* hidden class — losing the shared optimization.

```
  const a = { x: 1, y: 2 };     const b = { x: 9, y: 8 };
        │                             │
        └──────────────┬──────────────┘
                       ▼
            same structure: { x, y }
                       ▼
            ┌────────────────────┐
            │   Hidden Class C1  │   "x → slot 0
            │      { x, y }      │    y → slot 1"
            └────────────────────┘
   Both objects share C1 → V8 treats them as one fast type.
```

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// ❌ BAD: y is added conditionally -> two different shapes
function Point(x, y) {
  this.x = x;
  if (y) this.y = y;     // some Points are {x}, others are {x, y}
}

// ✅ GOOD: every Point has the SAME shape {x, y} -> one hidden class
function Point(x, y) {
  this.x = x;
  this.y = y || 0;       // y always exists
}
```
> **Note (simplification):** "slot 0 / slot 1" is the *idea*; the real memory layout has more detail. The mental model that matters is: *same shape = shared fast blueprint.*

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A hidden class is like a **standardized shipping form**. If everyone fills the boxes in the same order (Name → Address → Item), the sorting machine reads them at full speed. The moment someone reorders or skips a box, the machine has to stop and read that form *by hand*.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Do `{ x: 1, y: 2 }` and `{ y: 2, x: 1 }` get the same hidden class?**
A: **No.** Property *insertion order* is part of the shape, so these are two different hidden classes — even though they hold the same keys. Creating objects with a consistent key order keeps them sharing one class (and stays fast).

---

### ⚡ Inline Caches — Monomorphic vs. Polymorphic vs. Megamorphic

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **Inline Cache (IC)** is V8's memory of *where it found a property last time*. When one line like `return obj.x` runs over and over, the IC remembers the answer for that shape so it can skip the lookup next time. This is the single biggest reason hidden classes matter.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it?
Looking a property up fresh every time is slow. The IC lets V8 reason: "last time this exact shape came through, `x` was at slot 0 — I'll bet it's there again." The **fewer different shapes** a line sees, the faster its IC.

#### ⚙️ How does it work? (three states)
```
  How many object SHAPES does one line (e.g. `obj.x`) see?

  Monomorphic   1 shape       {x,y}                       ⚡ fastest
  Polymorphic   2–4 shapes    {x,y} {x} {a,x}             🙂 ok-ish
  Megamorphic   5+ shapes      everything else            🐢 slow — V8 gives up
```

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
function getX(obj) { return obj.x; }

// ⚡ MONOMORPHIC — getX only ever sees the shape { x, y }
getX({ x: 1, y: 2 });
getX({ x: 3, y: 4 });

// 🐢 MEGAMORPHIC — getX now sees many different shapes
getX({ x: 1 });
getX({ a: 1, x: 2 });
getX({ x: 1, z: 9, q: 0 });
```
> **Real-World Example:** A `render(props)` function that receives objects of wildly different shapes becomes megamorphic and slow. UI frameworks normalize props into a consistent shape precisely to keep these hot functions monomorphic.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A barista who sees the **same regular order all morning** (monomorphic) makes it on autopilot. A few regulars with *different usuals* (polymorphic) — still manageable. But if **every customer orders something totally different** (megamorphic), they give up memorizing and re-check the full menu every time. Slow.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why is "monomorphic" code faster than "megamorphic" code?**
A: A monomorphic call site has seen only one object shape, so its inline cache is a single direct memory offset — basically instant. A megamorphic site has seen too many shapes (5+), so V8 abandons the per-shape cache and falls back to a slower generic lookup on every call.

---

### 💥 Deoptimization & Optimization Killers

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Deoptimization** ("bailing out") is when TurboFan throws away its fast machine code for a function and drops back to slow bytecode — because an assumption it had made turned out to be wrong.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know it?
TurboFan optimizes by *guessing* ("this function always gets numbers"). If you later break that guess ("now it got a string"), the engine must un-optimize and re-profile — which can be **worse than never optimizing**. Knowing what triggers this lets you keep hot code fast.

#### ⚙️ How does it work? — the common killers
Keep these **out of hot paths**:
- **Changing an object's shape after creation** — adding/deleting properties breaks its hidden class.
- **Mixed-type arrays** like `[1, "two", {}]` — stops V8 specializing the array's storage.
- **Leaking `arguments`** — passing the magic `arguments` object out of its function. *(This was a severe deopt in older V8; modern V8 copes far better, but rest params `...args` are still cleaner and safer.)*
- **`delete obj.prop`** on hot objects — prefer `obj.prop = undefined`, or use a `Map`.

```javascript
// 🔍 Diagnose it yourself — watch functions optimize and bail out live:
//   node --trace-opt --trace-deopt yourfile.js
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
TurboFan is a **strict club bouncer**. Wear the exact same outfit every night (one shape) and he waves you in instantly (optimized). Show up in a different hat each time (changing shapes) and he stops to check your ID every single time (deopt).

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is a hidden class, and how do you avoid breaking it?**
A: A hidden class is V8's internal blueprint of an object's structure, used to look up properties by direct offset. You avoid breaking it by giving objects **all their properties at creation, in a consistent order**, and not adding/deleting properties or swapping prototypes afterward.

---

<h2 id="module-2">Module 2 — Memory Model & Garbage Collection</h2>

Unlike C, JavaScript never makes you manually free memory — there's no `malloc`/`free` step you call by hand. The engine hands you memory when you create things and quietly cleans up what you stop using. But "quietly" doesn't mean "magically": understanding *where* data lives and *how* it gets freed is what separates a developer who ships memory leaks from one who doesn't.

---

### 🧠 Stack vs. Heap — Where Your Data Lives

#### 🧩 What is it?
JavaScript stores your data in two places. The **Stack** holds small, fixed-size values (primitives) and *pointers*. The **Heap** holds everything that can grow — objects, arrays, functions.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this?
It explains the single most confusing beginner bug: *"I copied my object, changed the copy, and the original changed too!"* Knowing what lives where tells you when you're copying a **value** versus copying a **pointer** to the same thing.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **Primitives** (`number`, `string`, `boolean`, `null`, `undefined`, `symbol`, `bigint`) are copied **by value** — each variable gets its own independent copy on the stack.
- **Objects / arrays / functions** are handled **through a reference** — the variable on the stack only holds a *pointer* (an address) to the real data in the heap. Copy the variable and you copy the *pointer*, not the object — so both point at the **same** heap object.

```
   STACK  (primitives + pointers,        HEAP  (objects, freed by GC)
           freed when the function ends)
   ┌──────────────────────┐              ┌─────────────────────┐
   │ a = 10               │              │                     │
   │ b = 10   (a's copy)  │              │   ┌─────────────┐   │
   │ x = ●────────────────┼─────────────▶│   │  { n: 11 }  │   │
   │ y = ●────────────────┼─────────────▶│   └─────────────┘   │
   └──────────────────────┘              └─────────────────────┘
       x and y are two pointers to the SAME heap object
```
> **Note (simplification):** "primitives on the stack" is the mental model. Real engines are cleverer — e.g. strings (a primitive) actually live in the heap with a pointer on the stack — but *value vs. pointer* is the distinction that matters.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
let a = 10, b = a;   // b is a COPY of the value
b++;                 // a is still 10  (independent)

let x = { n: 10 }, y = x;  // y copies the POINTER, not the object
y.n++;                     // x.n is now 11  (same object!)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A primitive is a **fact written on a sticky note** — photocopy it and you have two independent notes; scribble on one and the other is untouched. An object is a **shared Google Doc link** — copy the link to a friend and you're both editing the *same* document; their edit shows up for you.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why does changing a copied object also change the original?**
A: Because the copy isn't a copy of the *object* — it's a copy of the **reference** (the pointer). Both variables now point at the **same** heap object, so a mutation through either is visible through both. To get an independent object you must clone it (e.g. `structuredClone` or a deep copy). *(Pedantic but interview-worthy: JS is always copy-**by-value** — the value being copied just happens to be a pointer; there is no true "pass by reference.")*

---

### ♻️ Garbage Collection — How Memory Gets Freed Automatically

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Garbage Collection (GC)** is the engine's background process that finds objects your program can no longer use and frees their memory — so you don't have to.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it?
In low-level languages you free memory by hand; forget to, and you leak; free too early, and you crash. GC removes that whole class of bugs by deciding *for* you what's safe to delete. The key question it must answer is: **"Can the program still reach this object?"**

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
**1. Reachability.** GC starts from **roots** (global variables, and everything on the current call stack) and follows every reference. Anything it can reach is *alive*; anything it can't is *garbage*.

```
   ROOTS (globals, current call stack)
        │ follow every reference
        ▼
   ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐         ┌──────┐
   │  A   │───▶│  B   │         │  C   │   ← nothing reaches C
   └──────┘    └──────┘         └──────┘
   reachable   reachable        UNREACHABLE → collected (freed)
```
Because it works by *reachability from roots* (not by counting references), GC correctly cleans up objects that reference **each other** but that nothing else points to.

**2. Generational split.** Most objects die almost immediately (a temporary inside a function), so V8 splits the heap and collects the young area constantly and cheaply:

```
   NEW objects are born here           Long-lived survivors move here
   ┌────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────┐
   │   NEW SPACE (young)     │ survive  │   OLD SPACE             │
   │   small (~1–8 MB)       │  a few   │   large                │
   │   GC runs OFTEN + FAST  │ ──GCs──▶ │   GC runs RARELY,      │
   │   ("Scavenger" copy GC) │          │   heavier "Mark-Sweep" │
   └────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────┘
   Most objects die young → never make it out of New Space.
```

**3. Marking details.** The slower Old-Space collection uses **mark-and-sweep**: mark everything reachable, then sweep (free) the rest. V8 does this with **tri-color marking** (objects go white → grey → black as they're scanned; anything still white at the end is garbage) and its **Orinoco** collector runs much of the work **concurrently** on background threads, so your app barely pauses.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of every object as a **balloon tied to your wrist by a string**. As long as a string still connects it to *you* (a root), it stays. The moment every string to a balloon is cut, it floats away — and GC is the cleanup crew that collects floated-away balloons. Generational GC just notices that most party balloons pop within minutes, so it sweeps the floor near the entrance constantly and only tidies the back room occasionally.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How does the garbage collector decide what to collect?**
A: By **reachability**, not reference counting. It traces from roots (globals + the call stack) and keeps everything reachable; the unreachable rest is collected. This is why two objects that only reference each other are still collected if nothing else points to them.

---

### 💧 Memory Leaks — When Garbage Won't Die

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **memory leak** is an object you're *done* with, but that the GC still considers reachable — so it's never freed. Over time these pile up and your app's memory creeps upward until it slows or crashes.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know them?
GC is automatic, so leaks feel impossible — but they're common in long-running apps (SPAs, Node servers). They almost always come from an **accidental leftover reference** keeping something alive.

#### ⚙️ How does it work? — the classic leak sources
- **Detached DOM nodes** — you remove an element from the page but still hold it in a variable/array.
- **Forgotten timers / intervals** — a `setInterval` you never `clearInterval`, whose callback holds big state.
- **Lingering event listeners** — `addEventListener` with no matching `removeEventListener` on teardown.
- **Unbounded caches** — a `Map` used as a cache that only ever grows.
- **Closures capturing large scopes** — a returned function keeping a huge object alive (see Module 3's "elephant").
- **Accidental globals** — an undeclared assignment (`x = …` in non-strict mode) sticks `x` on `globalThis` forever.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// LEAK 1 — detached DOM: the node is off-screen but still referenced
const detached = [];
function removeButton() {
  const el = document.getElementById('my-button');
  document.body.removeChild(el);
  detached.push(el);   // ❌ GC can't free it — `detached` still points to it
}

// LEAK 2 — forgotten interval keeps `bigData` alive forever
function startPolling() {
  const bigData = new Array(1_000_000).fill('*');
  setInterval(() => console.log(bigData.length), 1000); // ❌ never cleared
}
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like **moving out of an apartment but never returning one key**. As far as the landlord (GC) knows, *someone still has access*, so the place can't be re-rented. That one forgotten key (a stray reference) keeps the whole apartment (object) occupied.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why might a long-running SPA's memory grow over time, and how do you find the leak?**
A: Usually leftover references — detached DOM, uncleared timers/listeners, or ever-growing caches — keep objects reachable. To find it, use DevTools → Memory and the **three-snapshot technique**: snapshot, repeat the suspect action (open/close a view) several times, snapshot again, and look for objects whose count and **retained size** keep growing.

---

### 🔗 Weak Collections — Leak-Proof References

#### 🧩 What is it?
`WeakMap`, `WeakSet`, `WeakRef`, and `FinalizationRegistry` let you reference an object **weakly** — meaning your reference does *not* keep it alive. If nothing else points to the object, GC is free to collect it (and drop the weak entry too).

#### 🤔 Why do we need them?
They solve the "unbounded cache" leak. With a normal `Map`, every cached object is kept alive forever by the map itself. A `WeakMap` lets entries disappear automatically once their key object is gone — ideal for caches and attaching private metadata to objects.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **`WeakMap` / `WeakSet`** — keys must be **objects** (or, since ES2023, unregistered symbols), are held weakly, and aren't enumerable (you can't list them).
- **`WeakRef`** — a weak pointer to one object; call `.deref()` to get it (or `undefined` if it's been collected).
- **`FinalizationRegistry`** — runs a cleanup callback *after* an object is collected. Use sparingly: the timing is **not guaranteed**.

```javascript
const meta = new WeakMap();
function getMeta(obj) {
  if (!meta.has(obj)) meta.set(obj, computeMeta(obj));
  return meta.get(obj);   // when `obj` is GC'd, this entry vanishes automatically
}
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A normal `Map` is a **bouncer gripping every guest's wrist** so nobody can ever leave (they leak). A `WeakMap` only **waves** at guests — when they decide to go home, the GC lets them walk out, and the wave is forgotten.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When would you use a `WeakMap` instead of a `Map`?**
A: When you want to associate data with an object **without preventing that object from being garbage-collected** — caches, or "private" per-object metadata. As soon as the key object becomes unreachable elsewhere, its `WeakMap` entry is cleaned up automatically, avoiding a leak.

---


<h2 id="module-3">Module 3 — Execution Model: Contexts, Scope & Closures</h2>

Every time a function runs, JavaScript spins up a little workspace to track its variables and remember where it came from. Master this one mental model — **execution contexts, the scope chain, and closures** — and the language's spookiest behaviors (hoisting, the TDZ, the famous loop bug) stop being magic and become obvious.

---

### 🏗️ Execution Context — The Workspace Every Function Gets

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **execution context** is the private workspace the engine creates each time code runs. There's one **Global** context for your whole program, and a brand-new one for **every function call**. Each context tracks: which variables exist, the value of `this`, and a link to its outer scope.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Code can't just run as loose text — the engine needs somewhere to record *"what does the name `x` mean right here, right now?"*. Without a per-call workspace, two calls to the same function would stomp on each other's variables. The context is what keeps each call's data separate.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Each context is built in **two phases**:

1. **Creation phase** (before any line runs): the engine scans the code and *sets up* the variables — `var` declarations get reserved and set to `undefined`, `let`/`const` get reserved but left **uninitialized** (this is the TDZ, covered later), and function declarations are stored whole. `this` is determined.
2. **Execution phase**: the code runs top to bottom, *assigning* real values to those reserved slots.

Contexts stack on a **call stack** (the engine's to-do pile) — the active one sits on top:

```
   CALL STACK  (top = currently running)
   ┌────────────────────────────┐
   │  greet()  context          │ ◀── running now
   ├────────────────────────────┤
   │  main()   context          │
   ├────────────────────────────┤
   │  Global   context          │ ◀── created once, at start
   └────────────────────────────┘
   A call PUSHES a context; a return POPS it off.
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a **chef's workstation** for a single order. Each order (function call) gets a fresh, clean counter with its own ingredients laid out. First the kitchen **preps** the station (creation phase — bowls placed, labels stuck on), then the chef **cooks** (execution phase — fills the bowls). When the dish ships, the station is wiped and reused for the next order.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// CREATION PHASE happens first, before line 1 "runs":
//   - `name` is reserved and set to undefined
//   - `greet` (a function declaration) is stored whole
console.log(name);   // undefined  (reserved, not yet assigned)
greet();             // "Hi!"      (whole function already available)

var name = "Ada";    // EXECUTION PHASE assigns the real value now
function greet() { console.log("Hi!"); }

console.log(name);   // "Ada"
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between the creation phase and the execution phase of an execution context?**
A: In the **creation phase** the engine scans the scope and *sets up* bindings before running any code — `var`s become `undefined`, `let`/`const` are reserved but uninitialized (TDZ), function declarations are fully stored, and `this` is fixed. In the **execution phase** the code runs line by line and *assigns* the actual values. This split is exactly why hoisting exists.

---

### 🔗 Scope Chain — How a Name Gets Resolved

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **scope chain** is the ordered list of environments the engine searches to find a variable. Each context holds a **Lexical Environment** — a record of its own variables (`let`/`const`/functions) plus a pointer to its **outer** environment. Following those pointers outward *is* the scope chain.

> **Note (simplification):** the spec splits an environment into a **Variable Environment** (holds `var` + function declarations) and a **Lexical Environment** (holds `let`/`const`). For everyday reasoning you can treat them as one "scope record"; we only separate them when the `var`/`let` difference matters.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Inner code constantly uses variables defined further out (a helper reading a config value, a callback reading a loop counter). The engine needs a deterministic rule for *"if it's not here, where do I look next?"* — otherwise nested code couldn't safely reach outer values.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
To resolve a name, the engine looks in the **current** environment; if it's missing, it follows the **outer** pointer one level up, and repeats until it finds the name or hits Global. Not found anywhere → `ReferenceError`. Crucially, "outer" is decided by **where the code is written** (lexical / "as-written" scope), **not** by who called it.

```
   inner()  env:  { y }
        │ outer ▼   (not found here? go up)
   outer()  env:  { x }
        │ outer ▼
   Global   env:  { z }
        │ outer ▼
       null   ──▶ ReferenceError if still not found

   Lookup walks UP only — never sideways, never down.
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like asking to borrow something in a **family home**. You check your own room first. Not there? You ask the shared hallway closet (the room that *contains* yours). Still nothing? You go up to the main house storage. You search outward through the rooms that **physically enclose** you — you don't barge into a sibling's separate room. Where the rooms are *built* decides the search path.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const z = "global";

function outer() {
  const x = "outer";
  function inner() {
    const y = "inner";
    // engine resolves each name by walking UP the chain:
    console.log(y);  // "inner"  (found in inner's own env)
    console.log(x);  // "outer"  (not in inner → found in outer)
    console.log(z);  // "global" (not in inner/outer → found in Global)
  }
  inner();
}
outer();

// console.log(y); // ReferenceError: y is not defined (out of scope)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How does JavaScript decide what a variable name refers to?**
A: Through the **scope chain**, resolved **lexically** (by where code is written). The engine looks in the current environment, then follows each environment's outer pointer upward until it finds the name or reaches the global scope; if it's never found, it throws a `ReferenceError`. It never searches the *caller's* scope — only the enclosing scopes from the source text.

---

### 🪜 Hoisting & the Temporal Dead Zone — `var` vs `let`/`const`

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Hoisting** is the effect of the creation phase: declarations are "set up" before any code runs, so they *seem* to move to the top of their scope. But `var`, `let`/`const`, and function declarations are hoisted **differently**. The **Temporal Dead Zone (TDZ)** is the gap where a `let`/`const` name exists but can't be touched yet.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
This explains two classic "WAT?" moments: why reading a `var` before its line gives `undefined` (not an error), but reading a `let` before its line **throws**. Get this wrong and you'll either be baffled by silent `undefined`s or surprised by `ReferenceError`s.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
During the **creation phase**:
- **`var`** → reserved *and* initialized to `undefined`. Reading it early is allowed (you get `undefined`).
- **`let` / `const`** → reserved but left **uninitialized**. Reading it before its declaration line throws `ReferenceError`. That "reserved-but-untouchable" window is the **TDZ**.
- **Function declarations** → stored **whole**, so you can call them above their definition.
- **Function *expressions*** (`const f = function(){}`) follow their variable's rules — the *variable* is hoisted, the function value is not.

```
   A let variable's lifetime inside its scope:

   scope starts ──────[ TDZ ]──────▶ `let x = 5;` ──────▶ usable
                 reserved but        initialized          normal
                 untouchable         here                 reads/writes
                 (read = throws)

   var has NO TDZ: it's `undefined` from the very start.
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A `let` variable is like a **hotel room reserved under your name but not yet checked in**. The front desk knows the room is *yours* (the name is reserved), but until you actually check in (the declaration line), you can't go up and use it — try, and security stops you (the `ReferenceError`). A `var` room, by contrast, is unlocked and empty from the moment you book it.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// --- var: hoisted AND initialized to undefined ---
console.log(a);   // undefined  (no error)
var a = 1;
console.log(a);   // 1

// --- let: hoisted but in the TDZ until its line ---
try {
  console.log(b); // throws before reaching the next line
} catch (e) {
  console.log(e.name); // "ReferenceError"
  // message: "Cannot access 'b' before initialization"
}
let b = 2;
console.log(b);   // 2

// --- function declaration: fully hoisted, callable above ---
hello();          // "hi"  (works!)
function hello() { console.log("hi"); }
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is the TDZ?**
A: The **Temporal Dead Zone** is the span from the start of a scope until a `let`/`const`'s declaration line, during which the variable is hoisted (it *exists*) but **uninitialized**, so any read or write throws a `ReferenceError: Cannot access '…' before initialization`. It's why `let`/`const` "aren't accessible before declaration" while `var` quietly returns `undefined`. The TDZ exists to catch use-before-declare bugs that `var` silently hid.

---

### 🔒 Closures — Functions That Remember Where They Were Born

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **closure** is a function bundled together with the **variables from the scope where it was defined**. Even after that outer function has returned and its context is gone, the inner function can still read and update those captured variables — they stay alive as long as the closure does.

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
Closures are how JavaScript does **private state**. Without them, the only way to remember data between calls would be global variables (which anyone can clobber). Closures let a function keep its own persistent, hidden data — powering counters, caches, event handlers, and the entire module pattern.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
When a function is **created**, the engine attaches a hidden link (`[[Environment]]`) to the environment it was born in. Calling the function later resolves outer names through that saved link, not through whoever called it. The captured environment can't be garbage-collected while the closure is reachable — that's the **memory cost**.

The single most important rule: **a closure closes over the *variable*, not the *value***. It holds a live reference to the binding, so if that variable changes later, the closure sees the **new** value.

```
   makeCounter() runs, then RETURNS — its context pops off
   the stack, BUT its environment survives because the
   returned function still links to it:

   returned fn ──[[Environment]]──▶ { count: 0 }   ← kept alive
                                       ▲
              each call reads/updates the SAME live binding
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A closure is a **backpack** the function carries everywhere. When the function is created, it packs the variables from its home scope into the backpack. No matter where it's later unzipped and run, it reaches into that *same* backpack — and since everyone shares the one backpack, a change one function makes is seen by the others.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
function makeCounter() {
  let count = 0;                 // private — lives in the backpack
  return function () {
    count++;                     // updates the SAME captured binding
    return count;
  };
}

const next = makeCounter();
console.log(next()); // 1
console.log(next()); // 2
console.log(next()); // 3   (count persists between calls)

const other = makeCounter();
console.log(other()); // 1   (a separate counter — its own backpack)

// "closes over the VARIABLE, not the value" — proof:
let msg = "first";
const show = () => console.log(msg); // captures the binding `msg`
msg = "second";                      // change it AFTER defining show
show();                              // "second"  (sees the new value!)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Explain closures with a real use case.**
A: A closure is a function that retains access to variables from the scope where it was defined, even after that scope has returned. A real use case is a **counter or rate limiter with private state**: `makeCounter` returns a function that keeps incrementing a hidden `count` no outside code can touch — giving encapsulation without globals. The same mechanism powers memoization caches, debounced handlers, and the module pattern. The key subtlety to mention: a closure captures the **variable (binding)**, not a snapshot of its value.

---

### 🐛 The Classic `for`-Loop Bug — `var` vs `let` (The #1 Closure Question)

#### 🧩 What is it?
The most-asked closure puzzle: a loop that creates functions (or `setTimeout` callbacks) referencing the loop counter. With `var` they all print the **same** final number; with `let` each prints its **own** value. This is the closure-captures-the-*variable* rule in action.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
It's the single most common closure interview question, and the bug shows up for real — attaching handlers in a loop, scheduling timeouts, building callbacks. Misunderstand it and you ship code where every button "thinks" it's the last one.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **`var`** is **function-scoped**: the whole loop shares **one** `i` binding. By the time the deferred callbacks run, the loop has finished and that one `i` is `3`. All closures read the same backpack → `3, 3, 3`.
- **`let`** is **block-scoped**: the engine creates a **fresh `i` binding for each iteration**, copying the current value in. Each closure captures its *own* `i` → `0, 1, 2`.

```
   var  →  ONE shared binding             let  →  NEW binding per loop
   ┌──────────────┐                       ┌──────────────┐
   │ i ───────────┼─ all 3 fns point      │ i=0  ◀─ fn0  │
   │   (ends = 3) │   here  ▶ 3,3,3       │ i=1  ◀─ fn1  │  ▶ 0,1,2
   └──────────────┘                       │ i=2  ◀─ fn2  │
                                          └──────────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
With `var`, three people write *"call extension #i"* on sticky notes that all point to **one shared whiteboard cell**. By the time anyone reads their note, the cell has been overwritten to its final value — everyone dials the same wrong number. With `let`, each person gets their **own private card** with the number copied at that moment — so each dials correctly.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// --- THE BUG: var shares ONE binding ---
const bug = [];
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  bug.push(function () { return i; });
}
console.log(bug[0](), bug[1](), bug[2]()); // 3 3 3
// every function reads the SAME i, which is 3 after the loop ends

// --- THE FIX: let gives each iteration its OWN binding ---
const fixed = [];
for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
  fixed.push(function () { return j; });
}
console.log(fixed[0](), fixed[1](), fixed[2]()); // 0 1 2

// Same story with setTimeout (deferred callbacks):
for (var k = 0; k < 3; k++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log("var:", k), 0); // var: 3  var: 3  var: 3
}
for (let m = 0; m < 3; m++) {
  setTimeout(() => console.log("let:", m), 0); // let: 0  let: 1  let: 2
}

// Pre-let fix (still seen in old code): an IIFE to snapshot the value
for (var n = 0; n < 3; n++) {
  (function (copy) {
    setTimeout(() => console.log("iife:", copy), 0); // iife: 0  1  2
  })(n);
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why does `let` in a `for` loop behave differently from `var`?**
A: Because `var` is **function-scoped** — the entire loop shares a **single** `i` binding, so every closure created inside the loop captures that one variable and sees its final value (e.g. `3`). `let` is **block-scoped** and the spec creates a **fresh binding per iteration** (copying the previous value forward), so each closure captures its own `i` and sees the value from its iteration (`0, 1, 2`). Pre-ES2015, you reproduced `let`'s behavior with an **IIFE** that passed `i` in as an argument to snapshot it.

---
<h2 id="module-4">Module 4 — `this`, Binding & Function Invocation</h2>

In most languages `this` means "the current object" and never changes. In JavaScript it's the trickiest keyword in the language: the *same* function can see a different `this` every time you call it, decided not by where it was written but by **how it was called**. This module makes that rule boringly predictable.

---

### 🎯 The Four Binding Rules — How `this` Gets Decided

#### 🧩 What is it?
`this` is a special keyword that points at some object while a function runs. Its value is **not** fixed when you write the function — it's chosen fresh at the **call site** (the exact spot where the function is invoked). There are exactly **four rules**, and they're checked in priority order.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The same function often needs to act on *different* objects. A `sayHi` method should greet whichever user it belongs to. If `this` were locked at definition time, you'd have to write a separate function per object. Dynamic `this` lets one function serve many objects — but it also means you must know the four rules, or `this` will silently be the wrong thing.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Look at the call site and ask these questions **top to bottom** — the first match wins:

```
 Was it called with `new`?      ──► new binding: this = brand-new object
        │ no
        ▼
 Called with .call/.apply/.bind? ──► explicit binding: this = what you passed
        │ no
        ▼
 Called as `obj.fn()` (a dot)?   ──► implicit binding: this = obj (left of dot)
        │ no
        ▼
 Plain `fn()` call               ──► default binding:
                                     strict mode  -> this = undefined
                                     sloppy mode  -> this = globalThis
```

The biggest beginner trap is implicit binding: only the object **immediately left of the dot at the call** counts. Pull the method off into a plain variable and call it bare, and you fall all the way down to **default** binding — `this` is lost.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of `this` as the word "**you**" shouted across a room. The word itself never changes, but *who it points at* depends entirely on **who the speaker is facing** at that moment. The call site is the speaker's facing direction. Move the speaker (call the function differently) and "you" points at someone else.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
"use strict"; // so default binding gives `undefined`, not globalThis

const user = {
  name: "Tharun",
  sayHi() { return `Hi, ${this.name}`; },
};

// IMPLICIT: object left of the dot is `user`
console.log(user.sayHi());        // "Hi, Tharun"  -> this = user

// DEFAULT: pulled off the object, called bare -> this is lost
const bare = user.sayHi;
// console.log(bare());           // TypeError: Cannot read properties of
                                  // undefined (reading 'name') -> this = undefined

// EXPLICIT: force `this` with .call()
console.log(user.sayHi.call({ name: "Asha" })); // "Hi, Asha"

// NEW: `this` becomes a fresh object (see the `new` topic below)
function Person(name) { this.name = name; }
console.log(new Person("Rex").name);            // "Rex"
```
> **Note (simplification):** In **sloppy mode** (no `"use strict"`), default binding makes `this` the global object (`globalThis`), so `bare()` would read `globalThis.name` (`undefined` or `""`) instead of throwing. Modern code and all ES modules and `class` bodies are strict by default — assume the strict behavior.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What determines the value of `this` inside a regular function?**
A: The **call site**, not the definition. Check four rules in priority order: `new` binding (called with `new`) → explicit binding (`call`/`apply`/`bind`) → implicit binding (`obj.fn()`, `this` is the object left of the dot) → default binding (bare `fn()`, `this` is `undefined` in strict mode or `globalThis` in sloppy mode).

---

### 🔧 `call`, `apply` & `bind` — Borrowing and Locking `this`

#### 🧩 What is it?
These three methods live on every function and let you set `this` **explicitly** instead of relying on a dot.
- **`call(thisArg, a, b)`** — run the function *now*, args listed one by one.
- **`apply(thisArg, [a, b])`** — run the function *now*, args passed as one array.
- **`bind(thisArg, a, b)`** — don't run yet; return a **new function** with `this` (and any leading args) permanently locked in.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Implicit binding is fragile — the moment a method is passed as a callback, `this` is lost. You also sometimes want one object to "borrow" another's method. `call`/`apply` solve borrowing and one-off invocation; `bind` solves "I need to hand this method to someone else later and guarantee `this` survives."

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`call` and `apply` are twins — they differ **only** in how arguments arrive (a list vs. an array). `bind` is different in *time*: it produces a new function for later, with `this` (and optional **partial arguments** — pre-filled leading args) baked in. A bound function's `this` can never be re-pointed again, even by another `bind` or `call`.

```
   call(ctx, a, b)   --> runs NOW, args spread:  fn(a, b)
   apply(ctx, [a,b]) --> runs NOW, args array:   fn(a, b)
   bind(ctx, a)      --> returns g; later g(b) runs fn(a, b)
                         with `this` = ctx forever
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`call` and `apply` are like **handing a chef one specific kitchen to cook in, right now** — the only difference is whether you list the ingredients aloud (`call`) or hand over a labeled box of them (`apply`). `bind` is **signing a contract** that says "from now on, whenever this recipe is made, it's *always* made in *this* kitchen" — you hand the contract to someone else, and the kitchen can never be swapped.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
function intro(greeting, punctuation) {
  return `${greeting}, I'm ${this.name}${punctuation}`;
}
const me = { name: "Tharun" };

// call: args one by one, runs immediately
console.log(intro.call(me, "Hello", "!"));   // "Hello, I'm Tharun!"

// apply: args as an array, runs immediately
console.log(intro.apply(me, ["Hi", "."]));   // "Hi, I'm Tharun."

// bind: locks `this` (and the partial arg "Hey") -> new function
const sayHey = intro.bind(me, "Hey");
console.log(sayHey("?"));                     // "Hey, I'm Tharun?"

// classic apply trick: spread an array as args (Math.max takes a list)
console.log(Math.max.apply(null, [4, 1, 9, 2])); // 9
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `call`, `apply`, and `bind`?**
A: `call` and `apply` both invoke the function immediately with a chosen `this`; the only difference is `call` takes arguments individually while `apply` takes them as a single array. `bind` doesn't invoke — it returns a *new* function with `this` (and any partial arguments) permanently fixed, to be called later.

---

### 🛠️ Implementing `call`, `apply` & `bind` From Scratch — The Classic Test

#### 🧩 What is it?
A staple "build it yourself" interview task: re-create `call`, `apply`, and `bind` using nothing but the rules you already know. The whole trick rests on **implicit binding** — if you temporarily stick the function onto the target object as a property and call it *through the dot*, the engine sets `this` to that object for free.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
It proves you actually understand `this` rather than memorizing API names. It also shows you can reason about edge cases: avoiding property-name collisions, handling a missing context, and (for `bind`) making the result still work when called with `new`.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
 fn.myCall(ctx, ...args)
     │  attach fn onto ctx under a UNIQUE key
     ▼
   ctx[key] = fn
     │  call THROUGH the dot -> implicit binding sets this = ctx
     ▼
   result = ctx[key](...args)
     │  clean up so we don't leave junk on ctx
     ▼
   delete ctx[key]  ──►  return result
```
We use a `Symbol` (a guaranteed-unique value) as the temporary key so we never overwrite a real property the object already had.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like making someone an **honorary member of a club just long enough to use the members' door**. You pin a temporary badge on the function (`ctx[key] = fn`), walk it in through the members' entrance (the dot, which sets `this`), then quietly take the badge back on the way out (`delete`). To everyone watching, nothing was ever added.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// --- myCall: invoke now, args spread ---
Function.prototype.myCall = function (ctx, ...args) {
  ctx = ctx ?? globalThis;          // null/undefined -> default to global
  const key = Symbol("fn");         // unique key: never clobbers real props
  ctx[key] = this;                  // `this` here IS the function being called
  const result = ctx[key](...args); // call through the dot -> implicit binding
  delete ctx[key];                  // clean up the temporary property
  return result;
};

// --- myApply: same, but args arrive as an array ---
Function.prototype.myApply = function (ctx, args = []) {
  ctx = ctx ?? globalThis;
  const key = Symbol("fn");
  ctx[key] = this;
  const result = ctx[key](...args);
  delete ctx[key];
  return result;
};

// --- myBind: returns a new function with `this` + partial args locked ---
Function.prototype.myBind = function (ctx, ...bound) {
  const fn = this;                          // remember the original function
  return function (...rest) {
    return fn.apply(ctx, [...bound, ...rest]); // merge preset + later args
  };
};

function greet(greeting) { return `${greeting}, ${this.name}`; }
const user = { name: "Tharun" };

console.log(greet.myCall(user, "Hi"));          // "Hi, Tharun"
console.log(greet.myApply(user, ["Hello"]));    // "Hello, Tharun"
console.log(greet.myBind(user, "Hey")());       // "Hey, Tharun"
```
> **Note (simplification):** A fully spec-faithful `bind` also makes the bound function usable with `new` (when called as a constructor, the `new` target's `this` should win over `ctx`) and copies the original's `name`/`length`. The version above nails the everyday behavior — `this` plus partial application — which is what interviews almost always want.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Implement `bind` from scratch. What's the core idea?**
A: Capture the original function in a closure, then return a *new* function that, when called, invokes the original via `apply` with the locked-in `this` and merges the preset (partial) arguments with the new ones: `return (...rest) => fn.apply(ctx, [...bound, ...rest])`. The closure is what keeps `ctx` and the preset args alive for every future call.

---

### 🏹 Arrow Functions & Lexical `this` — The Rule That Has No Rule

#### 🧩 What is it?
An arrow function (`() => …`) does **not** get its own `this`. It has no binding rules at all — it simply **inherits `this` from the surrounding code** where it was *written* (this is called **lexical** `this`: decided by position in the source, not by the call). It also has no `arguments` object and cannot be used with `new`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The single most common `this` bug: you pass a regular function as a callback, it gets invoked bare, and default binding wipes out `this`. Before arrow functions, people fixed this with `const self = this;` or `.bind(this)`. Arrow functions bake the fix into the syntax — inside an arrow, `this` is *whatever it was outside*, so callbacks just work.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A regular function decides `this` at the call site. An arrow function **ignores the call site entirely** and reaches up to the nearest enclosing regular function (or module/global scope) for `this`:

```
   method() {              // regular fn: `this` = the object
     setTimeout(function () {
       this   // ← call site is bare -> this = undefined (lost!)
     });
     setTimeout(() => {
       this   // ← arrow: borrows `this` from method() -> the object
     });
   }
```

Because an arrow has no `this` of its own, `call`/`apply`/`bind` **cannot change it** — they run, but the `this` argument is simply ignored. And since `new` works by assigning a brand-new object to the function's `this`, an arrow (which has no `this` slot to assign) **throws** when you try to `new` it.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A regular function is a **new employee who asks "whose desk am I working at today?"** every single morning (call site decides). An arrow function is a **kid who just uses whatever's in their parents' house** — they don't have their own address, so they permanently share their parents' (`this` from the enclosing scope). You can't assign the kid a separate office (`new`) because they have no address slot of their own.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const counter = {
  count: 0,
  // ❌ regular function callback: `this` is lost when setTimeout calls it bare
  brokenStart() {
    setTimeout(function () {
      this.count++;                  // `this` is undefined/globalThis here
      console.log("broken:", this.count);
    }, 0);
  },
  // ✅ arrow callback: `this` lexically = `counter`
  workingStart() {
    setTimeout(() => {
      this.count++;                  // `this` is `counter`
      console.log("working:", this.count); // "working: 1"
    }, 0);
  },
};
counter.workingStart();              // logs "working: 1"

// arrows ignore call/apply/bind:
const arrow = () => this;            // captured at module/top level
console.log(arrow.call({ x: 1 }) === arrow()); // true -> `this` unchanged

// arrows can't be constructors:
const Arrow = () => {};
// new Arrow();                      // TypeError: Arrow is not a constructor
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why can't an arrow function be used with `new`, and how does its `this` work?**
A: An arrow function has no `this` binding of its own — it captures `this` lexically from its enclosing scope, fixed at definition time, and `call`/`apply`/`bind` can't override it. The `new` operator works by creating a fresh object and assigning it to the constructor's `this`; an arrow has no internal `this` slot to assign to (and no `[[Construct]]` method), so calling `new` on one throws `TypeError: ... is not a constructor`.

---

### 🏗️ The `new` Operator & `myNew` — How Objects Are Born

#### 🧩 What is it?
`new Fn()` is the operator that turns an ordinary function into a **constructor** — a factory that builds and returns a new object. Behind the scenes it runs a fixed four-step recipe, and you can reproduce that recipe by hand as a `myNew` function.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Constructors (and the `class` syntax built on top of them) are how most JavaScript objects with shared behavior get created. If you don't know what `new` actually does, the `this`-binding and prototype wiring feel like magic — and you can't explain why a constructor that `return`s an object behaves differently from one that returns a number. Implementing `myNew` makes all of it concrete.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`new Fn(args)` performs exactly four steps:

```
 1. Create a fresh empty object:        obj = {}
 2. Link its prototype to the blueprint: obj.[[Prototype]] = Fn.prototype
 3. Run Fn with `this` = obj:            Fn.call(obj, ...args)
 4. Return obj  --  UNLESS step 3 itself
    returned its own OBJECT, in which
    case that object wins instead.
```

Step 4 is the subtle one: if the constructor explicitly returns an **object**, `new` hands back *that* object and discards the one it made. If the constructor returns a **primitive** (a number, string, `undefined`, etc.) — or returns nothing — `new` ignores it and gives you the freshly built object. The `[[Prototype]]` link from step 2 is what lets the new object find methods stored on `Fn.prototype`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`new` is a **factory line stamping out a product**: (1) grab a blank chassis, (2) bolt it to the model's blueprint so it inherits all the standard parts (`prototype`), (3) let the assembly robot (`constructor`) customize it, then (4) ship it off the line — *unless* the robot slaps down a completely different finished product of its own, in which case *that* one gets shipped and the blank chassis is scrapped.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
function myNew(Constructor, ...args) {
  // steps 1 + 2: fresh object whose [[Prototype]] is Constructor.prototype
  const obj = Object.create(Constructor.prototype);

  // step 3: run the constructor with `this` bound to the new object
  const result = Constructor.apply(obj, args);

  // step 4: if the constructor returned its OWN object, that wins;
  // otherwise (primitive / undefined) we return the object we built.
  return (result !== null && typeof result === "object") ? result : obj;
}

function Person(name) {
  this.name = name;
}
Person.prototype.greet = function () { return `Hi, I'm ${this.name}`; };

const a = myNew(Person, "Tharun");
const b = new Person("Tharun");      // real `new`, for comparison
console.log(a.name);                 // "Tharun"
console.log(a.greet());              // "Hi, I'm Tharun" -> prototype link works
console.log(a instanceof Person);    // true

// step-4 override: constructor returns its OWN object
function Weird() {
  this.ignored = 1;
  return { real: true };             // an object -> this one wins
}
console.log(myNew(Weird));           // { real: true }  (ignored: 1 is discarded)
console.log(new Weird());            // { real: true }  (same behavior)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does the `new` operator actually do, step by step?**
A: Four steps: (1) create a brand-new empty object; (2) set that object's internal `[[Prototype]]` to the constructor's `.prototype`; (3) call the constructor with `this` bound to the new object; (4) return the new object — *unless* the constructor explicitly returns its own object, in which case that object is returned instead (a returned **primitive** is ignored). You can replicate it with `Object.create(Fn.prototype)`, `Fn.apply(obj, args)`, and a check on the return type.

---
<h2 id="module-5">Module 5 — Objects, Prototypes & Inheritance</h2>

In JavaScript, an object isn't just a bag of keys and values — each property carries hidden *settings*, and every object secretly points at another object it can borrow from. Master these two ideas and `class`, inheritance, and `instanceof` stop being magic and become things you could rebuild by hand.

---

### 🏷️ Property Descriptors — The Hidden Settings on Every Property

#### 🧩 What is it?
Every property on an object isn't just a `key: value` pair — it secretly carries three on/off switches called a **descriptor**: `writable` (can you change the value?), `enumerable` (does it show up in loops?), and `configurable` (can you delete it or change these switches?). `Object.defineProperty` lets you set them by hand.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
When you write `obj.x = 1`, all three switches default to `true` — fully open. But built-in properties (like an array's `length`) and "constants" you want to protect need to be locked down. Without descriptors you couldn't create a read-only property, hide a property from `for...in`, or build the getters/setters libraries rely on. They're the foundation the whole object system sits on.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A "normal" property is a **data descriptor**: it has a `value` plus the three switches.

```
   obj.x  →  data descriptor
   ┌──────────────────────────────┐
   │ value:        42             │
   │ writable:     true  ← assign?│
   │ enumerable:   true  ← loops? │
   │ configurable: true  ← delete?│
   └──────────────────────────────┘
```

- `writable: false` → assignment is ignored (silent in non-strict, throws in strict).
- `enumerable: false` → skipped by `for...in`, `Object.keys`, spread `...`.
- `configurable: false` → can't `delete` it and (mostly) can't re-define it.

Key trap: a plain `obj.x = 1` makes all three `true`, but `Object.defineProperty` defaults every omitted switch to `false`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a property as a **document in a shared folder**, and the descriptor as its permissions sticker: *writable* = "can edit", *enumerable* = "shows in the folder listing", *configurable* = "can be deleted or have its permissions changed". A normal file is fully open; a locked legal record is read-only, visible, but un-deletable.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const user = {};

Object.defineProperty(user, 'id', {
  value: 7,
  writable: false,     // can't reassign
  enumerable: false,   // hidden from loops
  configurable: false, // can't delete or redefine
});

user.id = 99;          // ignored (silent in non-strict mode)
console.log(user.id);  // 7

console.log(Object.keys(user));       // []  (id is non-enumerable)
console.log('id' in user);            // true (it exists, just hidden)

delete user.id;                       // false-y; nothing happens
console.log(user.id);                 // 7

// Inspect the switches:
console.log(Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(user, 'id'));
// { value: 7, writable: false, enumerable: false, configurable: false }

// Contrast: a normal assignment is fully open
const o = {};
o.name = 'Ada';
console.log(Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(o, 'name'));
// { value: 'Ada', writable: true, enumerable: true, configurable: true }
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `obj.x = 1` and `Object.defineProperty(obj, 'x', { value: 1 })`?**
A: `obj.x = 1` creates a data property with all three flags `true` (writable, enumerable, configurable). `Object.defineProperty` defaults every flag you don't specify to `false` — so `{ value: 1 }` alone makes a read-only, non-enumerable, non-configurable property. The assignment form is "fully open by default"; `defineProperty` is "fully locked by default".

---

### 🎚️ Getters & Setters — Properties That Run Code

#### 🧩 What is it?
A getter/setter (an **accessor property**) looks like a normal property when you read or write it — `obj.fullName` — but instead of storing a value, it secretly runs a function. The getter runs on read; the setter runs on assignment.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Sometimes a "property" is really *derived* from other data (a full name from first + last) or needs **validation** on write (reject a negative age). With plain data properties you'd need explicit methods like `getFullName()` and `setAge(x)`. Accessors let you keep the clean `obj.fullName` syntax while running logic behind the scenes — and add validation later without changing call sites.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
An accessor property is a *different shape* of descriptor: instead of `value`/`writable`, it has `get` and `set` functions.

```
   obj.fullName  →  accessor descriptor
   ┌──────────────────────────────┐
   │ get:          ƒ () {...}      │ ← runs on read
   │ set:          ƒ (v) {...}     │ ← runs on assign
   │ enumerable:   true            │
   │ configurable: true            │
   └──────────────────────────────┘
   (no `value`, no `writable` — those are for DATA props)
```

A property is **either** a data property **or** an accessor — never both. Inside a getter/setter, `this` is the object you accessed the property on.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A data property is a **number painted on a sign** — you just read it. A getter is a **digital display wired to a sensor**: every time you look, it computes the current value fresh. A setter is the **thermostat dial** — turning it doesn't just store a number, it kicks off machinery (validation, side effects).

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const person = {
  first: 'Grace',
  last: 'Hopper',

  get fullName() {                 // runs on READ
    return `${this.first} ${this.last}`;
  },

  set fullName(value) {            // runs on ASSIGN
    [this.first, this.last] = value.split(' ');
  },
};

console.log(person.fullName);      // "Grace Hopper"  (getter ran)

person.fullName = 'Ada Lovelace';  // setter ran, split the string
console.log(person.first);         // "Ada"
console.log(person.last);          // "Lovelace"

// Validation example with defineProperty:
const account = { _balance: 0 };
Object.defineProperty(account, 'balance', {
  get() { return this._balance; },
  set(v) {
    if (v < 0) throw new Error('No negative balance');
    this._balance = v;
  },
});
account.balance = 100;             // ok
console.log(account.balance);      // 100
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How is an accessor property different from a data property, and can a property be both?**
A: A data property stores a `value` and has a `writable` flag. An accessor property stores `get` and/or `set` functions and has *no* `value`/`writable` — reading it calls the getter, assigning calls the setter. A single property is **either** data **or** accessor, never both; trying to define both `value` and `get` in one descriptor throws a `TypeError`.

---

### 🔗 The Prototype Chain — How Objects Inherit

#### 🧩 What is it?
Every JavaScript object has a hidden internal link (the spec calls it `[[Prototype]]`) pointing to **another object**. When you read a property that an object doesn't have, the engine follows that link to the next object, then the next — a **chain** — until it finds the property or runs out (hitting `null`). That search path is the **prototype chain**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Imagine 10,000 user objects that all need a `greet` method. Copying the function onto each one wastes memory and means fixing a bug in 10,000 places. Instead, you put `greet` on **one** shared prototype object, and every user *inherits* it through the chain. This is how JavaScript does code reuse — it's prototype-based, not class-based, under the hood.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The single most confusing pair of names in JS:

- **`__proto__`** (and the real internal `[[Prototype]]`) is the link **an object follows** to find inherited properties. *Every* object has one.
- **`prototype`** is a property that lives **only on functions**. It's the object that will become the `[[Prototype]]` of instances made with `new`.

So `prototype` is the *blueprint slot on a constructor function*; `__proto__` is the *live link on an instance*. They connect like this: after `const d = new Dog()`, `d.__proto__ === Dog.prototype`.

```
   const d = new Dog('Rex')

   d  ──.__proto__──▶  Dog.prototype ──.__proto__──▶ Object.prototype
   ┌──────────┐        ┌────────────┐    ┌─────────────────────────┐
   │ name:Rex │        │ bark: ƒ    │    │ toString: ƒ, hasOwn...   │
   └──────────┘        └────────────┘    └───────────┬─────────────┘
                                                     │.__proto__
                                                     ▼
                                                   null   ← chain ends

   Lookup d.bark:  not on d → found on Dog.prototype ✓
   Lookup d.xyz :  not on d → not on Dog.prototype
                            → not on Object.prototype → undefined
```

`Object.create(proto)` makes a fresh object whose `[[Prototype]]` is exactly `proto` you pass — the most direct way to set the chain.

> **Note (simplification):** `__proto__` is a legacy accessor (defined on `Object.prototype`) kept alive for compatibility. The spec-blessed tools are `Object.getPrototypeOf(obj)` to read the link and `Object.setPrototypeOf(obj, p)` to change it. We use `__proto__` here only because it reads clearly.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
The prototype chain is like **asking up a chain of command**. You need an answer; you don't have it, so you ask your manager; they don't know, so they ask *their* director. The question travels up until someone answers — or you reach the top (the CEO's boss is `null`) and the answer is "nobody knows" (`undefined`). Nobody copied the company handbook onto every desk — there's one shared copy higher up.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Build a chain by hand with Object.create:
const animal = {
  describe() { return `I am a ${this.type}`; },
};

const dog = Object.create(animal); // dog.__proto__ === animal
dog.type = 'dog';

console.log(dog.describe());                 // "I am a dog"  (inherited)
console.log(dog.hasOwnProperty('describe')); // false (it's on `animal`)
console.log(dog.hasOwnProperty('type'));     // true  (own property)

console.log(Object.getPrototypeOf(dog) === animal); // true

// __proto__ (instance link) vs prototype (function blueprint):
function Cat(name) { this.name = name; }
Cat.prototype.meow = function () { return `${this.name} says meow`; };

const c = new Cat('Felix');
console.log(c.meow());                          // "Felix says meow"
console.log(c.__proto__ === Cat.prototype);     // true
console.log(Cat.prototype.__proto__ === Object.prototype); // true
console.log(c.hasOwnProperty('prototype'));     // false — only functions have it
console.log(typeof Cat.prototype);              // "object"
console.log(typeof c.prototype);                // "undefined" — instances don't have it
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is the difference between `__proto__` and `prototype`?**
A: `prototype` is a property that exists only on **constructor functions** — it's the object that becomes the `[[Prototype]]` of any instance created with `new`. `__proto__` (the legacy accessor for the internal `[[Prototype]]`) exists on **every object** and is the *actual live link* the engine follows during property lookup. The relationship: `instance.__proto__ === Constructor.prototype`. Reading a property walks the `__proto__` chain; `prototype` is just the blueprint that seeds the first link.

---

### 🍬 `class` — Syntactic Sugar Over Prototypes

#### 🧩 What is it?
A `class` in JavaScript is **not** a new kind of thing — it's nicer-looking syntax that the engine turns into the exact constructor-function-and-prototype setup from the previous topic. "Syntactic sugar" just means a friendlier way to write something you could already do the long way.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The manual `Constructor.prototype.method = ...` pattern is verbose and easy to get wrong (forget `new`, mis-wire inheritance). `class` packages it into a clean, familiar block — with first-class support for inheritance (`extends`/`super`), truly private fields (`#x`), and `static` members — without changing the prototype machinery underneath.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A class **desugars** to a constructor function plus methods on its `prototype`:

```
   class Dog {              function Dog(name) {
     constructor(name) {       this.name = name;
       this.name = name;     }
     }              ≈        Dog.prototype.bark =
     bark() {...}              function () {...};
   }
```

Differences from the hand-rolled version (not *just* cosmetic):
- Class **methods are non-enumerable** (hidden from `for...in`) — manual `prototype` assignments are enumerable.
- The whole class body runs in **strict mode**.
- Calling a class **without `new` throws** — a plain constructor function silently runs with the wrong `this`.
- **`#fields`** are *truly* private — unreachable from outside, not just by convention. **`static`** members live on the class itself, not on instances. **`super`** calls the parent.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`class` is **flat-pack furniture with a picture manual** versus the prototype version's **raw lumber and a list of joinery instructions**. You end up with the identical chair — the manual just makes assembly clearer and harder to botch.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
class Animal {
  static kingdom = 'Animalia';   // static: on the class, not instances
  #energy = 100;                 // private field: only this class can see it

  constructor(name) { this.name = name; }

  rest() { this.#energy += 10; return this.#energy; }
  get energy() { return this.#energy; } // expose read-only via getter
}

class Dog extends Animal {
  constructor(name, breed) {
    super(name);                 // MUST call super before using `this`
    this.breed = breed;
  }
  speak() { return `${this.name} (${this.breed}) barks`; }
}

const d = new Dog('Rex', 'Lab');
console.log(d.speak());          // "Rex (Lab) barks"
console.log(d.rest());           // 110   (inherited method touching #energy)
console.log(d.energy);           // 110
console.log(Animal.kingdom);     // "Animalia"  (static, read off the class)

// Proof it's still prototypes underneath:
console.log(Object.getPrototypeOf(d) === Dog.prototype);          // true
console.log(Object.getPrototypeOf(Dog.prototype) === Animal.prototype); // true

// Methods are non-enumerable:
console.log(Object.keys(Dog.prototype)); // []  (speak is hidden)

// Privacy & new-enforcement:
console.log(d.energy);           // 110 (no way to read d.#energy directly)
try { Dog('x'); } catch (e) { console.log(e.constructor.name); } // "TypeError"
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Is `class` just syntactic sugar? Name a way it actually differs from a constructor function.**
