```
              ┌───────────────────────┐
              │  iterator.next()      │──▶ { value: 1, done: false }
              │  iterator.next()      │──▶ { value: 2, done: false }
              │  iterator.next()      │──▶ { value: 3, done: false }
              │  iterator.next()      │──▶ { value: undefined,
              │                       │      done: true }  ← STOP
              └───────────────────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a **vending machine**. You don't reach inside and grab items by position — you press the button (`next()`) and it hands you a tray that says either "here's your snack" (`{ value, done: false }`) or "sold out" (`{ value: undefined, done: true }`). You keep pressing until it says sold out. You never need to know *how* it stores the snacks inside.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Manually driving the protocol on an array, no for...of:
const arr = ["a", "b"];
const it = arr[Symbol.iterator](); // get the iterator

console.log(it.next()); // { value: "a", done: false }
console.log(it.next()); // { value: "b", done: false }
console.log(it.next()); // { value: undefined, done: true }
console.log(it.next()); // { value: undefined, done: true }  (stays done)
```
> **Note (simplification):** the result object can carry extra keys and the protocol has optional `return()`/`throw()` methods for cleanup. The `{ value, done }` core is all you need 99% of the time.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between an *iterable* and an *iterator*?**
A: An **iterable** is an object that *can be iterated* — it has a `[Symbol.iterator]()` method that produces an iterator. An **iterator** is the worker that actually walks the sequence — it has a `next()` method returning `{ value, done }`. An array is iterable; calling `arr[Symbol.iterator]()` gives you its iterator. (Many built-in iterators are *also* iterable — their `[Symbol.iterator]()` returns `this` — which is why you can use one directly in `for...of`.)

---

### 🛠️ Making a Custom Object Iterable — `range` from Scratch

#### 🧩 What is it?
Taking an ordinary object that JavaScript has *no idea* how to loop over, and teaching it the protocol by giving it a `[Symbol.iterator]()` method — so suddenly `for...of`, spread, and destructuring all just work on it.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A plain object like `{ from: 1, to: 3 }` is **not** iterable — `for (const x of {from:1,to:3})` throws `TypeError: ... is not iterable`. If you build your own data structure (a linked list, a tree, a number range), you want users to loop it with the same friendly syntax they use for arrays, instead of inventing a one-off `.forEach`-style method.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You add `[Symbol.iterator]()` and have it return a fresh iterator object with a `next()`. Keeping the loop state (like `current`) **inside the returned iterator** — not on the shared object — means each new loop starts clean and two loops don't fight over the same counter.

```
   range { from:1, to:3 }
        │  [Symbol.iterator]() called
        ▼
   new iterator { current:1, last:3 }
        │  next() → {value:1, done:false}
        │  next() → {value:2, done:false}
        │  next() → {value:3, done:false}
        │  next() → {value:undefined, done:true}
        ▼
        done
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
The `range` object is a **book**. Asking for `[Symbol.iterator]()` is like handing a reader a fresh **bookmark** set to page 1. Each reader gets their *own* bookmark, so two people can read the same book at the same time without losing each other's place. The bookmark (iterator) tracks position; the book (iterable) just holds the content.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const range = {
  from: 1,
  to: 3,
  [Symbol.iterator]() {
    let current = this.from;       // state lives in the iterator, not on range
    const last = this.to;
    return {
      next() {
        if (current <= last) {
          return { value: current++, done: false };
        }
        return { value: undefined, done: true };
      }
    };
  }
};

console.log([...range]);          // [1, 2, 3]   (spread uses the iterator)
for (const n of range) console.log(n); // 1, then 2, then 3
const [first, second] = range;    // destructuring also iterates
console.log(first, second);       // 1 2
console.log([...range]);          // [1, 2, 3]  again — fresh iterator each time
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Make an object iterable that yields the numbers from `from` to `to`.**
A: Add a `[Symbol.iterator]()` method that returns an object with a `next()` producing `{ value, done }` (see the `range` above). The key detail interviewers look for: keep the cursor (`current`) **inside the returned iterator**, so the object is *re-iterable* — every `for...of` or spread gets a fresh, independent walk instead of resuming a shared, exhausted counter.

---

### 📦 Built-in Iterables — What You Can Already Loop

#### 🧩 What is it?
The data types that ship with `[Symbol.iterator]()` already wired in, so they work in `for...of`/spread out of the box: **`Array`**, **`String`** (by code points, so emoji stay whole), **`Map`**, **`Set`**, the function-local **`arguments`** object, **TypedArrays**, and DOM collections like **NodeList**. The glaring exception: a **plain object `{}` is NOT iterable**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this?
It explains a daily confusion: `for...of` over an array works, but `for...of` over `{ a: 1 }` throws. Knowing the line between "iterable" and "not" tells you when you must reach for `Object.keys()`, `Object.entries()`, or `for...in` instead — and why `[...someString]` splits text correctly while older index tricks mangle emoji.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Each built-in defines what its iterator yields:
- `Array` / `String` / `Set` → the values.
- `Map` → `[key, value]` pairs (handy with destructuring).
- Objects → nothing; you iterate a *view* of them: `Object.keys()`, `.values()`, or `.entries()` (all of which return arrays, which *are* iterable).

```javascript
console.log([..."hej"]);              // ["h", "e", "j"]
console.log([...new Set([1, 1, 2])]); // [1, 2]
console.log([...new Map([["a", 1]])]);// [["a", 1]]

const user = { name: "Ada", age: 36 };
// for (const x of user) {}           // ❌ TypeError: user is not iterable
for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(user)) {
  console.log(k, v);                  // "name" "Ada"  then  "age" 36
}
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Built-in iterables are **appliances that come with a power cord** — plug them straight into the `for...of` socket. A plain object is an appliance with **no cord in the box**: perfectly usable, but you must add an adapter (`Object.entries()`) before it fits the socket.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why does `for...of` fail on a plain object but `for...in` works?**
A: `for...of` consumes the **iterable protocol** (`[Symbol.iterator]`), which plain objects don't implement, so it throws. `for...in` is older and different: it walks an object's **enumerable string keys** (including inherited ones) without any iterator. For objects, prefer `for...of Object.keys(obj)` / `Object.entries(obj)` — you iterate an array (which *is* iterable) and avoid `for...in`'s inherited-key surprises.

---

### 🗺️ `Map` · `Set` · `WeakMap` · `WeakSet` — Choosing the Right Collection

#### 🧩 What is it?
Four keyed collections beyond plain objects and arrays:
- **`Map`** — an ordered dictionary of key → value where **keys can be anything** (objects, functions, numbers — not just strings).
- **`Set`** — a collection of **unique** values (no duplicates).
- **`WeakMap`** / **`WeakSet`** — like `Map`/`Set`, but they hold their keys/members **weakly**: the collection does *not* keep them alive, so the **garbage collector** (the engine's automatic memory cleaner — see Module 2) can reclaim them.

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
Plain objects have three sharp edges: keys are silently **coerced to strings** (`obj[1]` and `obj["1"]` collide; an object key becomes the useless string `"[object Object]"`), they **inherit** properties (`"toString" in {}` is `true`), and there's no built-in `.size`. `Map` fixes all three. `Set` gives you "is this already here?" and de-duplication for free. And a normal `Map` used as a cache **leaks memory** — it pins every key forever; `WeakMap` lets cached entries vanish when their key object is gone.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Key equality in `Map`/`Set` uses **SameValueZero** — basically `===`, with two tweaks: `NaN` is treated as equal to itself (so `NaN` works as a key), and `+0` and `-0` count as the *same* key. Crucially, **object keys match by identity**, not contents: two different `{}` literals are two different keys.

```
            keys     iterable?  GC-friendly?   key/member type
  Map      any        yes          no          anything
  Set      values     yes          no          anything
  WeakMap  objects*   NO           YES          objects only*
  WeakSet  objects*   NO           YES          objects only*
   (* + unregistered Symbols, since ES2023)
```
`WeakMap`/`WeakSet` are **not iterable** and have **no `.size`** — by design. If you could list them, the GC couldn't safely remove entries behind your back.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A `Map` is the **building's permanent guest registry** — once you sign in, your entry stays until someone manually crosses it out (it can pin people in memory forever). A `WeakMap` is a **visitor badge tied to the actual person**: the moment they leave the building for good (no references left), the badge dissolves on its own and the registry forgets them — no cleanup shift required.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Map: object keys + SameValueZero
const m = new Map();
const a = {}, b = {};
m.set(a, "first").set(b, "second");
console.log(m.get(a));          // "first"
console.log(m.get({}));         // undefined  (a fresh {} is a different key)
m.set(NaN, "not-a-number");
console.log(m.get(NaN));        // "not-a-number"  (NaN matches itself here)
console.log(m.size);            // 3   (O(1), no Object.keys needed)

// Set: uniqueness / de-dupe
const s = new Set([1, 1, 2, 2, 3]);
console.log([...s]);            // [1, 2, 3]
console.log(s.has(2));          // true

// WeakMap: metadata that auto-clears when the key object dies
const meta = new WeakMap();
let node = { id: 1 };
meta.set(node, { clicks: 0 }); // attach private data to `node`
node = null;                   // last reference gone → GC may drop the entry
// (no way to observe it: WeakMap has no .size and isn't iterable — by design)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When would you use a `Map` over a plain object — and when a `WeakMap`?**
A: Use a **`Map`** when keys aren't fixed strings (objects, numbers, functions), when insertion **order** or **`.size`** matters, or to dodge prototype surprises (`"toString" in {}` is `true`). Use a **plain object** for small fixed-shape records you'll JSON-serialize. Use a **`WeakMap`** to attach data to an object **without keeping it alive** — caches and per-object private metadata — so entries are garbage-collected automatically and you don't leak.

---

### ✨ Spread, Destructuring & Rest — All Powered by Iteration

#### 🧩 What is it?
Three syntaxes that look like separate features but, for arrays and other iterables, all run on the **same iterator protocol** under the hood:
- **Spread** `...` — expands an iterable into individual items (`[...a, ...b]`, `fn(...args)`).
- **Array destructuring** `const [x, y] = it` — pulls items out by *iterating*, not by index.
- **Rest** `const [first, ...others] = it` — gathers the remaining iterated items into a new array.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
People assume `[...x]` is "an array thing." It isn't — it's an **"any iterable" thing**. That's *why* `[...new Set(arr)]` de-dupes, `[..."hi"]` splits a string, and `[...map]` gives pairs. It also explains a classic gotcha: **object** spread `{...obj}` is the odd one out — it does **not** use the iterator protocol at all; it copies own enumerable properties. So an object can be perfectly spreadable with `{...}` yet still throw on `[...]`.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Array-spread, array-destructuring, and rest all call `[Symbol.iterator]()` and drain `next()`. Object spread/rest is a *different* mechanism — own-enumerable property copying — with no iteration involved.

```
  ARRAY context  →  uses [Symbol.iterator]   (iteration)
    [...iterable]      const [a, b] = iterable
    fn(...iterable)    const [a, ...rest] = iterable

  OBJECT context →  copies own enumerable props (NO iteration)
    { ...obj }         const { a, ...rest } = obj
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Array spread is a **Pez dispenser**: it doesn't grab candies by position, it just clicks out "next, next, next" until empty (driving the iterator). Object spread is a **photocopier**: it doesn't go in sequence — it lays the whole sheet down and duplicates every visible field at once. Same `...` symbol, two completely different machines.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
function sum(...nums) {                // rest: gather args into an array
  return nums.reduce((t, n) => t + n, 0);
}
const list = [1, 2, 3];
console.log(sum(...list));             // 6   (spread expands the iterable)

const [head, ...tail] = list;          // destructure + rest, via iteration
console.log(head, tail);               // 1 [2, 3]

console.log([...new Set([4, 4, 5])]);  // [4, 5]  works because Set is iterable
console.log([..."hi"]);                // ["h", "i"]  strings are iterable

const point = { x: 1, y: 2 };
console.log({ ...point, z: 3 });       // { x: 1, y: 2, z: 3 }  (no iterator used)
// console.log([...point]);            // ❌ TypeError: point is not iterable
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why does `[...someSet]` work, and why does `[...{a:1}]` throw while `{...{a:1}}` is fine?**
A: Array spread/destructuring/rest consume the **iterable protocol**, and `Set` implements `[Symbol.iterator]` — so it expands fine. A plain object has no `[Symbol.iterator]`, so `[...{a:1}]` throws `not iterable`. But **object** spread `{...obj}` is a separate feature that copies own enumerable properties without iterating at all, so `{...{a:1}}` works regardless.

---

### 🧱 Packed vs. Holey Arrays — Why Sparse Arrays Get Slow

#### 🧩 What is it?
V8 secretly tracks what *kind* of array you have. A **packed** array has every slot from `0` to `length−1` filled with no gaps. A **holey** (sparse) array has at least one missing index — a "hole" — created by skipping indexes, deleting elements, or growing `length` past the data.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
Arrays *feel* uniform, but V8 stores them in different internal modes ("**elements kinds**") for speed. The moment you punch a hole, V8 demotes the array to a slower mode for **all** access on it — and that demotion is **sticky**, it doesn't un-do itself. A single careless `arr[100] = x` on a 3-element array can quietly slow every later read.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A packed array of plain numbers lives in a tight, contiguous block V8 reads by direct offset — instant. Create a hole and V8 must switch to a representation that records "this index is absent," so each read must also **walk the prototype chain** to confirm the hole isn't a property defined upstream. That bookkeeping is far slower than a flat offset read.

```
  PACKED  [10, 20, 30]        contiguous, fast offset reads
          ┌──┬──┬──┐
          │10│20│30│
          └──┴──┴──┘

  HOLEY   arr[5] = 99   →   [10,20,30, <hole>,<hole>, 99]
          ┌──┬──┬──┬───┬───┬──┐
          │10│20│30│ ✗ │ ✗ │99│   each read must check
          └──┴──┴──┴───┴───┴──┘   "is this a real hole?" → slow
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A packed array is a **fully-booked airplane row** — the attendant walks straight down counting filled seats. A holey array is a row with **random empty seats**: now they must stop at every gap and double-check "is this truly empty, or is someone in the bathroom?" (the prototype-chain check). The scattered gaps make the whole walk slower.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// ✅ PACKED — stays fast
const fast = [1, 2, 3];
fast.push(4);                 // still packed, contiguous

// ❌ HOLEY — these all punch holes and demote the array:
const holey1 = [1, 2, 3];
holey1[10] = 99;              // indexes 3–9 are holes → demoted (sticky!)

const holey2 = [1, 2, 3];
delete holey2[1];             // delete leaves a hole; length is still 3
console.log(holey2);          // [1, <1 empty item>, 3]
console.log(holey2[1]);       // undefined  (a hole, not a stored value)

const holey3 = new Array(3);  // ❌ pre-sized but empty → all holes
console.log(holey3);          // [ <3 empty items> ]

// Build big arrays packed instead:
const ok = Array.from({ length: 3 }, (_, i) => i); // [0, 1, 2] — packed
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why can `arr[100] = x` on a small array hurt performance?**
A: It turns a **packed** array into a **holey** one. V8 keeps packed arrays in a contiguous, fast-offset layout; introducing gaps demotes it to a slower "holey" elements kind where each access must also check the prototype chain for missing indexes. The demotion is **sticky** — it persists even after you fill the holes — so it's best to build arrays densely (e.g. `Array.from` or `push`) and avoid `delete` / out-of-range index assignment on hot arrays.

---
<h2 id="module-10">Module 10 — Metaprogramming</h2>

Most code *uses* objects: read a property, call a method, move on. **Metaprogramming** is code that *changes how those basic operations behave* — what happens when you read a missing property, what `typeof`-style checks report, what `obj.x = 5` actually does. It's JavaScript reaching in and rewiring its own rules. Used well, it powers reactive frameworks and validation layers; used carelessly, it makes code that's impossible to reason about.

---

### 🪞 `Proxy` — Intercepting the Basic Operations

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **`Proxy`** is a wrapper you put around an object (the **target**) that lets you intercept and redefine the fundamental things people do to that object — reading a property, writing one, deleting one, checking if a key exists, even calling it like a function. You supply a second object called a **handler**, whose methods (called **traps**) run *instead of* the default behavior.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Before `Proxy`, the only way to react to a property access was to define that exact property ahead of time with a getter/setter (`Object.defineProperty`). But you can't define a getter for a property that *doesn't exist yet* — so you couldn't catch `user.naem` (a typo), supply a default for *any* missing key, or log *every* write generically. `Proxy` gives you one hook that fires for **all** keys, including ones nobody has created.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You create it with `new Proxy(target, handler)`. Each operation maps to one **trap** name. If the handler omits a trap, the operation falls through to the target normally.

```
   code: proxy.name          code: proxy.name = 'Sam'
        │                          │
        ▼                          ▼
   ┌─────────────┐            ┌─────────────┐
   │  get trap   │            │  set trap   │
   │ (run yours) │            │ (run yours) │
   └──────┬──────┘            └──────┬──────┘
          │ you decide               │ you decide
          ▼                          ▼
     real target               real target
```

The common traps and the operation each one intercepts:

- **`get(target, key, receiver)`** — reading `proxy.key`.
- **`set(target, key, value, receiver)`** — writing `proxy.key = value`. Must return `true` if the write is accepted, or a falsy value to make it fail (throws in strict mode).
- **`has(target, key)`** — the `key in proxy` check.
- **`deleteProperty(target, key)`** — `delete proxy.key`.
- **`apply(target, thisArg, argsList)`** — calling `proxy(...args)` when the target is a function.
- **`construct(target, argsList, newTarget)`** — using `new proxy(...args)`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A `Proxy` is a **personal assistant sitting in front of someone's office**. Every visitor (operation) talks to the assistant first. The assistant can let you straight through (default behavior), take a message and change it (transform the value), turn you away (block the write), or answer on the boss's behalf for questions the boss never explicitly prepared for (missing keys). The boss (target) is untouched; the assistant (handler) decides what each interaction means.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const target = { greeting: 'hi' };

const proxy = new Proxy(target, {
  get(obj, key) {
    // Fires for EVERY read, even keys that don't exist
    return key in obj ? obj[key] : `<no "${String(key)}">`;
  },
  set(obj, key, value) {
    console.log(`setting ${String(key)} = ${value}`);
    obj[key] = value;
    return true;            // signal the write succeeded
  },
  has(obj, key) {
    return key in obj;
  },
  deleteProperty(obj, key) {
    console.log(`deleting ${String(key)}`);
    delete obj[key];
    return true;
  },
});

console.log(proxy.greeting); // "hi"
console.log(proxy.missing);  // '<no "missing">'
proxy.name = 'Sam';          // logs: setting name = Sam
console.log('name' in proxy); // true
delete proxy.name;           // logs: deleting name
```

A function target uses the `apply` and `construct` traps:

```javascript
function add(a, b) { return a + b; }

const logged = new Proxy(add, {
  apply(fn, thisArg, args) {
    console.log(`called with ${args}`);
    return fn.apply(thisArg, args);
  },
});

console.log(logged(2, 3)); // logs "called with 2,3", then prints 5
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How is a `Proxy` different from a getter/setter defined with `Object.defineProperty`?**
A: A getter/setter is tied to **one named property you defined in advance**. A `Proxy` trap fires for **any** key — including ones that don't exist yet — and covers operations a getter can't touch, like `in`, `delete`, function calls, and `new`. So `Proxy` is the right tool for *generic* interception (defaults, logging every access, validation across all keys); `defineProperty` is for shaping a *specific, known* property.

---

### 🔁 `Reflect` — The Default Behaviors as Plain Functions

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`Reflect`** is a built-in object holding a set of static methods — `Reflect.get`, `Reflect.set`, `Reflect.has`, `Reflect.deleteProperty`, `Reflect.apply`, `Reflect.construct`, and more — that each perform the **exact default operation** a proxy trap is intercepting. `Reflect.get(obj, key)` does what `obj[key]` normally does; `Reflect.set(obj, key, val)` does what `obj[key] = val` normally does.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Inside a proxy trap, you often want to "do the normal thing, plus a little extra." You *could* write `obj[key]` by hand — but that subtly breaks down with getters and inheritance, because it loses the **`receiver`** (the object the access started on, which getters use as their `this`). `Reflect` methods take that `receiver` argument, so forwarding stays correct. There's a one-to-one pairing: every trap has a matching `Reflect` method with the *same arguments*, which makes "default + extra" trivial and bug-free.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The trap and the `Reflect` call line up argument-for-argument, so the cleanest trap body just forwards:

```
   get trap(target, key, receiver)
        │  add your logic here
        ▼
   Reflect.get(target, key, receiver)   ← does the real default read,
                                          preserving `receiver` for getters
```

`Reflect` also makes failures *non-throwing and checkable*. `Reflect.set` returns `true`/`false` instead of silently working or throwing, and `Reflect.deleteProperty` returns whether the delete succeeded — perfect for a `set`/`deleteProperty` trap, which is *required* to return such a boolean.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
If the proxy handler is the **office assistant**, `Reflect` is the **standard company rulebook** they keep on the desk. When the assistant decides "I'll handle this the normal way (but jot a note first)," they don't improvise the normal way from memory — they follow the rulebook exactly, so nothing subtle gets dropped. The assistant adds the note; the rulebook does the rest, identically every time.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const handler = {
  get(target, key, receiver) {
    console.log(`read ${String(key)}`);
    // Forward to the default read, KEEPING receiver so getters
    // resolve their `this` correctly:
    return Reflect.get(target, key, receiver);
  },
  set(target, key, value, receiver) {
    console.log(`write ${String(key)} = ${value}`);
    return Reflect.set(target, key, value, receiver); // returns boolean
  },
};

const obj = {
  _first: 'Ada',
  get name() { return this._first; }, // a getter relies on `this`
};
const p = new Proxy(obj, handler);

console.log(p.name); // logs "read name", "read _first", prints "Ada"
p.age = 30;          // logs "write age = 30"
```
> **Note (simplification):** plain `target[key]` works in this exact example because `receiver` happens to equal `target`. The `receiver`-forwarding matters once a proxy is used as a **prototype** of another object, or wraps an object with inherited getters — then `receiver` and `target` differ, and only `Reflect.get(target, key, receiver)` keeps the getter's `this` pointing at the right object.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why use `Reflect.get(target, key, receiver)` inside a `get` trap instead of just `target[key]`?**
A: Two reasons. First, correctness: `Reflect.get` forwards the `receiver`, so an inherited getter runs with its `this` bound to the original accessing object rather than the raw target — `target[key]` loses that. Second, symmetry: every trap has a same-signature `Reflect` method, so forwarding the default behavior is a clean one-liner, and `Reflect.set`/`deleteProperty` hand back the boolean those traps are required to return.

---

### ✨ Well-Known Symbols — Hooking Into Built-In Language Behavior

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **`Symbol`** is a unique, unforgeable value (glossed: a built-in "label" guaranteed not to clash with any other key). **Well-known symbols** are a handful of *pre-defined* symbols the language itself looks for on your objects — like `Symbol.iterator` and `Symbol.toPrimitive`. By giving your object a method under one of these symbol keys, you customize how built-in syntax (`for...of`, `+`, `instanceof`, `String()`) treats it.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You want *your* object to feel like a native one: loopable with `for...of`, sensible when added with `+`, recognizable by `instanceof`, and labeled nicely in logs. Without a hook, the engine has no idea what `myThing + 1` should mean. Well-known symbols are that hook — official extension points the spec promises to consult, instead of you monkey-patching internals.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Each symbol is consulted by a specific operation:

```
   for...of, spread [...x]   ──▶ obj[Symbol.iterator]
   for await...of            ──▶ obj[Symbol.asyncIterator]
   +, `${ }`, ==, String()   ──▶ obj[Symbol.toPrimitive]
   x instanceof Y            ──▶ Y[Symbol.hasInstance]
   Object.prototype.toString ──▶ obj[Symbol.toStringTag]
```

- **`Symbol.iterator`** — a function returning an iterator (an object with a `next()` method); enables `for...of`, spread, and destructuring.
- **`Symbol.asyncIterator`** — same idea for `for await...of`, where each `next()` returns a promise.
- **`Symbol.toPrimitive`** — a function `(hint)` the engine calls when it needs a primitive; `hint` is `"number"`, `"string"`, or `"default"`.
- **`Symbol.hasInstance`** — a function on a *class/constructor* that `instanceof` calls to decide membership.
- **`Symbol.toStringTag`** — a string that customizes the `"[object Tag]"` label from `Object.prototype.toString.call(obj)`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Well-known symbols are the **standardized slots on the back of a home appliance** — the HDMI port, the power socket, the headphone jack. The TV (the language) doesn't care who made your soundbar; it just looks for the labeled jack. Implement `Symbol.iterator` and you've plugged a cable into the "loop me" jack — now `for...of` works with your object, no special-casing required.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const money = {
  amount: 5,
  // Called when JS needs a primitive; behavior depends on the hint:
  [Symbol.toPrimitive](hint) {
    if (hint === 'string') return `$${this.amount}`;
    if (hint === 'number') return this.amount;
    return `Money(${this.amount})`;        // hint === 'default'
  },
  // Customize the Object.prototype.toString tag:
  [Symbol.toStringTag]: 'Money',
};

console.log(`${money}`);          // "$5"        (string hint)
console.log(money * 2);           // 10          (number hint)
console.log(money + '!');         // "Money(5)!" (default hint)
console.log(Object.prototype.toString.call(money));
                                  // "[object Money]"

// Make a custom object iterable with Symbol.iterator:
const range = {
  from: 1, to: 3,
  [Symbol.iterator]() {
    let current = this.from;
    const last = this.to;
    return {
      next() {
        return current <= last
          ? { value: current++, done: false }
          : { value: undefined, done: true };
      },
    };
  },
};

console.log([...range]); // [1, 2, 3]
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `Symbol.toPrimitive` do, and what are the three hints?**
A: It's a method the engine calls whenever it must convert an object to a primitive. It receives a `hint`: `"string"` (template literals, string contexts), `"number"` (arithmetic, comparisons like `<`), or `"default"` (the `+` operator and `==`, where the engine doesn't favor either type). Defining `[Symbol.toPrimitive](hint)` gives you one precise place to control all coercion, overriding the older `valueOf`/`toString` fallback pair.

---

### 🧊 Property Descriptors, Sealing & Freezing — Locking Objects Down

#### 🧩 What is it?
Every property of an object carries hidden metadata called a **descriptor**: `value`, plus three flags — **`writable`** (can the value change?), **`enumerable`** (does it show up in `for...in` and `Object.keys`?), and **`configurable`** (can the property be deleted or its flags changed?). `Object.defineProperty` sets these directly. `Object.seal` and `Object.freeze` flip many of them at once to make an object more rigid.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Sometimes you need guarantees: a constant that *can't* be overwritten, a hidden helper that *won't* appear in loops, or a fully **immutable** config object that no code can mutate by accident. Descriptors give per-property control; `freeze`/`seal` give whole-object lockdown. The catch — and a favorite interview trap — is that `Object.freeze` is **shallow**: it freezes the top level only.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Three levels of lockdown, from loosest to tightest:

```
   preventExtensions ─ can't ADD new props (existing ones editable)
          │
   seal ────────────── above + can't DELETE; all props non-configurable
          │
   freeze ──────────── above + can't CHANGE values; all props read-only
```

- **`Object.preventExtensions(obj)`** — no new properties; existing ones stay editable.
- **`Object.seal(obj)`** — also marks every property `configurable: false`, so you can't delete them or reconfigure them; values can still change.
- **`Object.freeze(obj)`** — also marks every property `writable: false`, so values are locked too. The object is now immutable... **but only one level deep**. A nested object stored inside a frozen object is *not* frozen and can still be mutated.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of an object as a **filing cabinet**. `preventExtensions` welds the cabinet shut so no one can add new drawers, but the existing drawers still open and their papers can be swapped. `seal` also locks the drawers in place — you can't remove a drawer, though you can still edit the paper inside. `freeze` super-glues every paper down too. The shallow catch: if one drawer holds *another, smaller box*, freezing the cabinet doesn't freeze that inner box — its contents are still fair game.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Descriptor control: a constant, non-enumerable property
const config = {};
Object.defineProperty(config, 'VERSION', {
  value: '1.0',
  writable: false,     // can't reassign
  enumerable: false,   // hidden from Object.keys / for...in
  configurable: false, // can't delete or redefine
});

config.VERSION = '2.0';          // silently ignored (throws in strict mode)
console.log(config.VERSION);     // "1.0"
console.log(Object.keys(config)); // []  (non-enumerable)

// Freeze is SHALLOW — the classic gotcha:
const settings = Object.freeze({
  theme: 'dark',
  nested: { fontSize: 12 },
});

settings.theme = 'light';        // ignored — top level is frozen
console.log(settings.theme);     // "dark"

settings.nested.fontSize = 20;   // ❌ NOT frozen — this succeeds!
console.log(settings.nested.fontSize); // 20

console.log(Object.isFrozen(settings));        // true
console.log(Object.isFrozen(settings.nested)); // false
```
> **Note (simplification):** to make an object *deeply* immutable you must recursively `Object.freeze` every nested object yourself (a "deep freeze"), since the built-in only touches the top level.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `Object.seal` and `Object.freeze`, and what does "shallow" mean here?**
A: `seal` stops you adding or deleting properties (everything becomes non-configurable) but you can still *change* existing values. `freeze` does all that **and** makes every property read-only, so the object's own values can't change either. "Shallow" means `freeze` only locks the object's *direct* properties — any object nested inside remains mutable. For true immutability you recursively freeze every level (a deep freeze).

---

### ⚠️ Proxy Risks — Overhead and the Invariants You Must Keep

#### 🧩 What is it?
Because a `Proxy` reroutes every operation through your handler functions, it carries two kinds of cost: a **performance** cost (each access is now a function call the engine can't optimize as aggressively) and a **correctness** cost — the spec enforces **invariants** (unbreakable rules) that your traps must respect, or they throw a `TypeError`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
A proxy is invisible at the call site — `p.x` looks like an ordinary read — so it's easy to wrap a hot, frequently-accessed object and quietly make it slow. And traps aren't allowed to *lie*: if the underlying target has a property that's non-configurable and non-writable, a `get` trap that returns a *different* value for it is a contradiction the engine refuses, throwing instead of returning your fake value. Knowing the rules keeps your proxy from blowing up in surprising places.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
**Overhead:** a normal property read on a stable-shaped object is something the engine optimizes heavily (see Module 1's inline caches). A proxied read instead calls your `get` trap every single time — far more work, and harder for the JIT to specialize. Fine for config objects or framework boundaries; risky inside a tight loop.

**Invariants:** the engine cross-checks your trap's answer against the real target. A few you must honor:

```
   target prop is non-configurable + non-writable
        │  get trap returns a DIFFERENT value
        ▼
   TypeError — the trap "lied" about an invariant

   target is non-extensible
        │  has trap hides an OWN property, or
        │  ownKeys omits a real one
        ▼
   TypeError
```

In short: a trap may add behavior, but it can't contradict facts the target guarantees about its non-configurable properties or its extensibility.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
The assistant from earlier can screen calls and take notes — but they **can't forge the boss's signature**. If the boss has signed a binding contract (a non-configurable, non-writable property), the assistant who tells a visitor "actually it says something different" is committing fraud, and the system (the engine) catches it and shuts the whole interaction down. The assistant also slows every visit slightly, because *everyone* now stops at their desk first instead of walking straight in.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// The invariant trap in action:
const target = {};
Object.defineProperty(target, 'locked', {
  value: 42,
  writable: false,
  configurable: false,
});

const p = new Proxy(target, {
  get(obj, key) {
    if (key === 'locked') return 99; // try to report a fake value
    return obj[key];
  },
});

try {
  console.log(p.locked);
} catch (e) {
  console.log(e.constructor.name); // "TypeError"
  // The engine refuses: a non-configurable, non-writable property
  // must report its real value (42), so returning 99 throws.
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What are the downsides of wrapping an object in a `Proxy`?**
A: Two big ones. Performance — every operation becomes a trap function call, which is slower and harder for the engine to optimize, so proxies are a poor fit for hot paths. And invariants — the spec forbids traps from contradicting the target's guarantees (e.g. reporting a different value for a non-configurable, non-writable property, or hiding own properties of a non-extensible object), and violating them throws a `TypeError`. Plus the general readability cost: a proxied object behaves unlike a normal one at a glance, which can surprise maintainers.

---

### 🔭 Putting It Together — A Reactive Validating Object

#### 🧩 What is it?
A small but complete example combining the module's ideas: a `Proxy` that (1) **validates** writes through a `set` trap, (2) **notifies** a subscriber whenever data changes (a tiny "reactive" object, the core trick behind frameworks like Vue), and (3) forwards defaults correctly with `Reflect`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
This is the canonical interview ask: *"build a reactive/observable object"* and *"validate writes with traps."* Both reduce to one idea — intercept `set`, run a rule, then run a side effect — and `Reflect` keeps the underlying write correct.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
   user code: state.age = 30
        │
        ▼
   set trap ── validate(age, 30) ── invalid? throw
        │ valid
        ▼
   Reflect.set(target, 'age', 30)   ← real write
        │
        ▼
   notify('age', 30)  ← side effect (re-render / log)
```

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
function reactive(target, { validate, onChange }) {
  return new Proxy(target, {
    set(obj, key, value, receiver) {
      const error = validate?.(key, value);   // optional rule check
      if (error) throw new TypeError(error);

      const ok = Reflect.set(obj, key, value, receiver); // real write
      if (ok) onChange?.(String(key), value);            // notify
      return ok;
    },
  });
}

const state = reactive(
  { name: 'Ada', age: 36 },
  {
    validate(key, value) {
      if (key === 'age' && (typeof value !== 'number' || value < 0)) {
        return 'age must be a non-negative number';
      }
      return null; // null = valid
    },
    onChange(key, value) {
      console.log(`changed: ${key} -> ${value}`);
    },
  },
);

state.name = 'Grace';  // logs: changed: name -> Grace
state.age = 40;        // logs: changed: age -> 40

try {
  state.age = -5;      // validation fails
} catch (e) {
  console.log(e.message); // "age must be a non-negative number"
}
console.log(state.age);   // 40  (the bad write never landed)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's a **bank teller for your data**. Every deposit (write) goes through the teller: they reject an invalid amount (validation), record the valid one in the ledger (`Reflect.set`), and then ring a bell so the manager updates the display board (the `onChange` notification). The account itself (the target) is only touched through this one trustworthy gate.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How would you build a reactive object that re-runs code whenever a property changes?**
A: Wrap the data in a `Proxy` with a `set` trap. Inside the trap, optionally validate the new value, perform the real write with `Reflect.set` (so the `receiver` is preserved and you get back a success boolean), and if it succeeded, invoke a callback / re-render — that callback is the "reaction." Real frameworks extend this by tracking *which* code read *which* key in the `get` trap, so only the affected reactions re-run, but the `set`-trap-plus-notify core is exactly this.

---

<h2 id="module-11">Module 11 — Modules & The Loading Pipeline</h2>

For years, JavaScript had no built-in way to split code across files — everything shared one giant global space, like a town where every person shouts their name into the same room. This module traces how we got from that chaos to two competing module systems (CommonJS and ES Modules), how each loads and links your code, and why understanding the difference is the line between "it works on my machine" and a 2 a.m. interop bug.

---

### 🏚️ The Global-Namespace Problem & the IIFE — life before modules

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **module** is just a file whose variables and functions stay *private* unless it deliberately shares them. Before JavaScript had modules, every `<script>` you loaded dumped *all* of its top-level variables into one shared bucket called the **global object** (`window` in the browser). The **IIFE** — *Immediately Invoked Function Expression*, a function you define and call in the same breath — was the hand-rolled trick people used to get privacy back.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
With everything global, two scripts could accidentally use the same variable name and silently clobber each other. Load `jquery.js` and `cart.js`, and if both define a top-level `total`, the second one wins — overwriting the first. This is **global-namespace pollution**: a shared room where every name collides. There was no privacy, no clear dependencies, and load order became a fragile game of Jenga.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A function in JavaScript creates its own private **scope** — names inside it are invisible outside. An IIFE wraps your whole file in a function and runs it instantly, so your variables live in that private scope instead of the global one. You then *return* (or attach) only the few things you want to expose.

```
  BEFORE (everything global = collisions)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GLOBAL  total, user, helper, init  │ ← all scripts share this
  └────────────────────────────────────┘
        a.js and b.js both write `total` → clash

  AFTER (IIFE = private box, 1 thing exposed)
  ┌──────────────┐   exposes only
  │ (function(){ │   ┌─────────┐
  │   total=... }│──▶│ Cart {} │ ← on global
  │  )()         │   └─────────┘
  └──────────────┘
   total stays hidden inside the box
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Imagine an office where everyone writes notes on **one shared whiteboard**. Two people both write "Budget: 500" in different spots and overwrite each other — chaos. An IIFE is like giving each team its **own private room**: they do their messy work inside, then walk out and pin a single clean summary on the shared board. Only the summary is public.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// The IIFE / "revealing module" pattern (pre-2015 privacy)
var Cart = (function () {
  // these are PRIVATE — invisible outside the function
  var items = [];
  function total() {
    return items.reduce((sum, p) => sum + p.price, 0);
  }
  function add(p) { items.push(p); }

  // expose ONLY what callers need
  return { add: add, total: total };
})();   // the () at the end runs it immediately

Cart.add({ price: 10 });
Cart.add({ price: 5 });
console.log(Cart.total()); // 15
console.log(Cart.items);   // undefined  ← `items` stayed private
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What problem did the IIFE / module pattern solve before real modules existed?**
A: It solved **global-namespace pollution**. Every script's top-level `var` landed on the global object, so independent files could collide on the same name and overwrite each other. An IIFE wraps a file in a function so its variables get a *private* scope, exposing only an explicit object of public members — giving you encapsulation and a clear public API without language-level module support.

---

### 📦 CommonJS — `require`, caching, and `module.exports`

#### 🧩 What is it?
**CommonJS (CJS)** is the module system **Node.js** was built on. You pull in other files with the `require()` function — *"give me what this file exports"* — and you publish your own file's public surface by assigning to `module.exports`. It predates JavaScript's official module syntax and is still everywhere in the Node ecosystem.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The IIFE pattern gave privacy but no real *dependency* system — you still loaded files by hand in the right order. CommonJS made dependencies **explicit and programmatic**: a file simply says `require('./cart')`, and Node figures out where that file is, runs it, and hands back its exports. No global namespace, no manual ordering.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`require()` is **synchronous** — *"do it now, block until finished."* When you `require` a file the first time, Node:
1. Resolves the path, reads the file, and wraps it in a function that injects `module`, `exports`, `require`, `__dirname`, and `__filename`.
2. Runs that file top to bottom.
3. Caches the resulting `module.exports` object keyed by the resolved file path.

Every later `require` of the *same* path returns that **cached** object — the file's code runs **only once**, no matter how many times it's required.

```
  require('./cart')  ── first time ──┐
        │                            ▼
        │                  run cart.js top→bottom
        │                            │
        │                  store module.exports
        │                  in the require CACHE
        ▼                            │
  require('./cart')  ── again ───────┘
        │
        ▼  returns the SAME cached object
   (cart.js body does NOT run a 2nd time)
```

> **Note (simplification):** `exports` starts out as just an alias — `module.exports` and `exports` point at the **same** object. So `exports.foo = 1` works. But `exports = {...}` only re-points the *local* `exports` variable; it does **not** change `module.exports`, so nothing gets exported. The rule: to replace the *whole* export, always assign to `module.exports`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`require` is like ordering a coffee at a shop with a **standing order book**. The first time you order a "Cart latte," the barista actually makes it (runs the file). They write the recipe-result in the order book (the cache). Next time anyone asks for the same drink, they just hand over the *already-made* one from behind the counter — they don't brew it again.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// cart.js
let calls = 0;
console.log('cart.js running, call #', ++calls);

module.exports.total = () => 15;
// exports.total = ... would ALSO work (same object).
// But: exports = { total } would NOT export — it re-points
// the local alias only. Use module.exports to fully replace.

// app.js
const a = require('./cart'); // logs: "cart.js running, call # 1"
const b = require('./cart'); // logs NOTHING — served from cache
console.log(a === b);        // true  ← same cached exports object
console.log(a.total());      // 15
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `module.exports` and `exports`, and why does `require` only run a file once?**
A: `exports` is simply a **local alias** pointing at the same object as `module.exports` initially, so adding properties to either works. But reassigning `exports = {...}` breaks the link and exports nothing — you must assign to `module.exports` to replace the whole export. `require` runs a file only once because Node **caches** `module.exports` by resolved path after first execution; subsequent requires return the cached object, which is also how shared singleton state works in CJS.

---

### 🔁 Circular Dependencies — when A needs B and B needs A

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **circular dependency** is when two modules require each other: `a.js` requires `b.js`, and `b.js` requires `a.js`. This creates a loop. Both CommonJS and ES Modules handle it — but in *different* ways that matter when you debug.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this?
Large codebases drift into cycles by accident (helpers cross-importing each other). Whether your code crashes, returns `undefined`, or works fine depends entirely on *which module system you're in* and *when* you read the value. Getting this wrong produces bugs that look impossible.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
**In CommonJS:** when the cycle is hit, Node returns the **partially-filled exports so far** — whatever the in-progress module had exported *up to that line*. The file is mid-execution, so anything assigned *after* the `require` point isn't there yet. You can get an incomplete object.

**In ES Modules:** because imports are resolved and **hoisted** before any code runs, the binding *exists* even mid-cycle. Reading it during initialization may give `undefined` (Temporal Dead Zone style) if the source hasn't run yet, but the *connection* is wired up — so by the time you actually *use* it (e.g. inside a function called later), it reads the final value via the **live binding** (next topic).

```
  CommonJS cycle:  b gets a PARTIAL copy of a's exports
  ┌─────┐ require ┌─────┐ require(a) ┌──────────────┐
  │a.js │────────▶│b.js │───────────▶│ a so far:    │
  │run..│         │     │            │ {half:done}  │ ← snapshot!
  └─────┘         └─────┘            └──────────────┘
   a finishes AFTER b — b never sees the rest.
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Two chefs co-write a recipe and each needs the other's section. **CommonJS** is like photocopying your colleague's page *right now* — you get whatever they've scribbled so far, even if it's half-blank, and your copy never updates. **ES Modules** is like writing down *where their page lives* — a reference. Even if it's blank when you note it down, by the time you actually read it during cooking, their finished text is there.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// ---- CommonJS ----
// a.js
exports.loaded = false;
const b = require('./b');     // b runs now, mid-way through a
console.log('a sees b.value:', b.value); // 'b done'
exports.loaded = true;        // assigned AFTER b grabbed its copy

// b.js
const a = require('./a');     // a is only PARTIALLY done here
console.log('b sees a.loaded:', a.loaded); // false  ← snapshot!
exports.value = 'b done';

// Run `node a.js` →
//   b sees a.loaded: false   (a.loaded was still false at copy time)
//   a sees b.value: b done
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do circular imports resolve differently in CommonJS vs ES Modules?**
A: In **CommonJS**, `require` returns the **partially-completed** `module.exports` at the moment the cycle is entered — so the second module may see an *incomplete snapshot* of the first, and that snapshot never updates. In **ES Modules**, all imports are hoisted and bindings are created before execution, so the binding always *exists*; reading it too early yields `undefined` (or a TDZ error for `const`), but because ESM imports are **live bindings**, code that runs *after* initialization sees the final, correct value. ESM cycles are more predictable; CJS gives stale partial copies.

---

### 🔗 ES Modules — static structure & live bindings

#### 🧩 What is it?
**ES Modules (ESM)** are JavaScript's *official*, language-level module system (standardized in ES2015), using `import` and `export`. Unlike CommonJS's function call, `import`/`export` are **static syntax** the engine reads *before* running your code — and the things you import are **live bindings**, not copies.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
CommonJS resolves dependencies *while running* (`require` is a function call you can put anywhere). That makes it impossible to know a file's true dependencies without executing it — which blocks safe **tree-shaking** (dropping unused code) and makes the value you imported a frozen *copy*. ESM's static, declarative structure fixes both: tools can analyze imports without running anything, and bindings stay *connected* to their source.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Key ESM behaviors a beginner must know:
- **Static structure:** `import`/`export` must sit at the top level — you can't conditionally `import` inside an `if`. This lets the engine build the full **module graph** before execution.
- **Import hoisting:** all `import` statements are processed first, so imported names exist before any line runs.
- **Live bindings:** an imported variable is a *read-only window* into the exporter's variable. If the exporter changes it later, **you see the new value** — it's not a one-time copy (this is the big difference from CJS).
- **Strict mode by default:** every ESM file runs in strict mode automatically — no accidental globals, `this` at top level is `undefined`.
- **Top-level `await`:** you can `await` directly at a module's top level; the engine waits for it before any importer's code runs.

```
  LIVE BINDING (ESM): import is a window, not a copy
  counter.js                  app.js
  ┌───────────────┐           ┌──────────────────┐
  │ let count = 0 │◀──window──│ import {count}    │
  │ inc(){count++}│           │ count reads LIVE  │
  └───────────────┘           └──────────────────┘
   call inc() → app's `count` now reads the new value
   (CommonJS would have copied 0 and stayed 0)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A CommonJS import is a **printed photo** of a whiteboard — accurate at the moment you took it, but it never changes when someone edits the board. An ESM live binding is a **live video feed** of that same whiteboard: you don't own the marker (it's read-only to you), but you always see the *current* state. Edit the board and every viewer's feed updates.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// counter.mjs
export let count = 0;
export function inc() { count++; }

// app.mjs
import { count, inc } from './counter.mjs';
console.log(count); // 0
inc();
console.log(count); // 1  ← LIVE binding: we see the updated value
// (With CommonJS, `count` would be a copy and still print 0.)

// You CANNOT reassign an imported binding — it's read-only:
// count = 99; // ❌ TypeError: Assignment to constant variable
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is a "live binding" and what makes ES Modules tree-shakeable?**
A: A **live binding** means an imported name is a *read-only reference* into the exporting module's variable, not a copied value — so if the exporter mutates it, importers see the updated value. ESM is **tree-shakeable** because `import`/`export` are *static*: their structure is fixed and analyzable *without running the code*, so a bundler can build the full module graph, see exactly which exports are actually used, and safely delete the unused ones. CommonJS's `require` is a runtime function call that could appear anywhere or be dynamic, so the bundler can't safely prove an export is unused.

---

### ⚡ ESM ↔ CJS Interop & Dynamic `import()` — mixing systems and code-splitting

#### 🧩 What is it?
Real Node projects mix both systems, so you must know how they **interoperate**. And **dynamic `import()`** is a *function-like* form of import — `import('./x.js')` — that loads a module **on demand** at runtime and returns a Promise, unlike the static `import` statement.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The ecosystem is mid-migration: some packages are ESM, some are CJS, and they must talk to each other — which creates real footguns. Separately, static `import` loads *everything up front*; for a large app you often want to **defer** loading a heavy feature until it's actually needed. Dynamic `import()` enables that, which is the foundation of **code-splitting** (shipping code in smaller on-demand chunks).

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
**Interop pitfalls in Node:**
- ESM **can** import CJS: `import pkg from './old.cjs'` — the CJS `module.exports` becomes the **default** export. Named imports from CJS often work via Node's static analysis but aren't guaranteed.
- CJS **cannot** `require()` an ESM file *synchronously* (ESM may use top-level `await`, which is async). To load ESM from CJS, use dynamic `import()`, which returns a Promise.
- `__dirname`/`__filename` don't exist in ESM (use `import.meta.url`); `require` isn't defined in ESM by default.

**Dynamic `import()`:** it's allowed *anywhere* (inside an `if`, a click handler, a function), returns a **Promise** that resolves to the module's namespace object, and lets bundlers split that module into a separate chunk fetched only when the import actually runs. It still works with tree-shaking because the imported module's *own* exports remain statically analyzable.

```
  CODE-SPLITTING via dynamic import()
  main bundle (loads instantly)
  ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ button.onclick = async ()│
  │   => { const m =         │
  │     await import('./chart')  ──┐ fetched
  │   }                      │     │ ONLY on
  └──────────────────────────┘     ▼ click
                          ┌──────────────────┐
                          │ chart.js chunk   │ (separate file)
                          └──────────────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Static `import` is **packing every tool you *might* need into your backpack before leaving home** — heavy, but everything's ready. Dynamic `import()` is **ordering a specialized tool to be delivered the moment you discover you need it** — your bag stays light, and the screwdriver only ships if you actually open that drawer. Code-splitting is the delivery service that keeps each shipment small.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Dynamic import: load a heavy module ONLY when needed.
async function showChart() {
  // returns a Promise → resolves to the module namespace object
  const chart = await import('./heavy-chart.mjs');
  chart.render();
}
// './heavy-chart.mjs' is NOT downloaded/run until showChart() is called.

// Loading an ESM file from a CommonJS file (the only way):
// (inside a .cjs file)
async function load() {
  const esm = await import('./modern.mjs'); // require() would throw
  esm.doThing();
}

// In an .mjs file, __dirname doesn't exist — use import.meta:
// console.log(import.meta.url); // 'file:///.../app.mjs'
```

> **Note (simplification):** Node's CJS↔ESM interop has many edge cases (named-export detection, the `"exports"` field, dual packages). The reliable mental model: **ESM can statically import CJS (as a default); CJS must use async dynamic `import()` to reach ESM.**

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why can't CommonJS `require()` an ES Module, and how does dynamic `import()` enable code-splitting?**
A: `require()` is **synchronous** — it must return the exports immediately. ES Modules can use **top-level `await`** and are evaluated asynchronously, so they can't be loaded synchronously; that's why CJS must use the **async** dynamic `import()` (which returns a Promise) to load ESM. Dynamic `import()` enables **code-splitting** because it's a *runtime* load: the bundler can carve that module into a **separate chunk** that's fetched and executed only when the `import()` call actually runs, keeping the initial bundle small while still letting each split module be statically tree-shaken internally.

---

<h2 id="module-12">Module 12 — Performance Engineering</h2>

Fast code is not the goal — *fast enough, where it matters* is. The skill here is not "make everything quick" but knowing **what** to measure, **where** the time actually goes, and **which** of a handful of well-worn tricks (debounce, throttle, batching, workers, code-splitting) fixes the jank you found. Guess, and you'll optimize the 1% that didn't matter; measure first, and the fix is usually obvious.

---

### 📏 Measure Before You Optimize — Don't Guess, Profile

#### 🧩 What is it?
Before changing a single line for speed, you **measure** where time is spent. The three core tools: the DevTools **Performance panel** (a visual timeline of everything the page did), `performance.now()` (a precise stopwatch), and `PerformanceObserver` (a subscriber that gets told when slow things happen).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Human intuition about performance is famously wrong. You'll *swear* a loop is the bottleneck, spend a day rewriting it, and discover the real cost was a layout being recomputed 1,000 times. Optimizing without measuring is like prescribing surgery without an X-ray — you might fix something, but probably not the thing that hurts.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **`performance.now()`** returns a high-resolution timestamp in **milliseconds** (with a fractional part), measured from when the page loaded. Subtract two readings to time a block.
- **DevTools Performance panel** records a **flame chart** — a stack of bars where width is time and depth is the call stack. Wide bars at the top are where time goes.
- **`PerformanceObserver`** lets you *subscribe* to performance entries (long tasks, paints, navigation) instead of polling. The browser pushes them to your callback.

```
   flame chart (width = time, vertical = call depth)
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ handleClick                                 │  ← top frame
   │   ┌──────────────┐ ┌───────────────────────┐│
   │   │ parseData    │ │ render (WIDE = slow!) ││  ← the real cost
   │   └──────────────┘ └───────────────────────┘│
   └────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          narrow = cheap        wide = optimize here
```

> **Note (simplification):** `performance.now()` precision is deliberately **clamped** by browsers (often to ~0.1ms, sometimes coarser) as a defense against timing-based attacks. It's precise enough for app profiling, not for nanosecond benchmarks.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A doctor doesn't amputate based on a hunch — they order an X-ray first. The Performance panel is your X-ray: it shows the *broken bone* (the slow function) instead of you treating a bruise that was never the problem.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// 1. performance.now() — a precise stopwatch
const start = performance.now();
doExpensiveWork();
const end = performance.now();
console.log(`took ${(end - start).toFixed(2)} ms`);
// e.g. "took 12.40 ms"

// 2. PerformanceObserver — get notified about slow "long tasks"
//    (a "long task" = the main thread was busy > 50ms straight)
const obs = new PerformanceObserver((list) => {
  for (const entry of list.getEntries()) {
    console.log(`long task: ${entry.duration.toFixed(0)}ms`);
  }
});
obs.observe({ entryTypes: ['longtask'] }); // browser pushes entries to us
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How would you find and fix jank ("jank" = visible stutter / dropped frames) in a page?**
A: **Measure first.** Record in the DevTools Performance panel while reproducing the stutter, then read the flame chart for wide bars and look for red "long task" markers and dropped frames. Identify whether the cost is **scripting** (JS), **layout/style** (reflow), or **paint**. Only then apply the matching fix — debounce/throttle a noisy handler, batch DOM reads/writes, move CPU work to a Worker, or split a big bundle. The headline is: *profile, find the real bottleneck, fix that one thing, re-measure.*

---

### ⏱️ The Frame Budget — Why 16.6ms Is the Magic Number

#### 🧩 What is it?
Most screens refresh **60 times per second** (60fps — "frames per second"). To keep up, the browser must finish *all* its work for one frame — running your JS, recalculating styles, layout, and painting — within **1000ms / 60 ≈ 16.6ms**. That window is your **frame budget**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
If your JavaScript hogs the **main thread** (the single thread where your code, layout, and painting all run) for longer than ~16.6ms, the browser **misses** the frame. The screen shows the *old* frame again — the animation stutters, scrolling feels sticky, a button looks frozen. That visible stutter is **jank**, and it almost always comes from one slow chunk of synchronous work.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Every frame, the browser tries to run this pipeline within the budget. Your JS runs *first*, so a long script eats into the time left for layout and paint:

```
   one frame = ~16.6ms budget (at 60fps)
   ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │  your JS   │ style │ layout │ paint │ slack   │   ✅ fits
   ├────────────┴───────┴────────┴───────┴─────────┤
   │  your JS (way too long!)         │ X X X      │   ❌ frame MISSED
   ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
                  main thread is blocked → jank
```

A "**long task**" is the formal name for any chunk that holds the main thread for **more than 50ms** straight — long enough that input (a tap, a click) can't be handled, so the page feels unresponsive.

> **Note (simplification):** Not every device is 60Hz — many phones and laptops now run 90Hz or 120Hz, shrinking the budget to ~11ms or ~8.3ms. The principle is identical; the number just gets stricter.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a chef plating dishes for a **conveyor belt** that moves every 16.6ms. Each slot must be filled in time. If the chef spends too long garnishing one plate, the belt moves on **empty** — diners see a gap. Your slow function is the over-garnished plate, and the empty slot is the dropped frame.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// A handler that BLOWS the budget → janky scroll
window.addEventListener('scroll', () => {
  // Suppose this synchronous loop takes 40ms.
  // 40ms > 16.6ms, so we miss ~2 frames every scroll event. JANK.
  heavyLayoutWork();
});

// FIX preview: break long work into chunks that yield to the browser,
// so each chunk fits the budget and the page stays responsive.
function processInChunks(items, doOne) {
  let i = 0;
  function chunk() {
    const deadline = performance.now() + 8; // use ~half a frame
    while (i < items.length && performance.now() < deadline) {
      doOne(items[i++]);
    }
    if (i < items.length) {
      // yield the thread so queued work can run, then continue.
      // (For a guaranteed paint between chunks, use requestAnimationFrame.)
      setTimeout(chunk, 0);
    }
  }
  chunk();
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is the "60fps frame budget" and why does it matter?**
A: At 60fps the browser has ~**16.6ms** per frame to run JS, compute style + layout, and paint. Because all of that shares the **single main thread**, any synchronous JS that runs longer than the budget delays the frame — the browser repeats the old frame and the user sees stutter (jank). The fix is to keep main-thread work per frame small: break long tasks into chunks, defer non-urgent work, or move heavy computation off the main thread entirely.

---

### 🪃 Debounce — Wait Until the User Stops

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Debounce** wraps a function so it only runs **after** events stop firing for a quiet period. Rapid-fire calls are collapsed into **one** call at the end of the burst. Classic use: a search box that should query the server only once the user *stops typing*.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Some events fire absurdly often — `keyup` on every keystroke, `resize` dozens of times per drag. If each one triggers an expensive action (a network request, a re-render), you flood the system with wasted work, most of which is instantly stale. Debounce throws away all but the **last** call.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Each call **resets a timer**. Only when the timer is allowed to finish — i.e. no new call arrived during the `delay` — does the real function run. A new call during the wait **cancels** the pending one.

```
   calls:   X X X X            X
   time ───────────────────────────────▶
            └ each X resets the timer ┘
                          ▲                  ▲
                  delay elapses,      fires again after
                  FIRES once          the next quiet gap
   → many calls collapse into one, after the burst ends
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
An **elevator** with a "door open" button: every time someone presses it, the close-timer restarts. The doors only close once people **stop** pressing for a few seconds. Ten presses in a row = doors close *once*, after the last press — not ten times.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// debounce: run `fn` only after `delay` ms of SILENCE.
function debounce(fn, delay) {
  let timer = null;                 // holds the pending timeout id
  return function (...args) {
    clearTimeout(timer);            // a new call cancels the old timer
    timer = setTimeout(() => {
      fn.apply(this, args);         // preserve `this` + pass latest args
    }, delay);
  };
}

// Usage: only search after the user pauses for 300ms
const search = debounce((q) => console.log('searching:', q), 300);
search('h');     // timer set
search('he');    // previous cancelled, timer reset
search('hel');   // previous cancelled, timer reset
// ...300ms of no typing later → logs ONCE: "searching: hel"
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Implement `debounce`, and say what happens to the intermediate calls.**
A: (See the code above.) Each invocation `clearTimeout`s the previous pending timer and schedules a fresh one, so the wrapped function fires **only after `delay` ms with no new calls**. All intermediate calls are discarded; only the **last** one's arguments survive. Using `fn.apply(this, args)` preserves the caller's `this` and forwards the most recent arguments. A common follow-up is a `leading` (fire on the first call) or `cancel()` option.

---

### 🚿 Throttle — At Most Once Every N Milliseconds

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Throttle** wraps a function so it runs **at most once per interval**, no matter how many times it's called. Where debounce waits for *silence*, throttle enforces a steady *rhythm* — perfect for things that should update *while* an event keeps firing (scroll position, drag tracking).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Debounce is wrong when you need **continuous** feedback. If you debounce a scroll handler, a "scroll-to-top" button never appears until the user *stops* scrolling — useless. Throttle instead lets the handler fire on a regular cadence (say every 200ms) *during* the activity, capping the cost without going silent.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The wrapper remembers the **timestamp of the last run**. A new call runs the function only if `interval` ms have passed since then; otherwise it's ignored. This guarantees an upper bound on how often `fn` runs.

```
   debounce vs throttle on the SAME burst of calls
   calls:   X X X X X X X X X X X X        (rapid, continuous)
   time ──────────────────────────────────▶
   throttle ✔ . . ✔ . . ✔ . . ✔ . . ✔     (steady, every N ms)
   debounce . . . . . . . . . . . . . ✔     (once, only at the end)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **turnstile** at a stadium lets one person through every few seconds and makes the rest wait, no matter how hard the crowd pushes. Throttle is that turnstile: a fixed maximum rate, regardless of how frantic the input is.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// throttle (leading-edge): run at most once per `interval` ms.
function throttle(fn, interval) {
  let last = 0;                       // timestamp of the last run (ms)
  return function (...args) {
    const now = Date.now();
    if (now - last >= interval) {     // enough time has passed?
      last = now;                     // record this run
      fn.apply(this, args);           // run with caller's `this` + args
    }
    // else: ignore this call entirely
  };
}

// Usage: log scroll position at most 5 times/sec (every 200ms)
const onScroll = throttle(() => console.log(window.scrollY), 200);
window.addEventListener('scroll', onScroll);
// during a fast scroll, fires ~ every 200ms, not on every pixel
```

> **Note (simplification):** This is the simplest **leading-edge** throttle: it fires immediately, then ignores calls until the interval passes. It does **not** guarantee a final **trailing** call, so the very last event in a burst can be dropped. Production helpers (e.g. lodash) add `{ leading, trailing }` options and a timer to capture that last call.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Debounce vs throttle — when do you use each?**
A: **Debounce** waits for activity to *stop*, then fires once — ideal for "the user finished" moments: search-as-you-type, validating a field after typing, saving after edits settle. **Throttle** fires at a steady capped rate *during* activity — ideal for "keep up while it's happening": scroll/resize handlers, drag tracking, infinite-scroll checks. Rule of thumb: need the **final** value after a burst → debounce; need **regular updates** throughout the burst → throttle.

---

### 🧱 Critical Rendering Path & Layout Thrashing — Stop Fighting the Browser

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **critical rendering path** is the sequence the browser follows to turn your DOM (the page's elements) into pixels: compute **styles** → **layout** (figure out the size and position of every box — also called *reflow*) → **paint** → **composite**. **Layout thrashing** is the anti-pattern of forcing that expensive *layout* step to run over and over inside one frame.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Layout is expensive. The browser is smart: it **batches** your DOM changes and recomputes layout *once*, lazily, when needed. But if you **read** a layout property (like `offsetHeight`) right after **writing** one, the browser is forced to compute layout *immediately* to give you a correct answer. Do that in a loop and you trigger a fresh layout on **every iteration** — that's thrashing, and it murders performance.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A write *invalidates* the layout (marks it "dirty"). A read of a layout value *forces* a recompute if the layout is dirty. Interleaving read-write-read-write forces a recompute per cycle. The fix: **batch all reads first, then all writes.**

```
   ❌ THRASHING (read-write-read-write)
   read h ─ write ─ read h ─ write ─ read h ─ write
      └ forces ┘    └ forces ┘    └ forces ┘   layout each time!

   ✅ BATCHED (all reads, then all writes)
   read read read │ write write write
   one layout ────┘ one layout flush at frame end
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Imagine asking a librarian for a book, then putting one back, then asking for another, then putting one back — each time making them **re-shelve and re-count the whole row**. Far better to ask for *all* the books you want at once, then return *all* of them at once. Batching reads and writes is handing the librarian one full list instead of pestering them per item.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const boxes = document.querySelectorAll('.box');

// ❌ THRASHING: each loop reads (offsetWidth) then writes (style),
//    forcing the browser to recompute layout every iteration.
boxes.forEach((box) => {
  const w = box.offsetWidth;        // READ  → forces layout (it's dirty)
  box.style.width = w + 10 + 'px';  // WRITE → dirties layout again
});

// ✅ BATCHED: read everything first, then write everything.
const widths = [];
boxes.forEach((box) => widths.push(box.offsetWidth)); // all READS
boxes.forEach((box, i) => {                           // all WRITES
  box.style.width = widths[i] + 10 + 'px';
});
// → layout is recomputed once, not once-per-box
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is layout thrashing and how do you avoid it?**
A: Layout thrashing is repeatedly forcing **synchronous layout (reflow)** by interleaving DOM **writes** (which dirty the layout) with **reads** of layout properties like `offsetWidth`, `getBoundingClientRect()`, or `scrollTop` (which force the browser to recompute immediately so it can return a correct value). In a loop this means one full layout per iteration. Fix it by **batching**: do all your reads first, then all your writes — so the browser only reflows once. Tools like `requestAnimationFrame` (and the `fastdom` library) help schedule reads and writes into separate phases.

---

### 🧵 Web Workers — Real Parallelism Off the Main Thread

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **Web Worker** is a separate background thread that runs JavaScript **in parallel** with the main thread. You hand it CPU-heavy work, it computes without freezing the UI, and it sends the result back. `SharedArrayBuffer` and `Atomics` let a worker and the main thread share *the same* block of memory safely.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
JavaScript on the main thread is **single-threaded** — your code, layout, and paint all share one lane. A heavy computation (parsing a huge file, image processing) blocks that lane and freezes everything, blowing way past the frame budget. A Worker moves that computation to a *different* lane, so the UI stays smooth.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The main thread and worker each have their **own** memory and communicate by **passing messages** (`postMessage`) — the data is *copied* across by default (the "structured clone"). For large data you can avoid the copy: **transfer** ownership of an `ArrayBuffer`, or share memory directly via `SharedArrayBuffer`. With shared memory, **`Atomics`** provides indivisible read-modify-write operations so the two threads don't corrupt each other's data in a race.

```
   MAIN THREAD                      WORKER THREAD
   ┌──────────────┐  postMessage    ┌──────────────┐
   │  UI, layout, │ ───(copy)─────▶ │  heavy CPU   │
   │  paint, JS   │ ◀──(result)──── │  work runs   │
   └──────────────┘                 └──────────────┘
        stays responsive                 runs in parallel

   SharedArrayBuffer: ONE memory block both can touch
   ┌──────────────┐                 ┌──────────────┐
   │ main ───▶ [ shared bytes ] ◀─── worker        │
   └──────────────┘  Atomics = safe concurrent R/W │
```

> **Note (simplification):** `SharedArrayBuffer` requires the page to be **cross-origin isolated** (specific `COOP`/`COEP` security headers) — it was restricted after the Spectre CPU vulnerability. Plain message-passing Workers need no such setup, which is why they're the common default.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
The main thread is a **cashier** who must keep serving customers. If they stop to count the entire day's inventory, the line freezes. A Web Worker is a **stockroom employee** counting inventory in the back — the cashier keeps the line moving and just gets a slip with the final count. `SharedArrayBuffer` is a **shared whiteboard** both can write on; `Atomics` is the rule that only one person writes a number at a time so it never smears.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// --- main.js (runs on the main thread) ---
const worker = new Worker('worker.js');
worker.postMessage({ numbers: bigArray });   // send work (copied across)
worker.onmessage = (e) => {
  console.log('worker result:', e.data);      // get result back
};
// the UI keeps responding while the worker computes

// --- worker.js (runs on its OWN thread) ---
self.onmessage = (e) => {
  const { numbers } = e.data;
  const total = numbers.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0); // heavy CPU work
  self.postMessage(total);                          // send result home
};

// --- Atomics on shared memory (sketch) ---
const sab = new SharedArrayBuffer(4);   // 4 bytes of shared memory
const view = new Int32Array(sab);
Atomics.add(view, 0, 1);                // safe, indivisible increment
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When do you move work off the main thread, and what's the cost?**
A: Move **CPU-bound** work (parsing, crypto, image/data processing, big computations) to a Web Worker whenever it would block the main thread long enough to cause jank — generally anything that can't fit in the frame budget.
