The cost is the **boundary**: messages are copied via structured clone by default, which is itself work for large payloads. Mitigate with **transferable** objects (transfer an `ArrayBuffer`'s ownership, zero-copy) or **`SharedArrayBuffer` + `Atomics`** for shared state. Don't worker-ize trivial work — the messaging overhead can cost more than you save.

---

### 📦 Code Splitting & Lazy Loading — Ship Less, Sooner

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Code splitting** breaks your one giant JavaScript bundle into smaller pieces. **Lazy loading** then downloads a piece **only when it's actually needed** — via dynamic `import()` — instead of forcing the user to download the whole app before they see anything.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A single big bundle means the browser must **download, parse, and execute** all your JS before the page is interactive — even code for routes the user may never visit. That delays the first meaningful paint and inflates the main-thread "parse + compile" cost. Splitting lets the initial load ship only what the first screen needs.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A static `import` at the top of a file is bundled into the **initial** download. A **dynamic** `import('./thing.js')` is a function that returns a **Promise** — the bundler sees it and splits that module into a **separate chunk** fetched on demand, at runtime, when that line executes.

```
   ❌ one big bundle                ✅ split + lazy
   ┌─────────────────────┐         ┌──────────┐  load now
   │ home + chat + admin │         │  home    │
   │ + charts (all now!) │         └──────────┘
   └─────────────────────┘         ┌──────────┐  load
        slow first paint           │  chat    │  on demand
                                    └──────────┘
                                    ┌──────────┐  load
                                    │  admin   │  on demand
                                    └──────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Streaming a movie vs. downloading the whole film before pressing play. Lazy loading **streams** — you get the opening scene instantly and the rest arrives as you go. Shipping one giant bundle is making everyone download the full 4K movie just to watch the trailer.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// ❌ static import: pulled into the INITIAL bundle, always downloaded
// import { Chart } from './heavy-chart.js';

// ✅ dynamic import: a SEPARATE chunk, fetched only when this runs
button.addEventListener('click', async () => {
  // download + run heavy-chart.js only the first time it's clicked
  const { Chart } = await import('./heavy-chart.js');
  new Chart(data).render();
});
// the chart code never burdens users who never click the button
```

> **Note (simplification):** Frameworks dress this up (`React.lazy` + `<Suspense>`, Next.js `dynamic()`, route-based splitting), but underneath it's the same primitive: a dynamic `import()` returning a Promise for a separately-bundled chunk.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is code splitting and how is it achieved in JavaScript?**
A: Code splitting divides a bundle into smaller chunks so the browser downloads only what each screen needs, shrinking the initial download, parse, and compile cost. It's achieved with **dynamic `import()`**, which returns a Promise and signals the bundler to emit a separate chunk loaded at runtime on demand. You typically split by **route** (load each page's code on navigation) and by **interaction** (load a heavy widget on first use). Pair it with **prefetch/preload** hints to fetch a likely-needed chunk during idle time so the lazy load feels instant.

---

<h2 id="module-13">Module 13 — Runtime Environments: Browser & Node Internals</h2>

Your JavaScript never runs in a vacuum — it runs *inside* a host: a **browser** that paints pixels on a screen, or **Node.js** that talks to files and network sockets. The language is the same, but the world around it is wildly different, and the gaps between those two worlds are where production bugs love to hide. This module opens up both hosts and shows you the machinery they wrap around V8.

---

### 🌳 The DOM as a Tree — How a Web Page Becomes Data

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **DOM** (Document Object Model) is the browser's live, in-memory representation of your page, shaped as a **tree** of nodes. Each HTML tag (`html`, `body`, a `div`, a `p`) becomes a node, and nesting tags inside tags becomes parent-and-child links. Your JavaScript reads and edits the page by walking and changing this tree — not by editing the raw HTML text.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
HTML arrives as a flat string of text. Text is awful to work with programmatically — there's no easy way to ask "find the third list item" or "add a button inside this card." A **tree** turns that flat text into a navigable structure with parents, children, and siblings, so code (and the browser itself) can find, change, add, and remove pieces precisely.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The browser parses your HTML top to bottom and builds one node per tag, linking each to its parent. The result is a tree with a single root (`document`).

```
   HTML                     DOM TREE
   <body>                   document
     <h1>Hi</h1>               │
     <ul>                   ┌─body─┐
       <li>A</li>           ▼      ▼
       <li>B</li>          h1     ul
     </ul>               "Hi"   ┌──┴──┐
   </body>                     li     li
                              "A"    "B"
```

Walking this tree (`parentNode`, `children`, `nextSibling`) is how every DOM API navigates. APIs like `document.querySelector('li')` search the tree for matching nodes.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a **company org chart**. The CEO is the root; each manager has reports beneath them. You'd never describe a company as one long paragraph — you'd draw the chart, because then you can point at any person and instantly see their boss, their team, and their peers. The DOM is the org chart of your page.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// The page: <ul id="list"><li>A</li><li>B</li></ul>
const list = document.getElementById('list');

console.log(list.children.length);     // 2   -> two <li> nodes
console.log(list.firstElementChild.textContent); // "A"

const li = document.createElement('li');
li.textContent = 'C';
list.appendChild(li);   // tree now has three <li> children -> A, B, C
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Is the DOM part of JavaScript?**
A: No. The DOM is a **host API** the browser exposes to JavaScript — it's defined by the WHATWG/W3C, not by ECMAScript. That's why `document` and `window` exist in a browser but not in Node. JavaScript the language only defines things like objects, arrays, and `Promise`; the DOM is something the *host* hands you.

---

### 🎨 Reflow vs Repaint — The Two Costs of Changing the Screen

#### 🧩 What is it?
When you change the page, the browser may do one of two kinds of work. **Reflow** (also called **layout**) recalculates the *geometry* — where every element sits and how big it is. **Repaint** fills in *pixels* — colors, text, shadows — without moving anything. Reflow is the expensive one; repaint is cheaper; and a reflow almost always forces a repaint afterward (you can't move something without redrawing it).

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
A surprising amount of UI "jank" (stutter) comes from triggering reflow too often. Changing a property like `width` or `top` makes the browser re-measure layout, and if you do that inside a loop over many elements, you can recalculate layout dozens of times in a single frame — freezing the page. Knowing which changes cause reflow versus a cheap repaint is the difference between smooth and sluggish.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **Reflow-triggering changes:** anything that affects size or position — `width`, `height`, `top`, `margin`, adding/removing nodes, or even *reading* a layout value like `offsetHeight` right after a write (it forces the browser to flush pending layout).
- **Repaint-only changes:** visual properties that don't move anything — `color`, `background-color`, `visibility`, `box-shadow`.
- **Neither (cheapest):** `transform` and `opacity` can often be handled by the GPU compositor, skipping both layout and paint.

```
  CHANGE TYPE        WORK THE BROWSER DOES        COST
  ───────────────    ─────────────────────────    ──────
  width / top        Layout -> Paint -> Composite  🐢 high
  add/remove node    Layout -> Paint -> Composite  🐢 high
  color / shadow     Paint -> Composite            🙂 mid
  transform/opacity  Composite only                ⚡ low
```

> **Note (simplification):** whether `transform`/`opacity` truly skip layout/paint depends on the element being on its own GPU layer; the browser decides. The mental model — *transform/opacity are the cheap ones* — is what matters for day-to-day code.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Imagine rearranging a room. **Reflow** is moving the furniture — once the couch moves, everything's spacing has to be re-measured, and the whole room must be re-photographed. **Repaint** is just repainting the wall a new color — nothing moves, you only re-photograph the changed wall. Moving furniture (reflow) is far more work than a coat of paint (repaint).

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const boxes = document.querySelectorAll('.box');

// ❌ LAYOUT THRASH: reads offsetHeight (forces reflow) right after each
//    write, inside the loop -> reflow happens once PER box.
boxes.forEach(box => {
  box.style.height = box.offsetHeight + 10 + 'px'; // write, then read -> reflow
});

// ✅ BATCH: read ALL first, then write ALL -> at most one reflow.
const heights = [...boxes].map(box => box.offsetHeight); // all reads
boxes.forEach((box, i) => {
  box.style.height = heights[i] + 10 + 'px';            // all writes
});
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What causes a reflow and how do you minimize it?**
A: A reflow is triggered by anything that changes geometry — size, position, adding/removing nodes — or by *reading* a layout property (like `offsetHeight`) while writes are pending, which forces the browser to flush layout early. Minimize it by **batching reads and writes** (read all measurements first, then apply all changes), animating with `transform`/`opacity` instead of `top`/`width`, and changing many elements via a single class toggle rather than per-element style writes in a loop.

---

### 🖼️ The Rendering Pipeline — From DOM to Pixels

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **rendering pipeline** is the fixed sequence of stages the browser runs to turn your DOM and CSS into pixels on screen. The classic stages are: **parse → build DOM + CSSOM → render tree → layout → paint → composite**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
If you don't know the stages, performance work is guesswork. Knowing the pipeline tells you *exactly* where a slowdown comes from: too much DOM to build, expensive CSS to match, layout recalculation, heavy painting, or too many GPU layers. It also explains why a change might be cheap (only the last stage runs) or expensive (it forces the pipeline to re-run from layout).

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
1. **Parse HTML** into the **DOM** tree (your elements).
2. **Parse CSS** into the **CSSOM** (CSS Object Model — the style rules as data).
3. Combine them into the **render tree** — only the nodes that will actually be displayed (skips things like `display: none`).
4. **Layout** — compute each box's exact size and position.
5. **Paint** — fill in pixels for text, colors, borders, shadows.
6. **Composite** — stack the painted layers in the right order and put them on screen (often using the GPU).

```
  HTML ─▶ DOM ┐
              ├─▶ RENDER TREE ─▶ LAYOUT ─▶ PAINT ─▶ COMPOSITE
  CSS  ─▶ CSSOM┘                  │          │         │
                              geometry    pixels    layers
                              (reflow)   (repaint)  to screen
```

A reflow re-runs from **Layout** onward; a repaint re-runs from **Paint** onward; a `transform` can re-run from **Composite** only — which is why it's cheap.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of printing a magazine. First you gather the **articles** (DOM) and the **style guide** (CSSOM). You decide **which pieces actually go in this issue** (render tree). You lay out **where each block sits on each page** (layout). The printer **inks the pages** (paint). Finally the binder **stacks and assembles the pages** into the finished magazine (composite). Change a headline's position and you must redo layout onward; just recolor a word and you only re-ink.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Demonstrating the cheap path: animate with transform (composite-only).
const card = document.querySelector('.card');

// Toggling a class that only changes `transform` keeps us in the
// composite stage -> no layout, no paint. Smooth even on weak devices.
card.classList.add('slide-in');
// .slide-in { transform: translateX(0); transition: transform .3s; }

// Reading layout flushes the pipeline — avoid in hot loops:
const w = card.offsetWidth; // forces layout to be up-to-date right now
console.log(w);             // e.g. 320  (a number in CSS pixels)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Walk me through how the browser turns HTML and CSS into pixels.**
A: It parses HTML into the **DOM** and CSS into the **CSSOM**, merges them into the **render tree** (visible nodes only), runs **layout** to compute geometry, **paints** pixels for each node, and **composites** the layers onto the screen. Knowing this lets you reason about cost: geometry changes re-run layout (expensive), color changes re-run paint, and `transform`/`opacity` can re-run only the composite step (cheapest).

---

### 💾 Browser Storage — localStorage vs IndexedDB vs Cookies

#### 🧩 What is it?
The browser gives you several places to store data on the user's machine so it survives page reloads. The three you must know:
- **`localStorage`** — a tiny, synchronous key→value store of **strings** (~5 MB).
- **IndexedDB** — a larger, **asynchronous** in-browser database for structured data and big blobs.
- **Cookies** — small key→value pairs (~4 KB) that are **automatically sent to the server** on every matching request.

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
JavaScript variables vanish on reload, and not everything belongs on a server. You need client-side persistence for things like a saved theme, a draft form, an offline cache, or a login token. But "store some data" isn't one problem — the right tool depends on *size*, *whether the server needs it*, and *whether blocking the main thread is acceptable*.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  STORE          SIZE     API STYLE     SENT TO SERVER?
  ───────────    ──────   ───────────   ───────────────
  cookies        ~4 KB    string KV     YES (every req)
  localStorage   ~5 MB    string KV     no
  IndexedDB      large    async DB      no
```
- **`localStorage`** is **synchronous** — a big read/write *blocks the main thread*, so keep it small. Values are always strings (use `JSON.stringify`).
- **IndexedDB** is **asynchronous** (callback/promise based), stores real objects and `Blob`s, and won't freeze the UI — ideal for offline data.
- **Cookies** ride along with HTTP requests automatically, which is why they're used for session tokens — but that auto-send is also why they carry CSRF risk and must be sized small.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
- A **cookie** is a **name tag the server clipped on you** — everywhere you go back to that server, it reads the tag automatically.
- **`localStorage`** is a **sticky note on your fridge** — quick to read, but you have to walk over and stop what you're doing to read it (synchronous), so you keep it short.
- **IndexedDB** is a **filing cabinet in another room** — holds a lot, but you send an assistant to fetch from it (asynchronous) so you keep working meanwhile.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// localStorage — synchronous, strings only
localStorage.setItem('theme', JSON.stringify({ mode: 'dark' }));
const theme = JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem('theme'));
console.log(theme.mode); // "dark"

// IndexedDB — asynchronous, stores real objects (simplified)
const open = indexedDB.open('app', 1);
open.onupgradeneeded = () => open.result.createObjectStore('notes', { keyPath: 'id' });
open.onsuccess = () => {
  const tx = open.result.transaction('notes', 'readwrite');
  tx.objectStore('notes').put({ id: 1, text: 'Buy milk' }); // no JSON needed
};
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When would you choose IndexedDB over `localStorage`?**
A: When the data is large, structured, or you can't afford to block the UI. `localStorage` is **synchronous** and string-only (~5 MB cap), so big reads/writes freeze the main thread. IndexedDB is **asynchronous**, holds real objects and binary blobs, and scales far larger — making it the right choice for offline caches and app data. Cookies are a third option, but only when the **server** needs the value on every request (e.g. a session token).

---

### 📦 Service Workers & Caching — A Programmable Network Proxy

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **Service Worker** is a special background script the browser runs **separately from your page**, sitting between your page and the network like a programmable proxy. It can intercept every network request your page makes and decide to answer from a **cache** instead of the network — which is what lets web apps load instantly and even work **offline**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Normally, if the network is slow or gone, your app fails. There was no way for web code to say "if offline, serve my saved version." Service Workers solve this: by intercepting requests, they can serve cached responses when the network is unavailable or slow, turning a fragile web page into a resilient app (a **PWA** — Progressive Web App).

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A Service Worker has its own lifecycle (`install` → `activate` → `fetch`) and runs even when no page is open. It listens for the page's network requests via a `fetch` event and chooses where the response comes from.

```
   PAGE  ──fetch('/data')──▶  SERVICE WORKER
                                   │ intercepts
                        ┌──────────┴───────────┐
                        ▼                      ▼
                   CACHE (fast)            NETWORK (fresh)
                        │                      │
                        └──────▶ response ◀────┘
   Common strategies: cache-first, network-first,
   stale-while-revalidate.
```
Key strategies: **cache-first** (fast, may be stale), **network-first** (fresh, slower), and **stale-while-revalidate** (serve cache instantly, update it in the background). The Service Worker runs on a **separate thread**, has no DOM access, and must be served over HTTPS.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A Service Worker is a **personal assistant who screens your mail**. When a letter (request) comes, the assistant decides: "I already have a copy of this in the filing cabinet — here, take it instantly" (cache), or "this needs to be fresh, let me go to the post office" (network). If the post office is closed (offline), they can still hand you the filed copy.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Inside the service worker file (sw.js)
self.addEventListener('install', event => {
  event.waitUntil(
    caches.open('v1').then(cache => cache.addAll(['/', '/app.js', '/style.css']))
  );
});

// Cache-first strategy: try cache, fall back to network
self.addEventListener('fetch', event => {
  event.respondWith(
    caches.match(event.request).then(cached => cached || fetch(event.request))
  );
});
// Result: on a repeat visit, '/app.js' loads from cache -> instant, works offline.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is a Service Worker and how does it enable offline support?**
A: It's a browser-managed background script that acts as a network proxy between your page and the server. By listening to `fetch` events it can intercept requests and answer them from the Cache Storage API instead of the network — so with a **cache-first** or **stale-while-revalidate** strategy, the app loads instantly on repeat visits and keeps working when offline. It runs on its own thread, has no DOM access, and requires HTTPS.

---

### 🌊 `fetch` & Streams — Reading a Response as It Arrives

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`fetch`** is the modern browser API for making network requests; it returns a **`Promise`** that resolves to a `Response`. The deeper idea is that a `Response` body is a **stream** — a flow of data **chunks** that arrive over time — not one giant blob you must wait for entirely. The **Streams API** lets you read those chunks **as they arrive**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
For a large download (a video, a big JSON file, an AI model's token-by-token reply), waiting for the *whole* response before doing anything is slow and memory-hungry. Streaming lets you **start processing the first bytes immediately** and handle the data piece by piece, keeping memory low and the UI responsive. It's also the foundation of progress bars and live, "typing" responses.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`fetch` resolves as soon as the **headers** arrive — the body may still be downloading. `response.body` is a **`ReadableStream`** you pull chunks from via a reader.

```
   SERVER ──chunk──chunk──chunk──▶  ReadableStream (response.body)
                                          │ reader.read()
                                          ▼
                          { value: <bytes>, done: false }  (a chunk)
                                          ▼ ...repeat...
                          { value: undefined, done: true } (stream end)
```
Each `read()` returns the next chunk (`value`) and whether the stream is finished (`done`). Convenience methods like `response.json()` or `response.text()` simply read the whole stream to the end for you.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Without streaming, getting a long letter means **waiting for the entire novel to be written, bound, and shipped** before you read word one. With streaming, it's like a **friend reading the letter aloud to you over the phone** — you hear and react to each sentence as it comes, never waiting for the whole thing. That's why a chatbot can show text appearing word by word.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const response = await fetch('/big-file.txt');
// `response` is ready once HEADERS arrive — body may still be downloading.

const reader = response.body.getReader();   // ReadableStream of bytes
const decoder = new TextDecoder();
let received = 0;

while (true) {
  const { value, done } = await reader.read(); // pull the next chunk
  if (done) break;                             // stream finished
  received += value.length;
  console.log('chunk of', value.length, 'bytes; total', received);
  // e.g. "chunk of 16384 bytes; total 16384"  -> process as it arrives
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Does `fetch`'s promise resolve only after the full response downloads?**
A: No — it resolves as soon as the **response headers** arrive; the body may still be streaming in. `response.body` is a `ReadableStream`, so you can read chunks via a reader as they arrive (great for progress bars and large downloads), or call `response.json()`/`response.text()` to consume the whole stream at once. This is also why a `fetch` 404 still *resolves* (the promise only rejects on network failure, not HTTP error status).

---

### 🏗️ Node Architecture — V8 + libuv + C++ Bindings

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Node.js** is not a language — it's a **runtime** that lets JavaScript run *outside* the browser, on a server. It's built from three main parts glued together: **V8** (the engine that runs your JS), **libuv** (a C library giving Node its event loop and asynchronous I/O), and **C++ bindings** (the bridge that lets JavaScript call down into operating-system features like files and sockets).

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
Beginners assume "Node is just JavaScript on a server," then get confused when JS — which has no concept of files or networking in the spec — suddenly reads files. The answer is the architecture: V8 only runs the *language*; everything else (file system, network, timers, threads) is provided by **libuv and C++**, exposed to your code through bindings. Knowing this demystifies *where* Node's powers actually come from.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Your JavaScript calls a Node API like `fs.readFile`. That call crosses a C++ binding into **libuv**, which performs the slow I/O on a background **thread pool** (or via the OS), then queues your callback back to V8 when it's done — all coordinated by libuv's event loop.

```
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │   YOUR JAVASCRIPT  (fs.readFile, http...)  │
   ├───────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │   NODE C++ BINDINGS  (the bridge layer)    │
   ├──────────────────┬────────────────────────┤
   │       V8         │        libuv           │
   │  runs your JS,   │  event loop +          │
   │  memory, GC      │  thread pool + async   │
   │                  │  file / network I/O    │
   ├──────────────────┴────────────────────────┤
   │        OPERATING SYSTEM (files, net)       │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

> **Note (simplification):** Node also bundles other C/C++ libraries (e.g. OpenSSL for crypto, c-ares for DNS, zlib for compression). V8 + libuv + bindings are the *core* trio that explains the model; the full runtime ships more pieces.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of Node as a **restaurant**. **V8** is the head chef who actually cooks (runs your code). But the chef can't farm, drive, or shop — so **libuv** is the team of runners and suppliers who fetch ingredients from outside (file/network I/O) on their own time. The **C++ bindings** are the order tickets that let the chef request things from the runners. The chef stays in the kitchen cooking; the runners handle the slow trips out.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const fs = require('node:fs');

console.log('1: before read');

// fs.readFile crosses a C++ binding into libuv, which reads the file on a
// background thread-pool thread, then queues the callback back to V8.
fs.readFile(__filename, 'utf8', (err, data) => {
  console.log('3: file read finished, length', data.length);
});

console.log('2: after read (runs first — read happens in background)');

// Output:
// 1: before read
// 2: after read (runs first — read happens in background)
// 3: file read finished, length <number>
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is Node.js made of, and where does its ability to read files come from?**
A: Node combines **V8** (runs the JavaScript), **libuv** (provides the event loop, a thread pool, and cross-platform async I/O), and **C++ bindings** that expose OS features to JS. JavaScript the language has no file or network APIs — those come from libuv via the bindings. So when you call `fs.readFile`, V8 runs the JS, the binding hands the work to libuv's thread pool, and your callback is queued back once the OS finishes the read.

---

### 🧱 Buffers — Handling Raw Binary Data

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **`Buffer`** is Node's container for **raw binary data** — a fixed-length chunk of bytes outside V8's normal string handling. Strings are great for text, but files, network packets, images, and encryption all deal in raw **bytes**, and a `Buffer` is how Node represents those bytes directly.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
JavaScript strings are sequences of characters (with encoding rules), not raw bytes — and not every byte sequence is valid text. When you read a JPEG, receive a TCP packet, or hash a password, mangling the bytes through string conversion would corrupt the data. `Buffer` gives you a faithful, byte-exact view so binary data stays intact.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A `Buffer` is a thin Node wrapper around a `Uint8Array` (a typed array of bytes), backed by memory allocated **outside** V8's heap. Each slot holds one byte (0–255). You can create them from text (with an encoding), slice them, and concatenate them.

```
   "Hi" as a Buffer (UTF-8 encoding):
   ┌──────┬──────┐
   │  72  │ 105  │   <- byte values (decimal)
   │ 0x48 │ 0x69 │   <- same bytes in hex
   └──────┴──────┘
     'H'    'i'
   Each cell is exactly one byte (0–255).
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A string is a **sentence you can read**; a `Buffer` is the **raw ink and paper** that sentence is printed on. To photocopy a document faithfully — including a watermark or a coffee stain — you copy the *physical page* (bytes), not your *interpretation* of the words (the string). Binary data needs the page, not the reading.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const buf = Buffer.from('Hi', 'utf8');

console.log(buf);          // <Buffer 48 69>   -> bytes in hex
console.log(buf.length);   // 2                -> two bytes
console.log(buf[0]);       // 72               -> byte value of 'H'
console.log(buf.toString('utf8')); // "Hi"     -> back to text

// A multi-byte character takes more than one byte:
console.log(Buffer.from('é', 'utf8').length); // 2  -> 'é' is 2 bytes in UTF-8
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why does Node have `Buffer` instead of just using strings?**
A: Because not all data is text. Files, network packets, images, and crypto operate on **raw bytes**, and forcing them through JavaScript's string handling (which assumes a character encoding) can corrupt them. A `Buffer` is a byte-exact container (a `Uint8Array` backed by memory outside V8's regular JS-object heap — small buffers come from a shared pool) that represents binary data faithfully — essential for I/O and any byte-level work.

---

### 🚰 Streams & Backpressure — Don't Overflow the Bucket

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **Stream** in Node processes data **piece by piece** as it flows, instead of loading it all into memory at once. **Backpressure** is the safety mechanism that kicks in when data is *produced faster than it can be consumed* — it signals the fast producer to **pause** until the slow consumer catches up, preventing memory from ballooning.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Imagine copying a 10 GB file. Reading it all into memory first would crash the process. Streams fix that by moving it in chunks. But a new problem appears: if you *read* from a fast disk faster than you can *write* to a slow network, the unwritten chunks pile up in memory until you run out. **Backpressure** is the flow-control that stops the reader when the writer's buffer is full — keeping memory bounded.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A writable stream has an internal buffer with a limit (the **high-water mark**). When you call `writable.write(chunk)` and the buffer is full, `write` returns **`false`** — your cue to **stop reading** and wait. When the buffer drains, the stream emits a **`'drain'`** event telling you it's safe to resume. The simplest correct approach is `.pipe()` (or `pipeline`), which wires up backpressure for you automatically.

```
   FAST PRODUCER            SLOW CONSUMER
   (read 10GB file)         (write to network)
        │  chunks                  │
        ▼                          ▼
   ┌─────────┐   write()    ┌────────────┐
   │ reader  │ ───────────▶ │  writer's  │
   │         │ ◀─ false ─── │  buffer    │  buffer FULL!
   │ PAUSES  │   (full)     │ [■■■■■■■■]  │
   └─────────┘              └─────┬──────┘
        ▲                         │ drains over time
        └──── 'drain' event ──────┘
              "ok, resume!"
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Picture a **bartender pouring drinks faster than guests can drink them**. Without backpressure, the bar counter overflows with full glasses (memory fills up). Backpressure is the bartender **watching the counter**: when it's full, they *stop pouring* and wait until guests finish a few (the `'drain'`), then resume. The flow self-regulates so nothing spills.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const fs = require('node:fs');

// ✅ pipe() handles backpressure automatically: if the WRITE side is slow,
// it pauses the READ side for you. Memory stays bounded even for huge files.
const read = fs.createReadStream('huge-input.dat');
const write = fs.createWriteStream('huge-output.dat');
read.pipe(write);   // copies 10GB using only small, constant memory

// ⚠️ Manual write WITHOUT respecting the return value -> unbounded memory:
// while (lots) writable.write(chunk);  // ignores `false` -> buffer explodes
//
// ✅ Manual, done right:
function writeChunk(writable, chunk, next) {
  const ok = writable.write(chunk);          // false means "buffer full"
  if (!ok) writable.once('drain', next);     // wait, then continue
  else next();
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Explain backpressure in streams.**
A: Backpressure is flow control for when a producer outpaces a consumer. A writable stream buffers up to its **high-water mark**; once full, `write()` returns **`false`**, signaling the producer to *pause*. When the buffer drains, the stream emits a **`'drain'`** event to resume. It prevents unbounded memory growth when, say, reading from a fast disk while writing to a slow socket. Using `.pipe()` or `stream.pipeline()` wires this up automatically — which is why those are preferred over manual `write` loops.

---

### 🧵 Worker Threads vs Cluster vs Child Processes — Choosing Parallelism

#### 🧩 What is it?
Node runs your JavaScript on **one thread**, so a heavy CPU task (like resizing 1000 images) blocks everything. Node offers three ways to get more parallelism:
- **Worker Threads** — extra JS threads **inside the same process**, able to share memory.
- **Cluster** — multiple **copies of your whole app**, sharing one server port to spread incoming connections.
- **Child Processes** — spawn **any separate program** (a script, a binary like `ffmpeg`) as its own OS process.

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
The single thread is great for I/O but terrible for CPU-bound work: one heavy computation freezes every request. And a single process can only use **one CPU core**, wasting a multi-core server. These three tools each unlock parallelism — but for *different* jobs, and picking the wrong one wastes effort or memory.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  TOOL             WHAT IT SPAWNS       SHARES MEMORY?  BEST FOR
  ──────────────   ──────────────────   ──────────────  ───────────────
  Worker Threads   threads in 1 process  yes (Shared-    CPU-bound JS
                                          ArrayBuffer)   in-process
  Cluster          full app copies       no (separate    scaling a
                   (forked processes)     processes)     server across
                                                         CPU cores
  Child Process    any external program  no (IPC msgs)   running other
                                                         binaries/scripts
```
- **Worker Threads** are lightest for *CPU-bound JavaScript*; they can share memory via `SharedArrayBuffer` and message via `postMessage`.
- **Cluster** forks several copies of your server and load-balances connections across them — the classic way to use **all CPU cores** for an HTTP server.
- **Child Processes** run *anything*, even non-JS programs, communicating over IPC (inter-process messages).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
- **Worker Threads** are **extra cooks in the same kitchen** — they share the same fridge (memory) and split one big dish's prep.
- **Cluster** is **opening identical franchise branches of your restaurant** — each is a full, independent restaurant, and a host out front sends each customer to whichever branch is free (sharing the port).
- **Child Process** is **calling a specialist outside vendor** — say a bakery (`ffmpeg`) — to make something your kitchen doesn't, and they deliver the result back.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Worker Threads — offload a CPU-bound computation off the main thread.
const { Worker, isMainThread, parentPort, workerData } = require('node:worker_threads');

if (isMainThread) {
  // Main thread: stays responsive while the worker crunches numbers.
  const worker = new Worker(__filename, { workerData: 40 });
  worker.on('message', sum => console.log('result:', sum)); // result: 102334155
} else {
  // Worker thread: heavy fibonacci runs HERE, not blocking the main thread.
  const fib = n => (n < 2 ? n : fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2));
  parentPort.postMessage(fib(workerData));
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Worker threads vs cluster — when do you use each?**
A: Use **Worker Threads** for **CPU-bound JavaScript** you want to run in parallel without freezing the main thread — they're lightweight, live in the same process, and can share memory via `SharedArrayBuffer`. Use **Cluster** to **scale a server across CPU cores**: it forks multiple copies of your app that share one port, so incoming requests are balanced across processes. Rule of thumb: cluster scales *throughput of I/O-bound servers* across cores; worker threads offload *CPU-heavy computation*. Use **child processes** when you need to run a *separate program* entirely.

---

### ⚙️ `process` & Lifecycle — Talking to the Outside World

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`process`** is a global object in Node representing the **running program itself**. It's your window into the environment around your code: command-line arguments, environment variables, the current working directory, and the events that fire as your program starts up and shuts down (its **lifecycle**).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A real server doesn't run in isolation — it needs configuration (database URLs via environment variables), it must read CLI arguments, and crucially it must **shut down cleanly**: when the host sends a "please stop" signal, a well-behaved app should finish in-flight requests, close database connections, and *then* exit. Without hooking into `process`, your app can't read its config or shut down gracefully, leading to dropped requests and corrupted data.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **`process.env`** — environment variables (e.g. `process.env.DATABASE_URL`), the standard way to inject config.
- **`process.argv`** — array of command-line arguments.
- **Lifecycle signals & events** — the OS can send signals like **`SIGTERM`** (graceful stop) and **`SIGINT`** (Ctrl-C). Node turns these into events you can listen for to clean up before exiting. `process.on('exit', ...)` fires as the process ends.

```
   START ─▶ run your code ─▶ event loop has work? ──yes──┐
                                   │ no                   │
                                   ▼                      │
                            process empties &  ◀──────────┘
                            exits naturally
            ── OR the OS sends a signal ──▶ SIGTERM / SIGINT
                                            │ you catch it,
                                            ▼ clean up, then
                                       process.exit()
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`process` is the **shop manager** of your program's storefront. It knows the **store rules posted out back** (`env` config), the **note left on the door** (CLI args), and — most importantly — it hears the **"closing time" announcement** (`SIGTERM`). A good manager doesn't just flip the lights off mid-customer; they finish serving the people inside, lock up properly, then leave (graceful shutdown).

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Read config from the environment (the 12-factor way)
const port = process.env.PORT || 3000;
console.log('starting on port', port); // e.g. "starting on port 3000"

// Graceful shutdown: finish work, then exit cleanly.
process.on('SIGTERM', () => {
  console.log('SIGTERM received — closing server, draining connections...');
  // server.close(() => process.exit(0));  // exit AFTER cleanup
});

// Last-chance hook as the process ends (must be synchronous here):
process.on('exit', code => console.log('exiting with code', code)); // e.g. 0
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you gracefully shut down a Node server?**
A: Listen for the OS termination signal — **`process.on('SIGTERM', ...)`** (and often `SIGINT` for Ctrl-C) — and in the handler stop accepting new connections, finish in-flight requests, close database/socket connections, then call `process.exit(0)`. This avoids dropping live requests or leaving resources half-closed during a deploy or container restart, where orchestrators like Kubernetes send `SIGTERM` before forcefully killing the process.

---

### 💣 Cross-Environment Differences — What Bites You in Production

#### 🧩 What is it?
Browser and Node both run JavaScript, but they are **different hosts** with different global objects, APIs, and module systems. Code that works in one can silently break in the other — and modern tools (server-side rendering, shared code between frontend and backend) run the *same* code in *both*, so these differences become real production bugs.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
A common painful scenario: you write a utility that reads `window.localStorage` or `document`, it works perfectly in the browser, then you import it into a server-rendered page and the server crashes with `window is not defined`. Or you assume `fetch` exists, but you're on an older Node. Knowing the gaps lets you write portable code and avoid "works on my machine" disasters.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  FEATURE          BROWSER             NODE.JS
  ──────────────   ─────────────────   ─────────────────────
  global object    window / self       global / globalThis
  DOM (document)   yes                  NO  -> ReferenceError
  localStorage     yes                  no (use a DB/file)
  fetch            yes                  yes (Node 18+ only)
  modules          ESM (import)         CJS + ESM both
  __dirname        no                   yes (CJS)
  Buffer           no                   yes
  process          no                   yes
```
The portable global is **`globalThis`** (works in both). The safe pattern is **feature detection**: check `typeof window !== 'undefined'` before touching browser-only APIs, rather than assuming the environment.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like the difference between driving in the **US (right side)** and the **UK (left side)**. Both are "driving a car" with the same basic skills, but the rules of the road differ — and assuming the wrong one is exactly how accidents happen. A careful driver *checks which country they're in* before pulling out (feature detection) instead of assuming.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// ❌ Crashes on the server during SSR: `window` doesn't exist in Node.
// const theme = window.localStorage.getItem('theme'); // ReferenceError!

// ✅ Feature-detect the environment before using host-specific APIs.
const isBrowser = typeof window !== 'undefined';

function getTheme() {
  if (isBrowser) {
    return window.localStorage.getItem('theme'); // browser path
  }
  return process.env.DEFAULT_THEME || 'light';   // Node/SSR path
}

// `globalThis` works in BOTH environments:
globalThis.appVersion = '1.0.0'; // safe in browser AND Node
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why might code that works in the browser crash on a Node server, and how do you write portable code?**
A: Because they're different hosts: the browser has `window`, `document`, and `localStorage`; Node has `process`, `Buffer`, and `__dirname` — and each is missing the other's globals, so touching `window` in Node throws `ReferenceError`. Write portable code by using **`globalThis`** for the global object and **feature-detecting** (`typeof window !== 'undefined'`) before using any host-specific API, rather than assuming the environment. This matters most for code shared between client and server, like SSR.

---

<h2 id="module-14">Module 14 — Error Handling, Debugging & Observability</h2>

Every program eventually hits something it didn't expect — a missing file, a dead network, a typo in user input. The difference between a fragile app and a trustworthy one isn't *avoiding* errors (impossible) — it's how cleanly you **catch**, **propagate**, and **learn from** them. This module is about turning crashes into information.

---

### 🏷️ Error Types & Custom Error Classes — naming what went wrong

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **error** in JavaScript is just an object — almost always an instance of the built-in `Error` class. It carries a human-readable `message`, a `name`, and a `stack` (a text snapshot of where it happened). JavaScript ships several built-in subclasses like `TypeError`, `RangeError`, and `SyntaxError`, and you can make your **own** by extending `Error`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
If every failure throws a generic `Error("something broke")`, your `catch` block can't tell a *bad user input* apart from a *database outage* — and those need totally different handling (show a form message vs. page an on-call engineer). Custom error **classes** give each failure a *type* you can branch on with `instanceof`, instead of fragile string-matching on the message text.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- You **subclass** `Error` and set a `name` so logs read clearly.
- Since ES2022, `Error` accepts an **`options` object** with a **`cause`** field — a place to attach the *original* error when you wrap it in a higher-level one. This builds an **error chain** without losing the root cause.
- A subclassing **pitfall:** the `name` property is *not* set automatically — if you forget `this.name = "..."`, it stays `"Error"`. Set it in the constructor.

```
  Low-level failure        Wrapped (higher-level) error
  ┌──────────────────┐     ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ SyntaxError      │ ───▶│ ConfigError                │
  │ "Unexpected {"   │ cause│ "Failed to load config"    │
  └──────────────────┘     │   .cause ─▶ SyntaxError     │
                           └────────────────────────────┘
   You keep BOTH: the friendly message AND the root cause.
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a hospital. "The patient is unwell" (generic `Error`) is useless to a nurse. A **diagnosis** — "broken arm" vs. "allergic reaction" (a *typed* error) — routes them to the right treatment. And the chart noting "broken arm, **caused by** a fall on the stairs" (`cause`) preserves the story so the next doctor isn't guessing.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// A custom error type. Note: we MUST set `name` ourselves.
class ValidationError extends Error {
  constructor(message, options) {
    super(message, options);   // forwards { cause } to Error (ES2022+)
    this.name = "ValidationError";
  }
}

try {
  try {
    JSON.parse("{bad");        // throws a built-in SyntaxError
  } catch (rootCause) {
    // Wrap it, but keep the original via `cause`.
    throw new ValidationError("Bad config file", { cause: rootCause });
  }
} catch (err) {
  console.log(err.name);                 // "ValidationError"
  console.log(err.message);              // "Bad config file"
  console.log(err.cause.name);           // "SyntaxError"  <- root preserved
  console.log(err instanceof Error);     // true
  console.log(err instanceof ValidationError); // true  <- branch on this
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why subclass `Error` instead of throwing strings or plain objects, and what's a common subclassing pitfall?**
A: Throwing a real `Error` gives you a `stack` trace and lets callers branch with `instanceof` — the type *is* the routing information. Throwing a raw string (`throw "oops"`) loses the stack entirely. The classic pitfall: the subclass's `name` is **not** set for you — you must assign `this.name` in the constructor, or every custom error still reports as `"Error"`. (Historically you also needed `Object.setPrototypeOf` when transpiling to ES5, but in modern engines extending `Error` works directly.)

---

### 🔀 Sync vs. Async Error Propagation — why `try/catch` sometimes misses

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Propagation** is how a thrown error travels *up* the call stack looking for a `catch`. For **synchronous** code (running right now, top to bottom), `try/catch` reliably catches it. For **asynchronous** code (code that runs *later*, on a future tick of the event loop — see Module 7), a plain `try/catch` often **cannot** catch the throw, because by the time the error fires, the `try` block has already finished.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A beginner wraps `setTimeout(...)` in a `try/catch`, the callback throws, and the app *still crashes* — the `catch` never ran. Understanding *when* an error is "in scope" of a `try` is the difference between error handling that works and error handling that's quietly decorative.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A `try/catch` only guards work that happens **during** the synchronous run of the `try` block. A callback scheduled for *later* (a timer, an I/O completion) runs on a fresh, empty call stack — your `catch` is long gone.

```
  TICK 1 (now)                    TICK 2 (later)
  ┌─────────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────┐
  │ try {               │         │ callback runs here  │
  │   setTimeout(cb) ───┼──sched─▶│   throw Error()  💥  │
  │ } catch (e) { ... } │         │ (try/catch is GONE) │
  └─────────────────────┘         └─────────────────────┘
   The catch protects TICK 1 only — not the later callback.
```

- For **Promises** (Module 8), a rejection propagates through `.catch()`, OR — inside an `async` function — through a normal `try/catch` around `await`. The `await` "pauses" the function so the rejection lands *as if* it were synchronous.
- A Promise that rejects with **no** `.catch()` anywhere triggers an **unhandled rejection** — a global warning (and in modern Node, a process crash) telling you an error fell on the floor.

> **Note (simplification):** `await` doesn't *literally* make async code synchronous — it suspends the function and resumes it on a later microtask. But for error-handling purposes, a `try/catch` around `await` behaves the way you'd intuitively want.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A `try/catch` is a **safety net stretched under a trapeze act**. It only catches acrobats who fall *while it's set up*. A `setTimeout` callback is an acrobat who performs **tomorrow**, after the crew has packed the net away — so when *they* fall, there's nothing underneath. To catch async failures you must put a *fresh* net under the future act (`.catch()` or `try/catch` around `await`).

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// ❌ This catch does NOTHING — the throw happens on a later tick.
try {
  setTimeout(() => {
    throw new Error("boom");   // fires after try/catch is gone
  }, 0);
} catch (e) {
  console.log("caught?", e.message); // never runs
}
// Output: an UNCAUGHT error later — the catch above never fired.

// ✅ With promises + await, a try/catch DOES work:
async function load() {
  try {
    await Promise.reject(new Error("network down"));
  } catch (e) {
    console.log("await caught:", e.message); // "await caught: network down"
  }
}
load();

// ❌ Unhandled rejection: no .catch(), no await-in-try
Promise.reject(new Error("nobody is listening"));
// -> triggers an "unhandledRejection" warning / crash
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you handle errors across async boundaries, and why can't a `try/catch` around `setTimeout` work?**
A: For Promises, you handle rejections with `.catch()` or with a `try/catch` wrapping an `await` inside an `async` function — `await` resumes the function so the rejection surfaces synchronously to that `catch`. A `try/catch` around `setTimeout` fails because the callback runs on a **later tick** with a brand-new call stack; the `try` block already completed, so there's no `catch` in scope when the throw happens. The error must instead be caught *inside* the callback, or the work modeled as a Promise.

---

### 🧹 `try/catch/finally` Semantics — and the `finally`-overrides-`return` trap

#### 🧩 What is it?
`try` runs risky code; `catch (e)` runs **only if** the `try` threw; `finally` runs **always** — whether the `try` succeeded, threw, or even `return`ed. `finally` is for *cleanup* (closing a file, releasing a lock) that must happen no matter what.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You open a database connection, then your query throws. Without `finally`, the connection stays open and leaks. `finally` guarantees the cleanup line runs on *every* exit path. But it has a sharp edge: a `return` (or `throw`) **inside** `finally` *overrides* whatever the `try` or `catch` was about to return or throw — silently discarding it. This surprises almost everyone.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
When control leaves the `try` (via normal end, `return`, or a thrown error), the engine **pauses** that exit, runs the `finally` block, and *then* completes the original exit — **unless** `finally` itself does a `return` or `throw`, which **wins**.

```
  try completes (value V or error E) pending
                │
                ▼
        run finally block
                │
       ┌────────┴─────────┐
   finally returns?     finally just
   finally throws?      cleans up?
       │                     │
       ▼                     ▼
  THAT wins; V/E       original V/E
   is discarded         proceeds
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`finally` is the **janitor who locks up the building every night** — they come no matter how your workday ended (great meeting, fire drill, you quit on the spot). But if that janitor *also rewrites your final report on the way out* (`return` in `finally`), nobody ever sees what you actually wrote — their version is the one that ships. Cleanup is good; *overwriting the outcome* is the trap.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// 1) finally OVERRIDES the try's return — surprising!
function f() {
  try {
    return "from try";
  } finally {
    return "from finally";   // ⚠️ this wins
  }
}
console.log(f()); // "from finally"   (NOT "from try")

// 2) finally with NO return just runs, then the try's value proceeds
function g() {
  try {
    return "from try";
  } finally {
    console.log("cleanup runs"); // logs, but doesn't change the result
  }
}
console.log(g());
// "cleanup runs"
// "from try"

// 3) DANGER: a return in finally SWALLOWS a thrown error
function h() {
  try {
    throw new Error("original");
  } finally {
    return "swallowed";   // ⚠️ the Error is silently lost
  }
}
console.log(h()); // "swallowed"  — the error vanished, no throw!
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `finally` do when the `try` block has a `return`?**
A: `finally` **always** runs before the function actually returns. If `finally` just does cleanup, the `try`'s return value is preserved. But if `finally` *itself* contains a `return` (or `throw`), that overrides and **replaces** the `try`'s pending return value or pending exception — silently discarding it. The rule of thumb: never `return` or `throw` from `finally`; use it for cleanup only.

---

### 🧭 Stack Traces, Async Stack Traces & Source Maps — reading the crime scene

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **stack trace** is the text list attached to an error's `.stack` property showing the chain of function calls that led to the throw — the **innermost** (where it broke) at the top, callers below. **Async stack traces** stitch this chain *across* `await` boundaries. **Source maps** are side files that translate a confusing minified/transpiled line back to your *original* source line.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
"`TypeError: cannot read 'x' of undefined`" tells you *what* broke but not *where*. The stack trace is your map to the exact line. The problem: across `await`, older engines lost the trail (the trace ended at some internal scheduler, not your code). And in production, code is **minified** (renamed to one letter, crunched onto one line) — so a raw trace points at `a.b.c:1:5028`, which is meaningless without a source map.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  Error: cannot read 'x' of undefined
      at parseUser   (user.js:12)   ← where it threw (innermost)
      at loadProfile (page.js:40)   ← who called parseUser
      at main        (app.js:8)     ← outermost caller
   Read top → down = failure point → original trigger.
```
- **Async traces:** modern V8 (Chrome/Node) reconnects the chain through `await`, so the trace shows the `async` caller, not just `Timeout._onTimeout`. This is mostly automatic in current engines.
- **Source maps:** the build tool emits a `.map` file; your error-tracking tool (or DevTools) uses it to **un-minify** the trace back to `user.js:12` from the garbled production line. You ship maps to your *monitoring* service, not necessarily to end users.

> **Note (simplification):** stack traces are not part of the ECMAScript standard — `Error.prototype.stack` is a near-universal extension, but its exact *format* varies by engine. Don't parse it with brittle regexes across browsers.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A stack trace is a **package's tracking history**, read newest-first: "Failed at the doorstep → out for delivery → left the warehouse." It tells you the whole journey to the failure. A source map is the **translation key** for a coded shipping label — the warehouse prints `7Q-X91`, and the key turns it back into "123 Main St" so a human can act on it.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
function parseUser(data) {
  return data.profile.name;   // throws if data.profile is undefined
}
function loadProfile() {
  return parseUser({});       // no .profile -> boom
}

try {
  loadProfile();
} catch (err) {
  console.log(err.message);
  // "Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'name')"
  console.log(err.stack.split("\n")[0]); // first line: the message
  // The full err.stack lists: parseUser -> loadProfile -> (caller)
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Your production error shows `at a (main.js:1:90213)` — useless. How do you get a readable trace?**
A: That's **minified** code. Generate and upload **source maps** from your build to your error-monitoring tool (or load them in DevTools); the map translates the crunched line/column back to the original file, function name, and line. For async code, rely on modern engines' **async stack traces**, which reconnect the call chain across `await` so the trace points at your real caller instead of an internal scheduler frame.

---

### 🛡️ Defensive Design & Production Observability — failing on purpose, learning in the open

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Defensive design** is deciding, on purpose, how your code reacts to bad conditions: **fail fast** (stop loudly and immediately when something is truly wrong) vs. **degrade gracefully** (keep working in a reduced mode). **Observability** is making a *running* system explain itself — through **structured logging** (logs as machine-readable data, not loose sentences) and **telemetry** (numbers and traces about health and performance).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Swallowing every error "to be safe" creates *zombie* programs that limp along producing wrong results no one notices for weeks. And once code is deployed on a server you can't attach a debugger to, the only way to know *what happened* is the evidence it left behind. Plain-text logs like `"error happened"` can't be searched, filtered, or alerted on at scale — you need structure.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
**Fail fast vs. degrade** — choose per situation:

```
  Is the data/state CORRECTABLE or optional?
        │
   ┌────┴─────────────────────────┐
   NO (corrupt config,            YES (one widget's API
   broken invariant)               is down, rest is fine)
        │                              │
        ▼                              ▼
   FAIL FAST: throw now,          DEGRADE: catch, log,
   crash loudly, fix root         show fallback UI, keep
   cause early.                   the app usable.
```

- **Structured logging:** emit logs as objects (often JSON) with consistent fields — `level`, `message`, `errorName`, `userId`, a `requestId` to tie related logs together. Now your log system can *query* them ("all `level: error` for `userId: 42`").
- **Telemetry:** counts, latencies, and **distributed traces** flowing to a monitoring service so you can alert *before* users complain.
- **Error boundaries (conceptual):** a wrapper that catches failures from a *region* of the app and shows a fallback instead of taking the whole thing down — degradation as a pattern.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Fail-fast is a **smoke detector**: the instant something's truly wrong, it shrieks so you fix it *now* — far better than a silent slow burn. Graceful degradation is a **car's limp-home mode**: the engine detects a fault, drops to low power, and still gets you to the garage instead of dying on the highway. Structured logging is the difference between a witness saying "stuff happened" and one handing you a **filled-out incident form** with time, location, and badge number — one you can file, search, and act on.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Structured logging: emit DATA, not loose prose.
function logError(err, context) {
  console.error(JSON.stringify({
    level: "error",
    name: err.name,
    message: err.message,
    cause: err.cause?.message ?? null,  // null-safe (Module 6)
    ...context,                          // e.g. requestId, userId
    at: new Date().toISOString(),
  }));
}

// FAIL FAST on a broken invariant — better a loud crash than silent wrong data.
function getConfig(env) {
  const url = env.DATABASE_URL;
  if (!url) {
    throw new Error("DATABASE_URL is required"); // stop now, fix the root cause
  }
  return { url };
}

// DEGRADE gracefully for an optional feature.
async function loadRecommendations(userId) {
  try {
    return await fetchRecommendations(userId);
  } catch (err) {
    logError(err, { userId, feature: "recommendations" });
    return [];   // app keeps working with an empty list
  }
}
// Example log line emitted on failure:
// {"level":"error","name":"TypeError","message":"...","userId":42,...}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When should code fail fast versus degrade gracefully, and what makes logging "structured"?**
A: **Fail fast** when the problem is *uncorrectable or violates an invariant* — a missing required config, corrupt state — because limping along produces silent wrong results; a loud, early crash is cheaper to fix. **Degrade gracefully** when the failure is *isolated and optional* — one non-critical widget's API is down — so you catch it, log it, show a fallback, and keep the core app usable. **Structured logging** means emitting logs as machine-readable records (e.g. JSON with fields like `level`, `errorName`, `requestId`) instead of free-form sentences, so a log system can search, filter, correlate, and alert on them at scale.

---

<h2 id="module-15">Module 15 — Security Internals</h2>

Most JavaScript security bugs aren't exotic — they're the predictable result of trusting data you shouldn't. An attacker rarely "hacks the engine"; they hand your code a string, an object key, or a request your code never expected, and *your own code* does the damage. This module shows the handful of attack patterns every senior engineer must recognize on sight — and the small, boring defenses that stop them.

---

### 🧬 Prototype Pollution — Poisoning Every Object At Once

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Prototype pollution** is an attack where an attacker writes to `Object.prototype` — the shared blueprint that **every** plain object in your program inherits from. Change one property there and *every* object in the app suddenly appears to have that property. The usual entry point is the special key `__proto__` (glossed: a back-door name that points at an object's prototype).

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know it? (the problem)
In JavaScript, almost every object inherits from one shared parent object (`Object.prototype`). If untrusted input can set a property on that parent, the attacker isn't editing *one* object — they're editing the **default** for all of them. That can flip a security flag (`user.isAdmin` becomes `true` even though it was never set), crash the app, or in the worst case lead to remote code execution when the polluted value reaches a sink like a template engine.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The classic bug is an **unsafe recursive merge** — code that copies keys from untrusted input into a target object, walking nested objects, *without* checking for the magic key `__proto__`. When the attacker's key path is `__proto__.isAdmin`, the merge "walks into" the prototype and writes there.

```
  attacker JSON:  { "__proto__": { "isAdmin": true } }
        │  unsafe deep-merge walks the key path
        ▼
  target.__proto__   ── is ──▶  Object.prototype
        │  merge writes isAdmin here
        ▼
  Object.prototype.isAdmin = true
        │  now EVERY object inherits it
        ▼
  ({}).isAdmin  ===  true     ← whole app poisoned
```

Mitigations all share one idea — **don't give attacker input a path to the prototype**:
- Create lookup objects with `Object.create(null)` — an object with **no** prototype, so `__proto__` is just an ordinary key, not a back door.
- Use a `Map` instead of a plain object for untrusted key/value data — `Map` keys never touch `Object.prototype`.
- Reject or skip the keys `__proto__`, `constructor`, and `prototype` in any merge.
- `Object.freeze(Object.prototype)` makes the prototype read-only (a blunt but effective lock).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Imagine a print shop with one **master template** behind every blank form. A customer is supposed to fill in *their* form. Prototype pollution is a customer who reaches past their form and scribbles "PRE-APPROVED" onto the **master template** — now every form anyone prints comes out pre-approved. `Object.create(null)` is handing customers a form with **no master behind it**; freezing is bolting the master template in a glass case.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// ❌ VULNERABLE — naive recursive merge, no key guard
function merge(target, source) {
  for (const key in source) {
    if (typeof source[key] === 'object' && source[key] !== null) {
      target[key] ??= {};
      merge(target[key], source[key]); // walks into __proto__
    } else {
      target[key] = source[key];
    }
  }
  return target;
}

const payload = JSON.parse('{"__proto__":{"isAdmin":true}}');
merge({}, payload);
console.log(({}).isAdmin); // true  ← every object is now "admin"

// ✅ SAFE — refuse the dangerous keys
const BANNED = new Set(['__proto__', 'constructor', 'prototype']);
function safeMerge(target, source) {
  for (const key in source) {
    if (BANNED.has(key)) continue;          // never walk into the prototype
    if (typeof source[key] === 'object' && source[key] !== null) {
      target[key] ??= {};
      safeMerge(target[key], source[key]);
    } else {
      target[key] = source[key];
    }
  }
  return target;
}

// ✅ SAFER STILL — store untrusted data where the prototype can't be reached
const lookup = Object.create(null); // no prototype at all
lookup['__proto__'] = 'just a string'; // ordinary key, harmless
```

> **Note (simplification):** modern `JSON.parse` does **not** itself pollute anything — it places `__proto__` as a normal own property on the result. The pollution only happens when *your* code (a merge, a `lodash.set`-style path setter, a query-string parser) later walks that key onto the real prototype. The vulnerability is in the handling, not in `JSON.parse`.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is prototype pollution and how do you defend against it?**
A: It's writing to `Object.prototype` via attacker-controlled keys — usually `__proto__` — so the injected property is inherited by *every* object, which can forge flags like `isAdmin` or crash the app. It comes from unsafe recursive merges / deep-set helpers that don't filter dangerous keys. Defenses: store untrusted data in a `Map` or an `Object.create(null)` object, explicitly skip `__proto__`/`constructor`/`prototype` in merges, validate input against a schema, and optionally `Object.freeze(Object.prototype)`.

---

### 💉 XSS — When The Page Runs The Attacker's Script

#### 🧩 What is it?
**XSS** (Cross-Site Scripting) is an attack where the attacker's JavaScript ends up running inside *your* page, in your users' browsers. Because it runs as your site, it can read cookies, session tokens, and anything the logged-in user can see.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know it? (the problem)
The browser can't tell the difference between script *you* wrote and script that arrived as user data — it just runs whatever is in the page. So if you take untrusted text (a comment, a username, a URL parameter) and drop it into the page **as HTML**, an embedded `<script>` or `onerror=` handler executes with full access to the victim's session.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
There are three flavors, by *where* the malicious data comes from:

```
  STORED    attacker's text saved in DB → served to every viewer
  REFLECTED attacker's text in the URL → echoed straight back
  DOM-BASED client JS reads location/URL → writes it into the page
```

The core mechanism is a **sink** — a place where a string becomes live HTML or code. `element.innerHTML = userText` is the classic dangerous sink: the browser *parses* the string as HTML. `element.textContent = userText` is the safe twin: it inserts the string as plain **text**, so `<script>` shows up as visible characters, not an executed tag.

Defenses:
- **Output encoding** — convert `<`, `>`, `&`, `"` into their harmless HTML entities (e.g. `<` becomes `&lt;`) *at the point you render*, so the browser shows them instead of running them. Frameworks like React do this automatically for `{value}`.
- Prefer `textContent` over `innerHTML` for any untrusted string.
- **Trusted Types** — a browser feature that *forbids* assigning a plain string to dangerous sinks like `innerHTML`; you must first pass it through a sanitizer policy, which makes risky sinks impossible to use by accident.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A radio host reads listener text messages live on air. **Output encoding** is the host reading a message *literally* — "open angle bracket, s-c-r-i-p-t" — so nothing happens. **XSS** is the host treating the message as a *production cue* and actually running it, so a listener can say "play this ad for free" and it airs. **Trusted Types** is a station rule: no message goes on air until a screener has approved it.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
const comment = '<img src=x onerror="steal(document.cookie)">';

// ❌ VULNERABLE — innerHTML parses the string AS HTML; onerror fires
document.getElementById('out').innerHTML = comment;

// ✅ SAFE — textContent inserts it as literal TEXT, never executed
document.getElementById('out').textContent = comment;
// page shows the characters: <img src=x onerror="...">

// ✅ If you truly need HTML, sanitize first (e.g. DOMPurify)
import DOMPurify from 'dompurify';
el.innerHTML = DOMPurify.sanitize(comment); // strips the onerror handler

// In React, this is automatic — {value} is text-encoded for you:
//   <div>{comment}</div>     → safe, rendered as text
//   <div dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: comment }} /> → NOT safe
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `innerHTML` and `textContent`, and why does it matter for XSS?**
A: `innerHTML` **parses** its string as HTML, so any tags or event handlers in it become live and can execute — it's an XSS sink. `textContent` inserts the string as **plain text**, so markup is shown literally and nothing runs. For untrusted data, default to `textContent` (or framework text-binding); only use `innerHTML` after sanitizing, ideally enforced by Trusted Types.

---

### 🎭 CSRF — Riding The User's Logged-In Session

#### 🧩 What is it?
**CSRF** (Cross-Site Request Forgery) tricks a logged-in user's browser into sending a real, authenticated request to *your* site **without the user meaning to** — for example, a hidden form on an attacker's page that submits "transfer money" to your bank API.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know it? (the problem)
Browsers **automatically attach cookies** to any request going to a site, no matter *who* triggered the request. So if a user is logged into `bank.com` (and the session lives in a cookie), a malicious page at `evil.com` can fire a request to `bank.com`, and the browser helpfully includes the bank's session cookie. The server sees a valid, authenticated request and obeys it.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  victim logged into bank.com  (session cookie stored)
        │ later visits evil.com
        ▼
  evil.com auto-submits hidden form → POST bank.com/transfer
        │ browser ATTACHES bank.com cookie automatically
        ▼
  bank.com sees a valid session → executes the transfer
```

The fix is to require something the attacker's site **cannot** supply or read:
- **`SameSite` cookies** — mark the session cookie `SameSite=Lax` or `Strict` so the browser **won't send it** on cross-site requests. `Lax` is the modern browser default and blocks cross-site POST/AJAX from sending the cookie — though state-changing **GET** endpoints still need their own protection.
- **CSRF tokens** — a secret random value the server puts in the page and expects back in the request body/header. Because of the same-origin policy, `evil.com`'s script can't read that token from your page, so it can't forge a valid request.

Note that CSRF is about *making* requests, not *reading* responses — the attacker never sees the reply, they just trigger the side effect.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Your house key (the cookie) is on a keyring the doorman (browser) carries and uses automatically. A con artist mails you a letter that says "walk to your door and turn the key" — and because the doorman always uses your key on command, the door opens. A **CSRF token** is a daily secret password the con artist can't possibly know; the doorman now also demands that password before turning the key.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// The attacker's page — no script needed, just an auto-submitting form
//   <form action="https://bank.com/transfer" method="POST">
//     <input name="to" value="attacker"><input name="amount" value="5000">
//   </form>
//   <script>document.forms[0].submit()</script>  // cookie auto-attached

// ✅ DEFENSE 1 — SameSite cookie (server sets this header):
//   Set-Cookie: session=abc; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax
//   → browser refuses to send `session` on the cross-site POST

// ✅ DEFENSE 2 — CSRF token the cross-site attacker cannot read:
const token = document.querySelector('meta[name="csrf"]').content;
fetch('/transfer', {
  method: 'POST',
  headers: { 'X-CSRF-Token': token },     // evil.com can't read this value
  body: JSON.stringify({ to, amount }),
});
// server rejects any request whose token doesn't match the session
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why does CSRF work, and how do you stop it?**
A: It works because browsers auto-attach cookies to requests by destination site, regardless of which page initiated them — so a malicious site can trigger authenticated actions on yours. Stop it with `SameSite=Lax`/`Strict` cookies (browser won't send the session cross-site) and/or CSRF tokens (a secret the attacker's origin can't read, thanks to the same-origin policy). The attacker can fire the request but never read the response.

---

### 🌐 CORS — The Rules For Talking To Another Origin

#### 🧩 What is it?
**CORS** (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) is a set of HTTP headers that let a server **opt in** to being called by JavaScript from a *different* origin (glossed: an origin is the scheme + host + port, e.g. `https://app.com:443`). By default, the **same-origin policy** blocks a page's script from *reading* responses from other origins.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The same-origin policy stops `evil.com`'s script from reading your `bank.com` data using your logged-in session. But legitimately, your frontend at `app.com` may *need* to call your API at `api.app.com`. CORS is the controlled way the API says "yes, this specific other origin is allowed to read my responses."

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
For "non-simple" requests (custom headers, methods like `PUT`/`DELETE`, certain content types), the browser first sends a **preflight** — an automatic `OPTIONS` request that asks permission *before* the real request:

```
  app.com JS → fetch PUT api.app.com  (non-simple)
        │ browser pauses, sends PREFLIGHT first
        ▼
  OPTIONS api.app.com
    Origin: https://app.com
    Access-Control-Request-Method: PUT
        ▼ server replies
  200 Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://app.com
      Access-Control-Allow-Methods: PUT
        ▼ allowed → browser now sends the REAL PUT
```

The crucial mental model: **CORS protects the user's browser, not the server.** The server still receives and can act on the request — CORS only controls whether the *browser* lets the calling page **read the response**. A non-browser client (curl, a backend, a script) ignores CORS entirely. So CORS is **not** an authentication or authorization mechanism; never rely on it to keep an endpoint "private."

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
The same-origin policy is a building rule: a visitor from Company A can't just walk into Company B's office and read its files. CORS is Company B posting a guest list at reception — "visitors from `app.com` may read these documents." The **preflight** is the receptionist phoning ahead — "someone from `app.com` wants to do a PUT, is that OK?" — before letting them in. And critically, the guard only controls *the visitor's reading*; mail (a server-to-server request) still gets delivered regardless of the guest list.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Browser side — a non-simple request triggers a preflight automatically
await fetch('https://api.app.com/items/42', {
  method: 'PUT',                                  // non-simple → preflight
  headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
  credentials: 'include',                         // send cookies cross-origin
  body: JSON.stringify({ done: true }),
});

// Server side (Express) — what the API must reply for the browser to allow it
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  res.set('Access-Control-Allow-Origin', 'https://app.com'); // not "*" w/ creds
  res.set('Access-Control-Allow-Methods', 'GET,PUT,POST');
  res.set('Access-Control-Allow-Headers', 'Content-Type');
  res.set('Access-Control-Allow-Credentials', 'true');
  if (req.method === 'OPTIONS') return res.sendStatus(204); // answer preflight
  next();
});
// ⚠️ This only governs what BROWSERS may read. curl/api clients are unaffected.
```

> **Note (simplification):** "simple" requests (e.g. a plain `GET`, or a `POST` with a form-style content type and no custom headers) skip the preflight — the browser sends them directly and only checks `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` on the *response* before letting the script read it. Preflight is specifically for requests that could have side effects the spec wants to confirm first.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Explain CORS and what a preflight request is.**
A: CORS is how a server opts in to cross-origin reads via `Access-Control-*` headers, relaxing the browser's same-origin policy. For non-simple requests the browser first sends a preflight `OPTIONS` request asking which origin/method/headers are allowed; only if the server approves does it send the real request. Key point: CORS protects the **browser/user** — it controls whether the calling page may read the response — not the server, which still processes the request. So CORS is not access control.

---

### 🛡️ CSP, Supply-Chain & The Dangers of `eval`

#### 🧩 What is it?
This topic bundles three defense-in-depth layers: **CSP** (Content Security Policy — a header telling the browser which scripts are allowed to run), **supply-chain hygiene** (trusting the third-party packages you install), and avoiding **`eval`/`Function`** (which turn strings into running code).

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
